# Preface

This document is a rewrite of the "System V Application Binary Interface,
AMD64 Architecture Processor Supplement (With LP64 and ILP32 Programming
Models), Version 1.0". The rewrite uses simple English (CEFR level B1). It
is made for readers whose first language is not English.

The rewrite changes the form of the text, not the technical content. Three
things are different from the original:

- The language is simpler. Sentences are short. Hard terms get a short
  explanation the first time they appear.
- All assembly examples use Intel syntax. The original document uses AT&T
  syntax. In Intel syntax, the destination operand comes first, and
  registers have no percent sign: `mov rax, rbx` copies `rbx` into `rax`.
- Extra examples were added. Each added example starts with the bold word
  "Example".

This rewrite is NOT the official specification. It has no normative power.
If you need the exact, official rules, read the official psABI document,
"System V Application Binary Interface, AMD64 Architecture Processor
Supplement".

The original document was edited by:

- H.J. Lu
- Michael Matz
- Milind Girkar
- Jan Hubicka
- Andreas Jaeger
- Mark Mitchell

> **Note:** Changes made to the original document by Intel Corporation are
> copyright Intel Corporation. Intel licensed these changes under the
> Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 license (CC BY 4.0). You can read this
> license at https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/legalcode.

# 1. Introduction

## What Is an ABI?

ABI means "Application Binary Interface". An ABI is a set of rules for
compiled programs. It says how functions get their arguments, how values
come back, how data is placed in memory, and what an object file looks
like. An API describes an interface at the source code level. An ABI
describes the same kind of interface, but at the machine code level.

If two tools follow the same ABI, their outputs can work together. A
program compiled by one compiler can call a library compiled by another
compiler. It can also run on any operating system that follows the same
ABI.

**Example:** Imagine two files. The file `main.c` is compiled with GCC.
The file `mathlib.c` is compiled with Clang. The function `main` calls the
function `add(int a, int b)` from `mathlib.c`. GCC must know where to put
the arguments `a` and `b`. Clang must look for them in the same place.
The ABI gives both compilers the same answer: the first integer argument
goes in the register `edi`, the second in `esi`, and the result comes back
in `eax`. Because both compilers follow this rule, the linker can join the
two object files into one working program. Without a shared ABI, the call
would read the wrong registers and the program would fail.

**Example:** The same idea applies to system libraries. Your program calls
`printf` from the C library. The C library on your system was built years
ago, by a different compiler version. The call still works, because both
sides follow the same ABI.

## The AMD64 Architecture

The AMD64 architecture is an extension of the x86 architecture. It adds
64-bit registers and a 64-bit address space to the older 32-bit design.

> **Note:** AMD64 was called x86-64 before. The old name x86-64 still
> appears in many places for historical reasons.

> **Note:** The architecture specification is available on the web at
> http://www.x86-64.org/documentation.

Every processor that implements the AMD64 architecture also provides
compatibility modes. In these modes, the processor can run programs made
for older descendants of the Intel 8086 architecture. This includes 32-bit
processors such as the Intel 386, the Intel Pentium, and the AMD K6-2.
Operating systems that conform to the AMD64 ABI may support programs that
run in these compatibility modes. But the AMD64 ABI does not apply to such
programs. This document applies only to programs that run in the "long"
mode of the AMD64 architecture. Long mode is the native 64-bit mode of the
processor.

## Two Programming Models: LP64 and ILP32

Binaries that use the AMD64 instruction set can follow one of two data
models:

- **ILP32**, a 32-bit model. Here the C types `int` and `long` and all
  pointer types are 32-bit objects.
- **LP64**, a 64-bit model. Here the C type `int` is 32 bits, but the C
  type `long` and all pointer types are 64-bit objects.

This specification covers both the LP64 and the ILP32 programming models.

**Example:** The table below shows the size of some C types in both
models. The names help you remember the rules: ILP32 means "Int, Long,
and Pointer are 32 bits". LP64 means "Long and Pointer are 64 bits".

**Table: Type sizes in the LP64 and ILP32 models**

| C type | Size in LP64 | Size in ILP32 |
|---|---|---|
| `int` | 32 bits | 32 bits |
| `long` | 64 bits | 32 bits |
| any pointer | 64 bits | 32 bits |

In both models, the program uses the full AMD64 instruction set and runs
in long mode. Only the sizes of `long` and of pointers differ.

## Relation to the Intel386 ABI and the System V ABI

The System V ABI has two parts. The first part is a generic document. It
holds the rules that are the same on every processor. The second part is a
processor supplement. It holds the rules for one processor family. This
document is the processor supplement for the AMD64 architecture.

Except where this document says something different, the AMD64 ABI follows
the conventions of the Intel386 ABI. The Intel386 ABI is the processor
supplement for the 32-bit Intel 386 architecture. The AMD64 ABI does not
repeat the full contents of the Intel386 ABI. It only describes the places
where it changes the Intel386 ABI. So, to get the complete picture, you
read the generic System V ABI, then the Intel386 ABI, and then this
document on top.

**Example:** The rules for the layout of the ELF file header come mostly
from the generic System V ABI. The AMD64 supplement only adds the specific
values for this architecture, for example the machine code `EM_X86_64` in
the `e_machine` field. You will not find a full ELF tutorial here; you
will find the AMD64-specific values.

## The Role of the C Language

This specification mostly uses the terms and concepts of the C programming
language. So, in a strict sense, it defines only an ABI for C. But many
other programming languages want to link with code written in C. For this
reason, the ABI rules in this document apply to those languages too.

> **Note:** See the section "C++" for details about the C++ ABI, and the
> section "Fortran" for details about the Fortran ABI.

**Example:** A Rust or Fortran program can call a C function directly.
When it does, the Rust or Fortran compiler generates the call using the
rules from this document. It puts the first integer argument in `rdi` and
expects the result in `rax`, exactly like a C compiler would.

## How This Document Is Structured

The document has these main chapters:

- **Introduction** (this chapter). It explains the scope of the ABI.
- **Software Installation**. It explains what the ABI says, and does not
  say, about installing software.
- **Low Level System Information**. It defines the machine interface: data
  types, registers, the stack frame, calling conventions, and the process
  start-up state.
- **Object Files**. It defines the ELF object file format details for
  AMD64: header values, special sections, and relocations.
- **Program Loading and Dynamic Linking**. It explains how programs are
  loaded into memory and how the dynamic linker resolves symbols.
- **Libraries**. It describes the C library interface and the unwind
  library interface.
- **Development Environment**. It lists compilation tool conventions.
- **Execution Environment**. It covers the runtime environment.
- **Conventions**. It documents features that are special to the AMD64
  ABI, such as C++, Fortran, the ILP32 programming model, alternate code
  sequences for security, and the Intel CET extension.
- Appendices about **Linux Conventions** and **Linker Optimization**.

# 2. Software Installation

This document does not specify how software must be installed on a machine
with the AMD64 architecture. The ABI defines how programs behave after
they are installed. It does not define the installation process itself.
Each operating system and each distribution can choose its own tools and
its own rules for installation.

**Example:** One Linux distribution installs software with `rpm` packages.
Another one uses `deb` packages. A third system may use a simple `tar`
archive that the user unpacks by hand. All three methods are fine. The ABI
does not prefer any of them. What the ABI does guarantee is this: after
installation, a conforming AMD64 binary can run on any conforming AMD64
system, because the binary format, the dynamic linker interface, and the
calling conventions are the same everywhere.

# 3. Low Level System Information

## Machine Interface

### Processor Architecture

Every program can expect that an AMD64 processor supports the baseline
features in the table "Micro-Architecture Levels" below. Most feature
names match CPUID bits. CPUID is a processor instruction that reports
which features a CPU has. The processor manual describes these bits.
There are two exceptions: OSFXSR and SCE. Bits in the `cr4` register
and in the `IA32_EFER` MSR control these two features. An MSR is a
"model-specific register", a special control register of the CPU.

**Table: Micro-Architecture Levels**

| Level Name | CPU Feature | Example instruction |
|---|---|---|
| (baseline) | CMOV | `cmov` |
| | CX8 | `cmpxchg8b` |
| | FPU | `fld` |
| | FXSR | `fxsave` |
| | MMX | `emms` |
| | OSFXSR | `fxsave` |
| | SCE | `syscall` |
| | SSE | `cvtss2si` |
| | SSE2 | `cvtpi2pd` |
| x86-64-v2 | CMPXCHG16B | `cmpxchg16b` |
| | LAHF-SAHF | `lahf` |
| | POPCNT | `popcnt` |
| | SSE3 | `addsubpd` |
| | SSE4_1 | `blendpd` |
| | SSE4_2 | `pcmpestri` |
| | SSSE3 | `phaddd` |
| x86-64-v3 | AVX | `vzeroall` |
| | AVX2 | `vpermd` |
| | BMI1 | `andn` |
| | BMI2 | `bzhi` |
| | F16C | `vcvtph2ps` |
| | FMA | `vfmadd132pd` |
| | LZCNT | `lzcnt` |
| | MOVBE | `movbe` |
| | OSXSAVE | `xgetbv` |
| x86-64-v4 | AVX512F | `kmovw` |
| | AVX512BW | `vdbpsadbw` |
| | AVX512CD | `vplzcntd` |
| | AVX512DQ | `vpmullq` |
| | AVX512VL | n/a |

The AMD64 baseline architecture is only the start. Later CPU models
added more features. So this ABI defines several *micro-architecture
levels*, starting at level `x86-64-v2`. A micro-architecture level is
a named group of CPU features. The levels exist so that a system can
load an optimized version of a library when the CPU supports it (see
below). The levels are cumulative. This means: each level also
includes all features from the levels before it.

Levels `x86-64-v3` and `x86-64-v4` are only available if the system
has fully enabled the matching features. The system must pass the
complete check sequence from the processor manual. This includes a
check of the XCR0 feature flags. The program reads these flags with
the `xgetbv` instruction.

#### Recommended Uses of Micro-Architecture Levels

The level names from the table "Micro-Architecture Levels" have two
main uses. First, they are used as directory names. The dynamic linker
searches these directories, based on the levels that the current CPU
supports. The *dynamic linker* is the program that loads shared
libraries when a program starts. Second, compilers use the names to
select groups of CPU features. A distribution may also state that it
requires CPU support for a certain level.

For example, take the level `x86-64-v3` (the second level after the
baseline). To build a shared object for it, a programmer uses the GCC
flag `-march=x86-64-v3`. A *shared object* is a library file that
programs load at run time. The result must go into the directory
`/usr/lib64/glibc-hwcaps/x86-64-v3`. On distributions with a
multi-arch file system layout, it goes into
`/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/glibc-hwcaps/x86-64-v3` instead. Some
systems only support the K8 baseline. To support them, you must also
install a fallback version in the default location: `/usr/lib64` or
`/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu`. You must build this fallback with
`-march=x86-64` (the upstream GCC default). If you do not follow this
rule, the library will fail to load on systems that do not support the
level of the optimized shared object.

A shared object installed under the matching `glibc-hwcaps`
subdirectory can freely use all CPU features of its level and of
earlier levels. It does not need extra detection logic for them. But
run-time detection is still required for other CPU features. This
covers features not listed in this section, and features that only
appear in later levels. This rule holds even if all current CPUs
happen to implement some features together.

A distribution may require support for a certain level. In that case,
the distribution builds everything with the matching `-march=` option.
It installs the built binaries in the default file system locations.
Programmers who target such a distribution can do the same: build with
the same `-march=` option and install into the default locations.
Optimized shared objects for later levels can still go into the
subdirectories with the matching names.

**Example:** Imagine a program that ships a math library in two
versions. The packager builds `libfastmath.so` twice. The first build
uses `-march=x86-64` and goes to `/usr/lib64/libfastmath.so`. The
second build uses `-march=x86-64-v3` and goes to
`/usr/lib64/glibc-hwcaps/x86-64-v3/libfastmath.so`. On an old CPU, the
dynamic linker loads the first file. On a CPU with AVX2, it loads the
second file. The program itself does not change at all.

### Data Representation

This specification uses fixed terms for object sizes. A *byte* is an
8-bit object. A *twobyte* is a 16-bit object. A *fourbyte* is a 32-bit
object. An *eightbyte* is a 64-bit object. A *sixteenbyte* is a
128-bit object.

> **Note:** The Intel386 ABI uses different terms. There, a *halfword*
> is a 16-bit object, a *word* is a 32-bit object, and a *doubleword*
> is a 64-bit object. But most IA-32 processor documentation defines a
> *word* as a 16-bit object, a *doubleword* as a 32-bit object, a
> *quadword* as a 64-bit object, and a *double quadword* as a 128-bit
> object.

#### Fundamental Types

The table "Scalar Types" shows how the scalar types of ISO C map to
the types of the processor. A *scalar type* is a simple type that
holds one value, like an integer or a pointer. The types `__int128`,
`_Float16`, `__float80`, `__float128`, `__m64`, `__m128`, `__m256`
and `__m512` are optional.

The table uses two data models. *LP64* is the normal 64-bit model:
`long` and pointers are 64 bits wide. *ILP32* is the x32 model: `int`,
`long` and pointers are 32 bits wide, but the code still runs in
64-bit mode. *Alignment* means: the address of the object must be a
multiple of this number.

**Table: Scalar Types**

| Type | C | sizeof | Alignment (bytes) | AMD64 Architecture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Integral | `_Bool` (1) | 1 | 1 | boolean |
| | `char`, `signed char` | 1 | 1 | signed byte |
| | `unsigned char` | 1 | 1 | unsigned byte |
| | `signed short` | 2 | 2 | signed twobyte |
| | `unsigned short` | 2 | 2 | unsigned twobyte |
| | `signed int`, `enum` (3) | 4 | 4 | signed fourbyte |
| | `unsigned int` | 4 | 4 | unsigned fourbyte |
| | `signed long` (LP64) | 8 | 8 | signed eightbyte |
| | `unsigned long` (LP64) | 8 | 8 | unsigned eightbyte |
| | `signed long` (ILP32) | 4 | 4 | signed fourbyte |
| | `unsigned long` (ILP32) | 4 | 4 | unsigned fourbyte |
| | `signed long long` | 8 | 8 (4) | signed eightbyte |
| | `unsigned long long` | 8 | 8 (4) | unsigned eightbyte |
| | `__int128` (2), `signed __int128` (2) | 16 | 16 | signed sixteenbyte |
| | `unsigned __int128` (2) | 16 | 16 | unsigned sixteenbyte |
| Pointer | `any-type *` (LP64), `any-type (*)()` (LP64) | 8 | 8 | unsigned eightbyte |
| | `any-type *` (ILP32), `any-type (*)()` (ILP32) | 4 | 4 | unsigned fourbyte |
| Floating-point | `_Float16` (6) | 2 | 2 | 16-bit (IEEE-754) |
| | `float` | 4 | 4 | single (IEEE-754) |
| | `double` | 8 | 8 (4) | double (IEEE-754) |
| | `__float80` (2) | 16 | 16 | 80-bit extended (IEEE-754) |
| | `long double` (5) | 16 | 16 | 80-bit extended (IEEE-754) |
| | `__float128` (2) | 16 | 16 | 128-bit extended (IEEE-754) |
| | `long double` (5) | 16 | 16 | 128-bit extended (IEEE-754) |
| Decimal-floating-point | `_Decimal32` | 4 | 4 | 32bit BID (IEEE-754R) |
| | `_Decimal64` | 8 | 8 | 64bit BID (IEEE-754R) |
| | `_Decimal128` | 16 | 16 | 128bit BID (IEEE-754R) |
| Packed | `__m64` (2) | 8 | 8 | MMX and 3DNow! |
| | `__m128` (2) | 16 | 16 | SSE and SSE-2 |
| | `__m256` (2) | 32 | 32 | AVX |
| | `__m512` (2) | 64 | 64 | AVX-512 |

> **Note (1):** This type is called `bool` in C++.

> **Note (2):** These types are optional.

> **Note (3):** C++ and some implementations of C permit enums larger
> than an `int`. In that case, the underlying type is bumped to
> `unsigned int`, `long int` or `unsigned long int`, in that order.

> **Note (4):** The `long long`, `signed long long`,
> `unsigned long long` and `double` types have 4-byte alignment in the
> Intel386 ABI.

> **Note (5):** On the Android(TM) platform, the `long double` type is
> 128-bit. There it is the same as the `__float128` type. More
> information on the Android(TM) platform is available from
> http://www.android.com/.

> **Note (6):** The `_Float16` type, from ISO/IEC TS 18661-3:2015, is
> optional.

In the table, IEEE-754 is the standard format for binary
floating-point numbers. BID means "Binary Integer Decimal". It is the
encoding used for decimal floating-point numbers (IEEE-754R).

The `__float128` type uses a 15-bit exponent and a 113-bit mantissa.
The highest significant bit of the mantissa is implicit, so it is not
stored. The exponent bias is 16383.

> **Note:** Early implementations of the AMD64 architecture are
> expected to support operations on the `__float128` type only through
> software emulation.

The `long double` type uses a 15-bit exponent and a 64-bit mantissa.
Here the highest significant bit is explicit, so it is stored. The
exponent bias is 16383.

> **Note:** This type is the x87 double extended precision data type.

A `long double` takes 16 bytes of storage, but only the first 10 bytes
have meaning. The other six bytes are tail padding. The content of
these padding bytes is undefined.

The `__int128` type is stored in little-endian order in memory. In
simple words: the 64 low-order bits sit at a lower address than the 64
high-order bits.

The value of `_Alignof(max_align_t)` is 16.

A null pointer (for all pointer types) has the value zero.

The type `size_t` is defined as `unsigned long` for LP64 and as
`unsigned int` for ILP32.

Booleans are stored in memory as single byte objects. In memory, the
value is always 0 (`false`) or 1 (`true`). The rule is different for
integer registers. When a boolean sits in an integer register, all 8
bytes of the register matter. Any nonzero value counts as `true`. One
exception: this register rule does not apply when the boolean is
passed as an argument.

Like the Intel386 architecture, the AMD64 architecture in general does
not require aligned data accesses. A misaligned access is slower than
an aligned access, but it behaves the same way. There are exceptions:
`__m128`, `__m256` and `__m512` must always be aligned properly.

**Example:** This short C program shows why alignment still matters.
The type `__m128` needs 16-byte alignment. If you break this rule, the
program can crash.

```c
#include <xmmintrin.h>

char buf[32];

int main(void)
{
    /* buf + 1 is almost never 16-byte aligned. */
    __m128 *p = (__m128 *)(buf + 1);
    __m128 v = *p;   /* aligned load: this can crash */
    (void)v;
    return 0;
}
```

For the load `*p`, the compiler uses an aligned SSE instruction:

```asm
movaps xmm0, [rax]   ; rax must be 16-byte aligned, or the CPU faults
```

For data at an unknown address, the compiler must use the unaligned
form instead:

```asm
movups xmm0, [rax]   ; works for any address, may be slower
```

**Example:** You can print sizes and alignments yourself. This program
uses `sizeof` and `_Alignof`:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    printf("int:    size %zu align %zu\n",
           sizeof(int), _Alignof(int));
    printf("long:   size %zu align %zu\n",
           sizeof(long), _Alignof(long));
    printf("double: size %zu align %zu\n",
           sizeof(double), _Alignof(double));
    printf("void *: size %zu align %zu\n",
           sizeof(void *), _Alignof(void *));
    return 0;
}
```

On an LP64 system, it prints size 4 and alignment 4 for `int`, and
size 8 and alignment 8 for `long`, `double` and `void *`. This matches
the table "Scalar Types".

#### Fundamental Types of N-bit Integers

`_BitInt(N)` comes from ISO/IEC WG14 N2763. Here `N` is an integral
constant expression. It gives the exact number of bits used to
represent the type. `_BitInt(N)` is a family of integer types. The
rules are:

- `_BitInt(N)` types are signed by default. `unsigned _BitInt(N)`
  types are unsigned.
- `_BitInt(N)` types are stored in little-endian order in memory. Bits
  in each byte are allocated from right to left.
- For N <= 64, a `_BitInt(N)` has the same size and alignment as the
  smallest type that can contain it. The candidates are the signed and
  unsigned forms of `char`, `short`, `int`, `long` and `long long`.
- For N > 64, the type is treated as a struct of 64-bit integer
  chunks. The number of chunks is the smallest number that can contain
  the type. These `_BitInt(N)` types are byte-aligned to 64 bits. The
  size is the smallest multiple of the 64-bit chunks that is greater
  than or equal to N.
- Some bits may be unused: bits beyond the width N but inside the size
  of the type. The value of these unused bits is unspecified, in
  memory and in registers.

These size rules allow arrays of these types. The common pattern
`sizeof(Array) / sizeof(ElementType)` works and gives the number of
elements.

**Example:** Here are some concrete `_BitInt` sizes on AMD64:

| Type | Fits in | sizeof | Alignment |
|---|---|---|---|
| `_BitInt(7)` | `char` | 1 | 1 |
| `_BitInt(21)` | `int` | 4 | 4 |
| `_BitInt(64)` | `long` | 8 | 8 |
| `_BitInt(100)` | two 64-bit chunks | 16 | 8 |
| `_BitInt(200)` | four 64-bit chunks | 32 | 8 |

For `_BitInt(100)`, bits 0 to 99 hold the value. Bits 100 to 127 are
unused, and their value is unspecified.

#### Special Types

The `__bf16` type is an alternate encoding format for 16-bit floats.
It has an 8-bit exponent and a 7-bit mantissa. `__bf16` represents the
Brain Floating Point Format. This format is a truncated (16-bit)
version of the 32-bit IEEE 754 single-precision floating-point format.
In simple words: it is the top half of a `float`. It has the same
size, alignment, parameter passing rules and return rules as
`_Float16`.

> **Note:** This type is used in `BF16` intrinsics.

> **Note:** This type is used to speed up machine learning algorithms,
> in particular deep learning training.

#### Aggregates and Unions

An *aggregate* is a structure or an array. A *union* is a type where
all members share the same memory. Structures and unions take the
alignment of their most strictly aligned member. Each member goes to
the lowest available offset that fits its alignment. The size of any
object is always a multiple of the alignment of the object.

An array uses the same alignment as its elements. There is one
exception. A local or global array variable of at least 16 bytes, or a
C99 variable-length array variable, always has an alignment of at
least 16 bytes.

> **Note:** This alignment requirement allows the use of SSE
> instructions on the array. The compiler cannot in general calculate
> the size of a variable-length array (VLA). But most VLAs are
> expected to need at least 16 bytes. So it is logical to require that
> VLAs have at least 16-byte alignment.

Structure and union objects can need padding to meet the size and
alignment rules. *Padding* means unused filler bytes. The content of
any padding is undefined.

**Example:** Let us compute the layout of this struct step by step:

```c
struct example {
    char   c;   /* size 1, alignment 1 */
    int    i;   /* size 4, alignment 4 */
    short  h;   /* size 2, alignment 2 */
    double d;   /* size 8, alignment 8 */
};
```

Each member gets the lowest offset that fits its alignment:

- `c` goes to offset 0. The next free offset is 1.
- `i` needs 4-byte alignment. Offset 1 does not fit. So the compiler
  adds 3 padding bytes. `i` goes to offset 4. The next free offset
  is 8.
- `h` needs 2-byte alignment. Offset 8 fits. `h` goes to offset 8. The
  next free offset is 10.
- `d` needs 8-byte alignment. Offset 10 does not fit. The compiler
  adds 6 padding bytes. `d` goes to offset 16. The next free offset
  is 24.

The strictest member alignment is 8 (from `d`). So the struct has
alignment 8. The size 24 is already a multiple of 8. So no tail
padding is needed. Result: `sizeof(struct example)` is 24, and
`_Alignof(struct example)` is 8.

```
offset  0        : c        (1 byte)
offset  1 ..  3  : padding  (3 bytes, undefined content)
offset  4 ..  7  : i        (4 bytes)
offset  8 ..  9  : h        (2 bytes)
offset 10 .. 15  : padding  (6 bytes, undefined content)
offset 16 .. 23  : d        (8 bytes)
total: 24 bytes, alignment: 8 bytes
```

If you sort the members from big to small (`d`, `i`, `h`, `c`), the
struct needs only 16 bytes. The ABI does not reorder members for you.
The order in the source code decides the layout.

#### Bit-Fields

C struct and union definitions may include *bit-fields*. A bit-field
is a member that holds an integral value of a chosen size in bits.

The table "Bit-Field Ranges" lists the allowed widths and value ranges
for each bit-field type. Here `w` is the width in bits. The notation
`2^w` means 2 to the power of `w`.

**Table: Bit-Field Ranges**

| Bit-field Type | Width w | Range |
|---|---|---|
| `signed char` | 1 to 8 | -2^(w-1) to 2^(w-1) - 1 |
| `char` | 1 to 8 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `unsigned char` | 1 to 8 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `signed short` | 1 to 16 | -2^(w-1) to 2^(w-1) - 1 |
| `short` | 1 to 16 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `unsigned short` | 1 to 16 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `signed int` | 1 to 32 | -2^(w-1) to 2^(w-1) - 1 |
| `int` | 1 to 32 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `unsigned int` | 1 to 32 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `signed long` (LP64) | 1 to 64 | -2^(w-1) to 2^(w-1) - 1 |
| `long` (LP64) | 1 to 64 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `unsigned long` (LP64) | 1 to 64 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `long` (ILP32) | 1 to 32 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `unsigned long` (ILP32) | 1 to 32 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `signed long long` | 1 to 64 | -2^(w-1) to 2^(w-1) - 1 |
| `long long` | 1 to 64 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |
| `unsigned long long` | 1 to 64 | 0 to 2^w - 1 |

The ABI does not permit bit-fields of type `__m64`, `__m128`, `__m256`
or `__m512`. Programs that use bit-fields of these types are not
portable.

Some bit-fields are declared without `signed` or `unsigned`, for
example plain `int b : 3;`. Such bit-fields always have non-negative
values. They may have the type `char`, `short`, `int` or `long`, and
these types can normally hold negative values. Still, these bit-fields
have the same range as a bit-field of the same size with the matching
unsigned type. Bit-fields follow the same size and alignment rules as
other structure and union members.

Also:

- Bit-fields are allocated from right to left. In simple words: the
  first bit-field gets the lowest bits (the least significant bits).
- A bit-field must fit inside one storage unit of its declared type.
  A *storage unit* is an object of the declared type, for example 4
  bytes for `int`. A bit-field cannot cross the border of its storage
  unit.
- Bit-fields may share a storage unit with other struct or union
  members.

Unnamed bit-fields have no name and only reserve space, for example
`int : 4;`. The types of unnamed bit-fields do not affect the
alignment of a structure or union.

**Example:** Look at this bit-field struct and its exact bit layout:

```c
struct flags {
    unsigned int a : 3;   /* bits  0 ..  2 */
    unsigned int b : 5;   /* bits  3 ..  7 */
    unsigned int c : 10;  /* bits  8 .. 17 */
};
```

All three bit-fields have the type `unsigned int`. So the storage unit
is one fourbyte (32 bits). All three fields fit together in one unit:
3 + 5 + 10 = 18 bits. Allocation goes from right to left, so `a`
starts at bit 0:

```
bit 31 ......... 18 | 17 ........ 8 | 7 ..... 3 | 2 ... 0
  unused (14 bits)  |  c (10 bits)  | b (5 bits)| a (3)
```

The struct has size 4 and alignment 4. If you store `a = 5`, `b = 9`
and `c = 300`, the unit holds the value
`(300 << 8) | (9 << 3) | 5` = 0x12C4D. The whole struct in memory is
the little-endian fourbyte `4D 2C 01 00`.

**Example:** A bit-field cannot cross its storage unit border. Watch
what happens here:

```c
struct split {
    unsigned int x : 30;  /* bits 0 .. 29 of unit 0 */
    unsigned int y : 4;   /* does not fit in unit 0 */
};
```

Only 2 bits are free in the first fourbyte, but `y` needs 4 bits. So
`y` moves to the next storage unit. It gets bits 0 to 3 of the second
fourbyte. The struct has size 8 and alignment 4. Bits 30 and 31 of the
first unit are unused padding.

## Function Calling Sequence

This section describes the standard way to call a function. It covers
the layout of the stack frame, the use of registers, and the passing
of parameters.

First, two important words. The *caller* is the function that makes
the call. The *callee* is the function that gets called.

The rules of the standard calling sequence apply only to global
functions. A local function that no other compilation unit can reach
may use different rules. Still, we recommend that all functions use
the standard calling sequence when possible.

### Registers

The AMD64 architecture gives you 16 general purpose 64-bit registers.
It also gives you 16 SSE registers, and each one is 128 bits wide. In
addition, there are 8 x87 floating point registers, and each one is 80
bits wide. In MMX/3DNow! mode, you can use each x87 floating point
register as a 64-bit register. All of these registers are global.
This means all active procedures of a thread share the same registers.

Intel AVX (Advanced Vector Extensions) adds 16 AVX registers that are
256 bits wide (`ymm0` - `ymm15`). The lower 128 bits of `ymm0` -
`ymm15` are the same as the 128-bit SSE registers (`xmm0` - `xmm15`).
Intel AVX-512 adds 32 SIMD registers that are 512 bits wide (`zmm0` -
`zmm31`). The lower 128 bits of `zmm0` - `zmm31` are the same as the
128-bit SSE registers (`xmm0` - `xmm31`). The lower 256 bits of
`zmm0` - `zmm31` are the same as the 256-bit AVX registers (`ymm0` -
`ymm31`).

> **Note:** The registers `xmm16` - `xmm31` and `ymm16` - `ymm31` only
> exist on processors with Intel AVX-512.

For parameter passing and function return, `xmmN`, `ymmN` and `zmmN`
name the same register. Only one of the three names can be in use at
the same time. In this document, the term *vector register* means an
SSE, AVX or AVX-512 register. Intel AVX-512 also adds 8 vector mask
registers (`k0` - `k7`). Each mask register is 64 bits wide.

Intel AMX (Advanced Matrix Extensions) is a programming model with two
parts. The first part is a set of 2-dimensional registers, called
*tiles*. A tile holds a sub-array from a larger 2-dimensional image in
memory. The second part is a set of accelerators that work on tiles.
The abilities of an Intel AMX implementation are listed by *palettes*.
Two palettes exist. Palette 0 is the initialized state. Palette 1
has 8 tile registers (`tmm0` - `tmm7`). Each tile register can be up
to 1 KB in size. A tile control register controls the tiles.

Intel APX (Advanced Performance Extensions) adds 16 more general
purpose 64-bit registers (`r16` - `r31`).

Now we look at how each register is used. The registers `rbp`, `rbx`
and `r12` through `r15` "belong" to the caller. The callee must
preserve their values. In other words, if the callee changes one of
these registers, it must restore the old value before it returns. We
call these registers *callee-saved*. All other registers "belong" to
the callee. If the caller wants to keep such a register value across
a call, the caller must save the value in its own stack frame.

> **Note:** This is different from the Intel386 ABI. On AMD64, `rdi`
> and `rsi` belong to the called function, not to the caller.

**Example:** The function `g` below wants to keep the value of `x`
across a call to `f`. It stores `x` in `rbx`, because `rbx` is
callee-saved. The call to `f` cannot destroy it. But `g` changes
`rbx`, so `g` must save and restore `rbx` for its own caller.

```c
long f(long x);

long g(long x)
{
    return f(x) + x;
}
```

```asm
g:
    push rbx          ; save rbx for the caller of g
    mov rbx, rdi      ; keep x in a callee-saved register
    call f            ; rax = f(x); rbx survives this call
    add rax, rbx      ; rax = f(x) + x
    pop rbx           ; restore the old rbx
    ret
```

The CPU must be in x87 mode when a function starts. So every function
that uses the MMX registers must run an `emms` or `femms` instruction
after it uses them. It must do this before it returns or calls
another function.

> **Note:** All x87 registers are caller-saved. So a callee that uses
> the MMX registers may use the faster `femms` instruction.

The direction flag `DF` in the `rFLAGS` register must be clear on
function entry and on function return. Clear means it is set to the
"forward" direction. The other user flags have no fixed role in the
standard calling sequence. They are *not* preserved across calls.

The `MXCSR` register has control bits and status bits. The control
bits are callee-saved, so they keep their values across calls. The
status bits are caller-saved, so they are not preserved. For the x87
unit, the rule is similar. The x87 status word is caller-saved. The
x87 control word is callee-saved.

The next table shows the use of every register. The last column tells
you if the callee must preserve the register.

**Table: Register usage**

| Register | Usage | Callee saved |
|---|---|---|
| `rax` | temporary register; with variable arguments it passes information about the number of vector registers used; 1st return register | No |
| `rbx` | callee-saved register | Yes |
| `rcx` | used to pass the 4th integer argument to functions | No |
| `rdx` | used to pass the 3rd argument to functions; 2nd return register | No |
| `rsp` | stack pointer | Yes |
| `rbp` | callee-saved register; optionally used as frame pointer | Yes |
| `rsi` | used to pass the 2nd argument to functions | No |
| `rdi` | used to pass the 1st argument to functions | No |
| `r8` | used to pass the 5th argument to functions | No |
| `r9` | used to pass the 6th argument to functions | No |
| `r10` | temporary register; used for passing a function's static chain pointer | No |
| `r11` | temporary register | No |
| `r12` - `r14` | callee-saved registers | Yes |
| `r15` | callee-saved register; optionally used as GOT base pointer | Yes |
| `r16` - `r31` | temporary registers | No |
| `xmm0` - `xmm1` | used to pass and return floating point arguments | No |
| `xmm2` - `xmm7` | used to pass floating point arguments | No |
| `xmm8` - `xmm15` | temporary registers | No |
| `xmm16` - `xmm31` | temporary registers | No |
| `tmm0` - `tmm7` | temporary registers | No |
| `mm0` - `mm7` | temporary registers | No |
| `k0` - `k7` | temporary registers | No |
| `st0`, `st1` | temporary registers; used to return `long double` arguments | No |
| `st2` - `st7` | temporary registers | No |
| `fs` | thread pointer | Yes |
| `mxcsr` | SSE2 control and status word | partial |
| x87 SW | x87 status word | No |
| x87 CW | x87 control word | Yes |
| tilecfg | tile control register | No |

### The Stack Frame

Besides registers, each function has a *frame* on the run-time stack.
The stack grows downwards, from high addresses to low addresses. The
next figure shows how the stack is organized. In the figure, `[rbp +
16]` means the memory at address `rbp` plus 16. An *eightbyte* is a
block of 8 bytes.

**Figure: Stack frame with base pointer**

| Position | Contents | Frame |
|---|---|---|
| `[rbp + 8n + 16]` | memory argument eightbyte n | previous frame |
| ... | ... | previous frame |
| `[rbp + 16]` | memory argument eightbyte 0 | previous frame |
| `[rbp + 8]` | return address | |
| `[rbp + 0]` | previous `rbp` value | |
| `[rbp - 8]` | unspecified | current frame |
| ... | ... | current frame |
| `[rsp + 0]` | variable size | current frame |
| `[rsp - 128]` | red zone | current frame |

The end of the input argument area must be aligned on a 16-byte
boundary. The boundary grows to 32 or 64 bytes if a `__m256` or
`__m512` value is passed on the stack. In simple words: the stack
must be 16 (32 or 64) byte aligned right before the `call` instruction
runs.

> **Note:** The maximum aligned boundary is the maximum alignment of
> all variables passed on the stack. In C11, a variable of the type
> `typedef struct { _Alignas (512) int i; } var_t;` is aligned to 512
> bytes.

The `call` instruction pushes the return address. So, right after
control reaches the function entry point, `rsp` points to the return
address. At that moment, the value of `rsp + 8` is a multiple of 16
(32 or 64).

> **Note:** You can avoid the common use of `rbp` as a frame pointer.
> Instead, you can use `rsp` (the stack pointer) to index into the
> stack frame. This trick saves two instructions in the prologue and
> epilogue. It also frees one more general purpose register (`rbp`).

The 128-byte area below the location that `rsp` points to is reserved.
Signal handlers and interrupt handlers must not change it. So a
function may use this area for temporary data that it does not need
across function calls. A *leaf function* is a function that calls no
other function. A leaf function may place its whole stack frame in
this area. Then it does not need to move the stack pointer in the
prologue and epilogue. This area is called the *red zone*.

> **Note:** Locations within 128 bytes of `rsp` can be addressed with
> one-byte displacements. This makes the code smaller.

**Example:** The leaf function below needs one temporary variable. It
stores the variable in the red zone, below `rsp`. It never changes
`rsp`. This is safe because the function calls no other function.

```c
int leaf(int x)
{
    int tmp = x * x;
    return tmp + x;
}
```

```asm
leaf:
    mov eax, edi
    imul eax, edi                  ; eax = x * x
    mov DWORD PTR [rsp - 4], eax   ; store tmp in the red zone
    mov eax, DWORD PTR [rsp - 4]   ; load tmp again
    add eax, edi                   ; eax = tmp + x
    ret
```

### Parameter Passing

First, the caller computes the argument values. Then it places them
in registers or pushes them on the stack. The next sections describe
how this works.

#### Definitions

We first define a number of *classes*. We use them to classify
arguments. The classes match the AMD64 register classes. They are:

- **INTEGER**: This class holds integral types that fit into one of
  the general purpose registers.
- **SSE**: This class holds types that fit into a vector register.
- **SSEUP**: This class holds types that fit into a vector register
  and can be passed and returned in the upper bytes of it.
- **X87, X87UP**: These classes hold types that are returned through
  the x87 FPU.
- **COMPLEX_X87**: This class holds types that are returned through
  the x87 FPU.
- **NO_CLASS**: This class is a start value in the algorithms. It is
  used for padding and for empty structures and unions.
- **MEMORY**: This class holds types that are passed and returned in
  memory, through the stack.

#### Classification

The size of each argument gets rounded up to whole eightbytes.

> **Note:** Because of this, the stack is always eightbyte aligned.

The basic types get their natural classes:

- Arguments of the types (signed and unsigned) `_Bool`, `char`,
  `short`, `int`, `long`, `long long`, and pointers are in the
  INTEGER class.
- Arguments of the types `_Float16`, `float`, `double`, `_Decimal32`,
  `_Decimal64` and `__m64` are in the SSE class.
- Arguments of the types `__float128`, `_Decimal128` and `__m128` are
  split into two halves. The least significant half is in the SSE
  class. The most significant half is in the SSEUP class.
- Arguments of the type `__m256` are split into four eightbyte
  chunks. The least significant chunk is in the SSE class. All the
  other chunks are in the SSEUP class.
- Arguments of the type `__m512` are split into eight eightbyte
  chunks. The least significant chunk is in the SSE class. All the
  other chunks are in the SSEUP class.
- For the type `long double`, the 64-bit mantissa is in the X87
  class. The 16-bit exponent plus 6 bytes of padding is in the X87UP
  class.
- Arguments of the type `__int128` offer the same operations as
  INTEGER types. But they do not fit into one general purpose
  register. They need two registers. For classification, `__int128`
  is treated as if it were implemented like this:

  ```c
  typedef struct {
    long low, high;
  } __int128;
  ```

  There is one exception: an `__int128` argument that is stored in
  memory must be aligned on a 16-byte boundary.
- Arguments of the type `_BitInt(N)` with N <= 64 are in the INTEGER
  class.
- Arguments of the type `_BitInt(N)` with N > 64 are classified as if
  they were a struct of 64-bit integer fields.
- Arguments of the type `complex T`, where `T` is one of `_Float16`,
  `float`, `double` or `__float128`, are treated as if they were
  implemented like this:

  ```c
  struct complexT {
    T real;
    T imag;
  };
  ```
- A variable of the type `complex long double` gets the class
  COMPLEX_X87.

An *aggregate* is a structure or an array. The classification of
aggregate and union types works like this:

1. If the size of an object is larger than eight eightbytes, or if it
   contains unaligned fields, it has the class MEMORY.

   > **Note:** The post merger cleanup below makes sure of the
   > following. For processors that do not support the `__m256` type:
   > if the size of an object is larger than two eightbytes, and the
   > first eightbyte is not SSE, or any other eightbyte is not SSEUP,
   > the object still has the class MEMORY. This in turn makes sure
   > of the next point. For processors that do support the `__m256`
   > type: if the size of an object is four eightbytes, the first
   > eightbyte is SSE, and all other eightbytes are SSEUP, the object
   > can be passed in a register. The same idea applies to the
   > `__m512` type. For processors that support the `__m512` type: if
   > the size of an object is eight eightbytes, the first eightbyte is
   > SSE, and all other eightbytes are SSEUP, the object can be passed
   > in a register. Otherwise it is passed in memory.

2. If a C++ object is non-trivial for the purpose of calls, as the
   C++ ABI defines, it is passed by invisible reference. This means
   the object is replaced in the parameter list by a pointer, and
   that pointer has the class INTEGER.

   > **Note:** See the section about the C++ ABI for details.

   > **Note:** An object whose type is non-trivial for the purpose of
   > calls cannot be passed by value. Such an object must have the
   > same address in the caller and in the callee. The same problem
   > appears when a function returns such an object. See C++17
   > [class.temporary] paragraph 3.

3. If the size of the aggregate is more than one eightbyte, each
   eightbyte is classified on its own. Each eightbyte starts with
   the class NO_CLASS.

4. Each field of an object is classified recursively. The algorithm
   always looks at two fields at a time. The resulting class of an
   eightbyte comes from the classes of the fields in it:

   > **Note:** Zero-width bit-fields have the class NO_CLASS.

   a. If both classes are equal, this is the resulting class.

   b. If one of the classes is NO_CLASS, the resulting class is the
      other class.

   c. If one of the classes is MEMORY, the result is the MEMORY
      class.

   d. If one of the classes is INTEGER, the result is INTEGER.

   e. If one of the classes is X87, X87UP or COMPLEX_X87, the result
      is the MEMORY class.

   f. Otherwise, the class SSE is used.

5. Then a *post merger cleanup* is done:

   a. If one of the classes is MEMORY, the whole argument is passed
      in memory.

   b. If X87UP is not preceded by X87, the whole argument is passed
      in memory.

   c. If the size of the aggregate is more than two eightbytes, and
      the first eightbyte is not SSE, or any other eightbyte is not
      SSEUP, the whole argument is passed in memory.

   d. If SSEUP is not preceded by SSE or SSEUP, it is converted to
      SSE.

**Example:** Let us classify this structure step by step:

```c
struct mix {
    int a;      /* 4 bytes, offset 0 */
    double b;   /* 8 bytes, offset 8 */
};
```

The structure is 16 bytes large, so it uses two eightbytes.

1. The size is not larger than eight eightbytes, and no field is
   unaligned. So the class is not MEMORY (rule 1 does not apply).
2. Both eightbytes start with the class NO_CLASS (rule 3).
3. First eightbyte: it holds the field `a` (class INTEGER) and 4
   bytes of padding (class NO_CLASS). We merge INTEGER with
   NO_CLASS. By rule 4b, the result is INTEGER.
4. Second eightbyte: it holds only the field `b` (class SSE). The
   result is SSE.
5. Post merger cleanup: no eightbyte is MEMORY, there is no X87UP,
   and the size is not more than two eightbytes. Nothing changes.

The final result: the first eightbyte is INTEGER, the second is SSE.
Now look at this call:

```c
void take(struct mix m);
take(s);
```

The INTEGER eightbyte (`s.a` plus padding) goes into the next free
integer register, which is `rdi`. The SSE eightbyte (`s.b`) goes into
the next free vector register, which is `xmm0`.

```asm
    mov rdi, QWORD PTR [rip + s]        ; s.a and padding
    movsd xmm0, QWORD PTR [rip + s+8]   ; s.b
    call take
```

#### Passing

After the arguments are classified, registers get assigned. This
happens in left-to-right order over the arguments. The rules are:

1. If the class is MEMORY, pass the argument on the stack. The
   address must respect the alignment of the argument. That
   alignment can be larger than the natural alignment of its type.

2. If the class is INTEGER, use the next free register from this
   sequence: `rdi`, `rsi`, `rdx`, `rcx`, `r8` and `r9`.

   > **Note:** The register `r11` does not need to be preserved, and
   > it is not used to pass arguments. Because `r11` is a free
   > scratch register, the code in the PLT can compute the jump
   > address without spilling any register. The register `al` tells a
   > function with a variable number of arguments how many vector
   > registers are used. The register `r10` passes a function's
   > static chain pointer.

3. If the class is SSE, use the next free vector register. The
   registers are taken in order, from `xmm0` to `xmm7`.

4. If the class is SSEUP, pass the eightbyte in the next free
   eightbyte chunk of the last used vector register.

5. If the class is X87, X87UP or COMPLEX_X87, pass the argument in
   memory.

Sometimes a value of a type with class INTEGER is smaller than a full
register. When such a value is passed or returned in a register or on
the stack, the extra bits are unspecified. The extra bits are the
bits that the memory representation of the type would not have (see
the figure about basic types in the section 'Fundamental Types').

> **Note:** This means the consumer of such a value must extend it, or
> must use short instruction forms. It is the same as in memory: for
> a value of the type `_Bool`, only the lowest 8 bits matter. Together
> they form the value 0 or 1.

Maybe no registers are left for some eightbyte of an argument. In
that case, the whole argument is passed on the stack. If some
eightbytes of that argument already got registers, those assignments
are taken back.

After the registers are assigned, the memory arguments are pushed on
the stack. They are pushed in reversed order, from right to left.

> **Note:** Right-to-left order on the stack makes functions with a
> variable number of arguments simpler. The location of the first
> argument can always be computed statically. Only the type of that
> argument is needed. With left-to-right order, this would be hard.

Some calls may reach functions that use varargs or stdarg. These are
prototype-less calls, or calls to functions with an ellipsis (`...`)
in the declaration. For such calls, `al` is a hidden extra argument.
It gives the number of vector registers used. The value in `al` does
not need to match the exact number. But it must be an upper bound on
the number of vector registers used. It must be in the range 0 to 8,
inclusive.

> **Note:** The rest of `rax` is undefined here. Only the contents of
> `al` are defined.

If you pass `__m256` or `__m512` arguments to functions that use
varargs or stdarg, you must provide function prototypes. Otherwise,
the run-time behavior is undefined.

**Example:** The function below takes 8 integer arguments. Only 6
integer registers exist for arguments. So the 7th and the 8th
argument go on the stack.

```c
long sum8(long a, long b, long c, long d,
          long e, long f, long g, long h);

long r = sum8(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8);
```

The register and stack assignment is: `rdi` = 1, `rsi` = 2, `rdx` = 3,
`rcx` = 4, `r8` = 5, `r9` = 6. The value 7 goes to `[rsp]`, and the
value 8 goes to `[rsp + 8]`. The code pushes them from right to left,
so the 7th argument ends at the lower address.

```asm
    sub rsp, 16                    ; space for 2 stack arguments,
                                   ; keeps the stack 16-byte aligned
    mov QWORD PTR [rsp + 8], 8     ; 8th argument h
    mov QWORD PTR [rsp], 7         ; 7th argument g
    mov r9d, 6                     ; 6th argument f
    mov r8d, 5                     ; 5th argument e
    mov ecx, 4                     ; 4th argument d
    mov edx, 3                     ; 3rd argument c
    mov esi, 2                     ; 2nd argument b
    mov edi, 1                     ; 1st argument a
    call sum8
    add rsp, 16                    ; remove the stack arguments
```

**Example:** `printf` is a variadic function. A *variadic function*
takes a variable number of arguments. So the caller must set `al` to
an upper bound on the number of vector registers used. Here one
double is passed in `xmm0`, so `al` gets the value 1.

```c
double d = 3.14;
printf("value: %d and %f\n", 42, d);
```

```asm
    lea rdi, [rip + .LC0]            ; 1st argument: format string
    mov esi, 42                      ; 2nd argument: the int 42
    movsd xmm0, QWORD PTR [rip + d]  ; 3rd argument: the double, in xmm0
    mov al, 1                        ; 1 vector register is used
    call printf@PLT
```

If the call used no vector registers at all, the caller would set
`al` to 0, for example with `xor eax, eax`.

#### Returning of Values

Functions return values with the following algorithm:

1. Classify the return type with the classification algorithm.

2. If the type has the class MEMORY, the caller gives the function
   space for the return value. The caller passes the address of this
   space in `rdi`, as if it were the first argument. In effect, this
   address becomes a "hidden" first argument. This space must not
   overlap any data that the callee can see through other names.

   On return, `rax` contains the address that the caller passed in
   `rdi`.

3. If the class is INTEGER, use the next free register of the
   sequence `rax`, `rdx`.

4. If the class is SSE, use the next free vector register of the
   sequence `xmm0`, `xmm1`.

5. If the class is SSEUP, return the eightbyte in the next free
   eightbyte chunk of the last used vector register.

6. If the class is X87, return the value on the x87 stack in `st0`,
   as an 80-bit x87 number.

7. If the class is X87UP, return the value together with the previous
   x87 value in `st0`.

8. If the class is COMPLEX_X87, return the real part in `st0` and
   the imaginary part in `st1`.

#### Parameter Passing Example

Now we look at a full example of the register passing rules. Study
the declarations and the function call in the next figure.

**Figure: Parameter passing example**

```c
typedef struct {
    int a, b;
    double d;
} structparm;

structparm s;
int e, f, g, h, i, j, k;
long double ld;
double m, n;
__m256 y;
__m512 z;

extern void func (int e, int f,
                  structparm s, int g, int h,
                  long double ld, double m,
                  __m256 y,
                  __m512 z,
                  double n, int i, int j, int k);

func (e, f, s, g, h, ld, m, y, z, n, i, j, k);
```

The structure `s` is 16 bytes. Its first eightbyte (`s.a` and `s.b`,
two INTEGER fields) merges to INTEGER. Its second eightbyte (`s.d`)
is SSE. The value `ld` has the classes X87 and X87UP, so it goes in
memory. The values `y` and `z` are passed in one vector register
each, because their first eightbyte is SSE and the rest are SSEUP.
The integers `j` and `k` find no free integer register, so they go on
the stack.

The next figure shows where every argument lands. The stack frame
offsets show the frame right before the call.

**Figure: Register allocation for the example**

| General purpose registers | Vector registers | Stack frame offset |
|---|---|---|
| `rdi`: `e` | `xmm0`: `s.d` | 0: `ld` |
| `rsi`: `f` | `xmm1`: `m` | 16: `j` |
| `rdx`: `s.a`, `s.b` | `ymm2`: `y` | 24: `k` |
| `rcx`: `g` | `zmm3`: `z` | |
| `r8`: `h` | `xmm4`: `n` | |
| `r9`: `i` | | |

Note that `ld` takes 16 bytes on the stack (offsets 0 to 15), because
its size is rounded up to two eightbytes and it needs 16-byte
alignment. So `j` starts at offset 16 and `k` at offset 24.

## Operating System Interface

This section describes how a program talks to the operating system.
It covers exceptions, the address space, page sizes, and the layout
of memory.

### Exception Interface

The processor changes its mode to handle *exceptions*. An exception
is an event that stops the normal flow of instructions. The AMD64
architecture manuals describe this in detail.

There are three groups of exceptions:

- *synchronous* exceptions, caused directly by an instruction,
- *floating-point/coprocessor* exceptions, caused by floating-point
  instructions,
- *asynchronous* exceptions, which come from outside the program.

A process can create synchronous and floating-point/coprocessor
exceptions on purpose, because they come from instruction execution.
So this section only defines the behavior for those exception types.

The x86-64 architecture also sorts exceptions into *faults*, *traps*,
and *aborts*. In simple words: a fault points at the instruction that
failed, a trap points after it, and an abort may not report a precise
place at all. See the Intel and AMD architecture manuals for more
information about the differences.

#### Hardware Exception Types

A *signal* is a message from the operating system to a process. The
operating system maps each hardware exception to one of the signals
that `signal`(BA_OS) defines. The table below shows this mapping.

Note one difference from the i386 architecture: in long mode (64-bit
mode), the x86-64 has no instruction that creates a bounds check
fault.

**Table: Hardware exceptions and signals**

| Number | Exception name | Signal |
|---|---|---|
| `0` | `divide error fault` | `SIGFPE` |
| `1` | `single step trap/fault` | `SIGTRAP` |
| `2` | `non-maskable interrupt` | `none` |
| `3` | `breakpoint trap` | `SIGTRAP` |
| `4` | `overflow trap` | `SIGSEGV` |
| `5` | `(reserved)` | |
| `6` | `invalid opcode fault` | `SIGILL` |
| `7` | `no coprocessor fault` | `SIGFPE` |
| `8` | `double fault abort` | `none` |
| `9` | `coprocessor overrun abort` | `SIGSEGV` |
| `10` | `invalid TSS fault` | `none` |
| `11` | `segment not present fault` | `none` |
| `12` | `stack exception fault` | `SIGSEGV` |
| `13` | `general protection fault/abort` | `SIGSEGV` |
| `14` | `page fault` | `SIGSEGV` |
| `15` | `(reserved)` | |
| `16` | `coprocessor error fault` | `SIGFPE` |
| `other` | `(unspecified)` | `SIGILL` |

When a floating-point exception raises `SIGFPE`, the system also
gives a code. The code tells the reason for the exception.

**Table: Floating-point exceptions**

| Code | Reason |
|---|---|
| `FPE_FLTDIV` | `floating-point divide by zero` |
| `FPE_FLTOVF` | `floating-point overflow` |
| `FPE_FLTUND` | `floating-point underflow` |
| `FPE_FLTRES` | `floating-point inexact result` |
| `FPE_FLTINV` | `invalid floating-point operation` |

**Example:** The next program divides an integer by zero. The
processor raises exception number 0 (`divide error fault`). The
operating system turns this exception into the signal `SIGFPE` and
delivers it to the process.

```c
#include <signal.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <unistd.h>

void handler(int sig)
{
    /* sig is SIGFPE here. */
    write(1, "Got SIGFPE\n", 11);
    _exit(1);
}

int main(void)
{
    signal(SIGFPE, handler);
    volatile int zero = 0;
    return 1 / zero;   /* divide error fault -> SIGFPE */
}
```

Without the handler, the default action for `SIGFPE` ends the
process.

### Virtual Address Space

The *virtual address space* is the set of memory addresses that a
process can use. The x86-64 architecture uses 64-bit pointers. But an
implementation only has to handle 48-bit addresses. So a conforming
process may only use addresses from `0x00000000 00000000` to
`0x00007fff ffffffff`.

> **Note:** `0x0000ffff ffffffff` is not a canonical address. A
> program cannot use it. In a canonical address, the high bits are all
> copies of bit 47.

A process starts with three logical *segments*. A segment is a
continuous block of memory with one purpose. The three common
segments are called text (code), data, and stack. Shared libraries
add more segments. A process may also create new segments while it
runs.

### Page Size

Memory is managed in blocks called *pages*. A system may use any page
size that is a power of two between 4KB and 64KB, inclusive. So 4KB,
8KB, 16KB, 32KB, and 64KB are all allowed.

**Example:** Do not write code that assumes 4KB pages. Ask the system
instead. In C you can call `sysconf(_SC_PAGESIZE)`, or read the value
`AT_PAGESZ` from the auxiliary vector (see the section 'Auxiliary
Vector' below).

### Virtual Address Assignments

In theory, a process can use the full address space. In practice,
several things limit the size of a process:

- The system reserves an amount of virtual space. The amount depends
  on the configuration.
- The system also reserves an amount of space per process. This
  amount also depends on the configuration.
- A process cannot run if it is bigger than the combined physical
  memory and secondary storage of the system. Some physical memory
  must be present to run any process. But the system can run
  processes bigger than physical memory. It does this by paging: it
  moves pages between memory and secondary storage. Physical memory
  and secondary storage are shared resources. The system load changes
  from one run to the next, so the available amount also changes.

A program that reads or writes through a null pointer is wrong. A
process should not expect address `0x0` to be valid.

**Figure: Virtual address configuration**

```
Address              Content                    Comment
------------------   ---------------------     -------------------
0xffffffffffffffff   Reserved system area      End of memory
                     ...
                     ...
0x80000000000        Dynamic segments
                     ...
0                    Process segments          Beginning of memory
```

An application may control where its memory goes. But the typical
arrangement looks like this:

**Figure: Conventional segment arrangements**

```
Address              Content
------------------   ---------------------
                     ...
0x80000000000        Dynamic segments
                     Stack segment
                     ...
                     ...
                     Data segments
                     ...
0x400000             Text segments
0                    Unmapped
```

In simple words: the program code (text) starts at `0x400000`. The
data comes above it. The stack sits below `0x80000000000` and grows
down. Dynamic segments, like shared libraries and `mmap` areas, start
at `0x80000000000`. The page at address 0 is not mapped, so a null
pointer access fails at once.

## Process Initialization

This section describes the state of a process at the moment it
starts.

### Initial Stack and Register State

#### Special Registers

The x86-64 architecture has floating-point instructions. There are
two floating-point units: SSE2 and x87. At process startup, both
units have all floating-point exception status flags cleared. The
control words have the values shown in the next two tables.

The x87 control word selects rounding, precision, and which
exceptions are masked. A *masked* exception does not stop the
program; the unit produces a default result instead.

**Table: x87 floating-point control word at startup**

| Field | Value | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `RC` | 0 | Round to nearest |
| `PC` | 11 | Double extended precision |
| `PM` | 1 | Precision masked |
| `UM` | 1 | Underflow masked |
| `OM` | 1 | Overflow masked |
| `ZM` | 1 | Zero divide masked |
| `DM` | 1 | De-normal operand masked |
| `IM` | 1 | Invalid operation masked |

The MXCSR register controls the SSE2 unit in a similar way.

**Table: MXCSR status bits at startup**

| Field | Value | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `FZ` | 0 | Do not flush to zero |
| `RC` | 0 | Round to nearest |
| `PM` | 1 | Precision masked |
| `UM` | 1 | Underflow masked |
| `OM` | 1 | Overflow masked |
| `ZM` | 1 | Zero divide masked |
| `DM` | 1 | De-normal operand masked |
| `IM` | 1 | Invalid operation masked |
| `DAZ` | 0 | De-normals are not zero |

The `rFLAGS` register holds the system flags, such as the direction
flag and the carry flag. Application software can access the low 16
bits (the FLAGS part) of `rFLAGS`. Their state at process start is
shown below.

**Table: `rFLAGS` bits at startup**

| Field | Value | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `DF` | 0 | Direction forward |
| `CF` | 0 | No carry |
| `PF` | 0 | Even parity |
| `AF` | 0 | No auxiliary carry |
| `ZF` | 0 | No zero result |
| `SF` | 0 | Unsigned result |
| `OF` | 0 | No overflow occurred |

#### Stack State

This section describes the machine state that `exec`(BA_OS) creates
for a new process. Each language implementation then transforms this
initial state into the state that its language standard needs.

For example, a C program begins at a function named `main`, declared
as:

```c
extern int main ( int argc , char *argv[ ] , char* envp[ ] );
```

where:

- `argc` is a non-negative argument count,
- `argv` is an array of argument strings, with `argv[argc] == 0`,
- `envp` is an array of environment strings, ended by a null pointer.

When `main()` returns, its value goes to `exit()`. If the user has
replaced `exit()` and that replacement returns, the value goes to
`_exit()`. The function `_exit()` must be safe from user replacement.

The figure below shows the initial state of the process stack. This
is the state at the moment the system calls `_start`, the first code
of the program. An *eightbyte* is a block of 8 bytes.

**Figure: Initial process stack**

| Purpose | Start address | Length |
|---|---|---|
| Unspecified | High addresses | |
| Information block, including argument strings, environment strings, auxiliary information ... | | varies |
| Unspecified | | |
| Null auxiliary vector entry | | 1 eightbyte |
| Auxiliary vector entries ... | | 2 eightbytes each |
| 0 | | eightbyte |
| Environment pointers ... | | 1 eightbyte each |
| 0 | `rsp + 8 + 8*argc` | eightbyte |
| Argument pointers | `rsp + 8` | argc eightbytes |
| Argument count | `rsp` | eightbyte |
| Undefined | Low addresses | |

The argument strings, the environment strings, and the auxiliary
information sit inside the information block. They appear in no
specific order there. They also do not need to be packed tightly.

Only the registers below have specified values at process entry:

- `rbp`: The content of this register is unspecified at process
  start. But user code should mark the deepest stack frame by setting
  the frame pointer to zero.
- `rsp`: The stack pointer holds the address of the lowest byte that
  is part of the stack. It is guaranteed to be 16-byte aligned at
  process entry.
- `rdx`: A function pointer. The application should register this
  function with `atexit`(BA_OS), so it runs at program exit.

It is unspecified whether the data and stack segments start with
execute permission or not. An application that needs to run code on
the stack or in the data segment should take care of this itself. For
example, it can call `mprotect()` to set the permission.

**Example:** A small C program that prints its arguments:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(int argc, char *argv[], char *envp[])
{
    for (int i = 0; i < argc; i++)
        printf("argv[%d] = %s\n", i, argv[i]);
    return 0;
}
```

Run it as `./demo one two`. Then `argc` is 3. At the moment `_start`
runs, the stack looks like this (`rsp` is the initial stack pointer):

```
Higher addresses
  ...        argument strings ("./demo", "one", "two"),
             environment strings, auxiliary information
  ...        auxiliary vector entries, ended by a null entry
  ...        environment pointers (envp), ended by 0
  rsp + 40   first environment pointer = envp[0]
  rsp + 32   0                   (end of the argv array)
  rsp + 24   pointer to "two"    (argv[2])
  rsp + 16   pointer to "one"    (argv[1])
  rsp + 8    pointer to "./demo" (argv[0])
  rsp        3                   (argc)
Lower addresses
```

The end marker of `argv` sits at `rsp + 8 + 8*argc`, which here is
`rsp + 32`. The `envp` array starts right after it, at `rsp + 40`.

**Example:** A minimal `_start` stub in assembly (Intel syntax). It
reads `argc` from `[rsp]`, builds the three arguments for `main`, and
exits with the return value of `main`:

```asm
    .intel_syntax noprefix
    .text
    .globl _start
_start:
    xor  ebp, ebp                # rbp = 0 marks the deepest frame
    mov  r12, rdx                # save the atexit function pointer
    mov  rdi, [rsp]              # rdi = argc (eightbyte at rsp)
    lea  rsi, [rsp + 8]          # rsi = argv (starts at rsp + 8)
    lea  rdx, [rsp + rdi*8 + 16] # rdx = envp (after argv and its 0)
    and  rsp, -16                # keep the stack 16-byte aligned
    call main                    # main(argc, argv, envp)
    mov  edi, eax                # exit code = return value of main
    mov  eax, 60                 # Linux system call number for exit
    syscall
```

A real `_start` would also register the function saved in `r12` with
`atexit`. This small stub skips that step for clarity.

### Thread State

A new thread inherits the floating-point state of its parent thread.
After that, the state is private to the new thread.

### Auxiliary Vector

The *auxiliary vector* is an array on the initial stack. The kernel
uses it to pass extra information to the process. Each element is a
structure of the following type. The `a_type` member tells how to
read the rest of the entry.

**Figure: `auxv_t` type definition**

```c
 typedef struct
 {
     int a_type;
     union {
         long a_val;
         void *a_ptr;
         void (*a_fnc)();
     } a_un;
 } auxv_t;
```

The x86-64 ABI uses the auxiliary vector types in the next table. The
last column shows which member of the union `a_un` holds the value.

**Table: Auxiliary vector types**

| Name | Value | `a_un` |
|---|---|---|
| `AT_NULL` | 0 | ignored |
| `AT_IGNORE` | 1 | ignored |
| `AT_EXECFD` | 2 | `a_val` |
| `AT_PHDR` | 3 | `a_ptr` |
| `AT_PHENT` | 4 | `a_val` |
| `AT_PHNUM` | 5 | `a_val` |
| `AT_PAGESZ` | 6 | `a_val` |
| `AT_BASE` | 7 | `a_ptr` |
| `AT_FLAGS` | 8 | `a_val` |
| `AT_ENTRY` | 9 | `a_ptr` |
| `AT_NOTELF` | 10 | `a_val` |
| `AT_UID` | 11 | `a_val` |
| `AT_EUID` | 12 | `a_val` |
| `AT_GID` | 13 | `a_val` |
| `AT_EGID` | 14 | `a_val` |
| `AT_PLATFORM` | 15 | `a_ptr` |
| `AT_HWCAP` | 16 | `a_val` |
| `AT_CLKTCK` | 17 | `a_val` |
| `AT_SECURE` | 23 | `a_val` |
| `AT_BASE_PLATFORM` | 24 | `a_ptr` |
| `AT_RANDOM` | 25 | `a_ptr` |
| `AT_HWCAP2` | 26 | `a_val` |
| `AT_EXECFN` | 31 | `a_ptr` |

The meaning of each type:

- `AT_NULL`: The auxiliary vector has no fixed length. Instead, the
  `a_type` member of the last entry has this value.
- `AT_IGNORE`: The entry has no meaning. The value of `a_un` is
  undefined for this entry.
- `AT_EXECFD`: At process creation, the system may pass control to an
  interpreter program. An *interpreter* is a helper program, usually
  the dynamic linker, that prepares the real program. In this case,
  the system puts either an `AT_EXECFD` entry or an `AT_PHDR` entry
  in the auxiliary vector. For `AT_EXECFD`, the `a_val` member holds
  a file descriptor. This descriptor is open for reading the object
  file of the application program.
- `AT_PHDR`: The system may build the memory image of the application
  program before it passes control to the interpreter. In this case,
  the `a_ptr` member of the `AT_PHDR` entry tells the interpreter
  where the program header table is in the memory image.
- `AT_PHENT`: The `a_val` member holds the size, in bytes, of one
  entry in the program header table that `AT_PHDR` points to.
- `AT_PHNUM`: The `a_val` member holds the number of entries in the
  program header table that `AT_PHDR` points to.
- `AT_PAGESZ`: If present, the `a_val` member gives the system page
  size, in bytes.
- `AT_BASE`: The `a_ptr` member holds the base address where the
  interpreter program was loaded into memory. See "Program Header" in
  the System V ABI for more information about the base address.
- `AT_FLAGS`: If present, the `a_val` member holds one-bit flags.
  Bits with undefined meaning are set to zero.
- `AT_ENTRY`: The `a_ptr` member holds the entry point of the
  application program. The interpreter program should transfer
  control to this address.
- `AT_NOTELF`: The `a_val` member is non-zero if the program is in a
  format other than ELF.
- `AT_UID`: The `a_val` member holds the real user id of the process.
- `AT_EUID`: The `a_val` member holds the effective user id of the
  process.
- `AT_GID`: The `a_val` member holds the real group id of the
  process.
- `AT_EGID`: The `a_val` member holds the effective group id of the
  process.
- `AT_PLATFORM`: The `a_ptr` member points to a string with the
  platform name.
- `AT_HWCAP`: The `a_val` member holds a bit mask of CPU features. It
  matches the value that CPUID function 1 returns in `edx`.
- `AT_CLKTCK`: The `a_val` member holds the frequency at which
  `times()` increments.
- `AT_SECURE`: The `a_val` member holds one if the program is in
  secure mode (for example, it started with suid). Otherwise it holds
  zero.
- `AT_BASE_PLATFORM`: The `a_ptr` member points to a string that
  names the base architecture platform. This may differ from the
  platform in `AT_PLATFORM`.
- `AT_RANDOM`: The `a_ptr` member points to 16 securely generated
  random bytes.
- `AT_HWCAP2`: The `a_val` member holds the extended hardware feature
  mask. It is currently 0, but it may hold more feature bits in the
  future.
- `AT_EXECFN`: The `a_ptr` member points to the file name of the
  executed program.

**Example:** On GNU/Linux, the function `getauxval()` reads a value
from the auxiliary vector. This program reads the page size from
`AT_PAGESZ` and two more values:

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <sys/auxv.h>

int main(void)
{
    unsigned long page_size = getauxval(AT_PAGESZ);
    unsigned long secure    = getauxval(AT_SECURE);
    const char *execfn      = (const char *) getauxval(AT_EXECFN);

    printf("page size  : %lu bytes\n", page_size);
    printf("secure mode: %lu\n", secure);
    printf("program    : %s\n", execfn);
    return 0;
}
```

On a typical system this prints a page size of 4096 bytes. But
remember: the ABI allows any power-of-two page size from 4KB to 64KB.
So always read the real value, as this program does.

**Example:** You can also walk the auxiliary vector by hand. It
starts right after the `envp` array on the initial stack:

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <elf.h>

int main(int argc, char *argv[], char *envp[])
{
    char **p = envp;
    while (*p != 0)        /* skip the environment pointers */
        p++;
    p++;                   /* skip the final 0 of envp */

    for (Elf64_auxv_t *aux = (Elf64_auxv_t *) p;
         aux->a_type != AT_NULL; aux++) {
        if (aux->a_type == AT_PAGESZ)
            printf("AT_PAGESZ = %lu\n", aux->a_un.a_val);
    }
    return 0;
}
```

The loop stops at the entry with type `AT_NULL`, because that entry
marks the end of the vector.

## Coding Examples

This section shows example code for basic operations. It shows how to
call functions, how to access static objects, and how to jump from one
part of a program to another. Different from the material before it,
this material is not normative. This means: it does not define rules.
It only shows common ways to do things.

All assembly in this section uses Intel syntax. In Intel syntax, the
destination operand comes first. So `mov rax, rbx` copies `rbx` into
`rax`. A memory access is written with square brackets, for example
`[rip + sym]`.

### Architectural Constraints

The AMD64 architecture usually does not allow an instruction to hold a
full 64-bit constant as an *immediate operand*. An immediate operand is
a constant that is stored inside the instruction itself. Most
instructions accept 32-bit immediates. The processor *sign extends*
them to 64 bits. Sign extension means: the processor copies the top bit
to fill the upper 32 bits. Also, 32-bit operations that write to a
register set the upper 32 bits to zero. This is called *zero
extension*. Because of this, loading a 64-bit value whose upper half is
zero is even cheaper.

Branch instructions also accept 32-bit immediate operands. The
processor sign extends them and adds them to the instruction pointer.
In the same way, there is an addressing mode for data that is relative
to the instruction pointer (`rip`). It has the same 32-bit limit.

To make code faster and smaller, it is good to use different *code
models* for different needs. A code model is a set of limits on the
addresses of code and data. These limits let the compiler generate
better code. Code models differ in four points: addressing (absolute or
position-independent), code size, data size, and address range. Only a
small number of generally useful code models are defined:

**Small code model.** The virtual address of the code is known at link
time. Also, all symbols are known to live in virtual addresses from 0
to 2^31 - 2^24 - 1, that is from 0x00000000 to 0x7effffff.

> **Note:** The number 24 is an arbitrary choice. It lets a program
> address every byte of an object of up to 2^24 bytes (16M bytes)
> directly, because the base address of such an object must be below
> 2^31 - 2^24, that is 0x7f000000. Without this rule, only the base
> address itself would be reachable directly, but not base plus offset.

With these limits, the compiler can encode symbol references in three
ways:

- Offsets from -(2^31) to 2^24 (0x80000000 to 0x01000000) fit directly
  in sign extended immediate operands.
- Offsets from 0 to 2^31 - 2^24 (0x00000000 to 0x7f000000) fit in zero
  extended immediate operands.
- Offsets from -(2^24) to 2^24 (0xff000000 to 0x01000000) can use
  `rip`-relative addressing.

This is the fastest code model. It should fit the vast majority of
programs.

**Kernel code model.** The kernel of an operating system is usually
small, but it runs in the negative half of the address space. So all
symbols are defined to be in the range from 2^64 - 2^31 to 2^64 - 2^24,
that is from 0xffffffff80000000 to 0xffffffffff000000. This model has
advantages similar to the small model. But zero extended symbol
references only work for offsets from 2^31 to 2^31 + 2^24 (0x80000000
to 0x81000000). The range for sign extended references changes to 0 to
2^31 + 2^24 (0x00000000 to 0x81000000).

**Medium code model.** The data sections are split into two parts. The
regular data sections keep the same limits as in the small code model.
The large data sections have no limits except the available address
space. The program layout must make sure that the large data sections
(`.ldata`, `.lrodata`, `.lbss`) do not sit between the regular text and
data sections.

In this model, the compiler must use `movabs` instructions to access
large static data and to load such addresses into registers. `movabs`
is the only instruction that can use a full 64-bit address or 64-bit
constant. For the small data and text sections, the model keeps the
advantages of the small code model. This is important for branches. By
default, only data larger than 65535 bytes goes into the large data
section.

**Large code model.** The large code model makes no assumptions about
the addresses and sizes of sections. It is not strictly needed, but the
data sections can still be split into normal and large parts, like in
the medium model. This improves interoperability.

The compiler must use the `movabs` instruction, as in the medium code
model, even for addresses inside the text section. Also, indirect
branches are needed when the code branches to an address whose distance
from the current instruction pointer is unknown.

You can avoid the text section limit of the small and medium models in
another way: break the program into multiple shared libraries. So the
large model is strictly needed only when the text of a single function
grows larger than the medium model allows.

**Small position-independent code model (PIC).** Position-independent
code (PIC) is code that works at any load address. Different from the
models above, the virtual addresses of instructions and data are not
known until dynamic link time. So all addresses have to be relative to
the instruction pointer. Also, the maximum distance between a symbol
and the end of an instruction is limited to 2^31 - 2^24 - 1, that is
0x7effffff. So for every symbol with an offset in the range -(2^24) to
2^24 (0xff000000 to 0x01000000), the compiler can use the
`rip`-relative branches and addressing modes of the hardware.

**Medium position-independent code model (PIC).** This model is like
the small PIC model. But like the medium static model, it adds large
data sections at the end of object files. In the medium PIC model,
`rip`-relative addressing cannot be used directly for large static
data. The offset can be bigger than the displacement field of the
instruction allows. Instead, the code needs an unwind sequence made of
`movabs`, `lea` and `add`.

**Large position-independent code model (PIC).** This model is like the
medium PIC model, but it makes no assumptions about the distance of
symbols. It has the same limits as the medium PIC model for addressing
static data. In addition, references to the global offset table, to the
procedure linkage table, and branch targets must be computed in a
similar way. The text segment may be up to 16EB in size. So the same
restrictions apply to all address references into the text segment,
including branches.

Only the small code model and the small position-independent code model
(PIC) are used in ILP32 binaries.

**Example:** How do you pick a code model in practice? Imagine three
programs. Program A is a normal tool. Its code and data together are a
