few megabytes, far below the 0x7effffff limit. It uses the small model.
Program B is a scientific program with one static array of 4 GB. The
array does not fit under the small model limit, so program B uses the
medium model. The array goes into the `.ldata` section, and only
accesses to the array need `movabs`. Program C is machine-generated
code where one single function is bigger than 2 GB. Only program C
needs the large model.

### Conventions

The coding examples use some special assembler symbols. They are:

- `name@GOT`: the offset from the base of the GOT to the GOT entry for
  the symbol `name`. The GOT (global offset table) is a table of
  addresses that the dynamic linker fills in.
- `name@GOTOFF`: the offset from the base of the GOT to the location of
  the symbol `name` itself.
- `name@GOTPCREL`: the offset from the current code location to the GOT
  entry for the symbol `name`.
- `name@PLT`: the offset from the current code location to the PLT
  entry of the symbol `name`. The PLT (procedure linkage table) is a
  table of small code stubs used to call functions in other modules.
- `name@PLTOFF`: the offset from the base of the GOT to the PLT entry
  of the symbol `name`.
- `_GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_`: the offset from the current code location to
  the base of the GOT.

### Position-Independent Function Prologue

The *prologue* of a function is the code at its start. It sets up the
stack frame and saves registers.

In the small code model, all addresses (including GOT entries) are
reachable with the `rip`-relative addressing of the AMD64 architecture.
So there is no need for an explicit GOT pointer, and no prologue code
to set one up.

In the medium and large code models, a register must be set aside to
hold the address of the GOT in position-independent objects. The reason
is that the AMD64 ISA does not support an immediate displacement larger
than 32 bits.

Because `r15` is preserved across function calls, the prologue
initializes it to hold the GOT address. This is done for non-leaf
functions that call other functions through the PLT. A *leaf function*
is a function that calls no other function. Other functions are free to
use any other register. In this document, the examples use `r15`.

> **Note:** Sometimes the compiler can see, at code generation time,
> that the function needs no GOT pointer. This happens when the
> function is a leaf function, or when all called function addresses
> can be resolved and are within 2GB, or when the function refers to no
> global data objects. In these cases the GOT address does not need to
> be stored in `r15`, and the prologue code that initializes it may be
> left out.

**Figure: Position-Independent Function Prologue Code**

Medium model:

```asm
    lea     r15, [rip + _GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_]  # GOTPC32 relocation
```

Large model:

```asm
    push    r15                                 # save r15
    lea     r11, [rip + 1f]                     # absolute value of rip at label 1
1:  movabs  r15, OFFSET _GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_   # offset to the GOT (R_X86_64_GOTPC64)
    lea     r15, [r11 + r15]                    # absolute address of the GOT
```

For the medium model, the GOT pointer is loaded directly. For the large
model, the code adds the absolute value of `rip` to the relative offset
of the GOT base. The result is the absolute address of the GOT (see the
figure "Position-Independent Function Prologue Code").

### Data Objects

This section describes only objects with static storage. Objects on the
stack are not covered, because programs always compute their virtual
addresses relative to the stack pointer or the frame pointer.

Only the `movabs` instruction can use 64-bit addresses directly. So,
depending on the code model, the code must either use `rip`-relative
addressing, or build the address in a register and access memory
through that register.

For absolute addresses, `rip`-relative encoding can be used in the
small model. In the medium model, the `movabs` instruction has to be
used to access addresses.

Position-independent code cannot contain absolute addresses. To access
a global symbol, the code has to load the address of the symbol from
the global offset table. In the small model, the address of the GOT
entry can be found with a `rip`-relative instruction.

#### Small Models

**Figure: Absolute Load and Store (Small Model)**

The C declarations and the matching assembler directives:

```c
extern int src[65536];      /* .extern src              */
extern int dst[65536];      /* .extern dst              */
extern int *ptr;            /* .extern ptr              */
static int lsrc[65536];     /* .local lsrc              */
                            /* .comm  lsrc,262144,4     */
static int ldst[65536];     /* .local ldst              */
                            /* .comm  ldst,262144,4     */
static int *lptr;           /* .local lptr              */
                            /* .comm  lptr,8,8          */
```

The C statements and the code for them:

```asm
        .text
# dst[0] = src[0];
        mov     eax, [rip + src]
        mov     [rip + dst], eax

# ptr = dst;
        mov     QWORD PTR [rip + ptr], OFFSET dst

# *ptr = src[0];
        mov     rax, [rip + ptr]
        mov     edx, [rip + src]
        mov     [rax], edx

# ldst[0] = lsrc[0];
        mov     eax, [rip + lsrc]
        mov     [rip + ldst], eax

# lptr = ldst;
        mov     QWORD PTR [rip + lptr], OFFSET ldst

# *lptr = lsrc[0];
        mov     rax, [rip + lptr]
        mov     edx, [rip + lsrc]
        mov     [rax], edx
```

Here `OFFSET dst` means: the constant address of `dst`, stored as an
immediate in the instruction. The linker fills in the real value.

**Figure: Position-Independent Load and Store (Small PIC Model)**

The declarations are the same as in the figure above. The code changes,
because the address of every global symbol must come from the GOT:

```asm
        .text
# dst[0] = src[0];
        mov     rax, [rip + src@GOTPCREL]
        mov     edx, [rax]
        mov     rax, [rip + dst@GOTPCREL]
        mov     [rax], edx

# ptr = dst;
        mov     rax, [rip + ptr@GOTPCREL]
        mov     rdx, [rip + dst@GOTPCREL]
        mov     [rax], rdx

# *ptr = src[0];
        mov     rax, [rip + ptr@GOTPCREL]
        mov     rdx, [rax]
        mov     rax, [rip + src@GOTPCREL]
        mov     eax, [rax]
        mov     [rdx], eax

# ldst[0] = lsrc[0];
        mov     eax, [rip + lsrc]
        mov     [rip + ldst], eax

# lptr = ldst;
        lea     rdx, [rip + ldst]
        mov     [rip + lptr], rdx

# *lptr = lsrc[0];
        mov     rax, [rip + lptr]
        mov     edx, [rip + lsrc]
        mov     [rax], edx
```

Note the difference: the local (static) symbols `lsrc`, `ldst` and
`lptr` do not need the GOT. The compiler knows they sit at a fixed,
small distance from the code. So it can still use direct
`rip`-relative access for them.

**Example:** Here is one tiny complete function, first for the small
model, then for the small PIC model.

```c
int x;

int get_x(void)
{
    return x;
}
```

Small model (absolute):

```asm
get_x:
        mov     eax, [rip + x]       # one memory access
        ret
```

Small PIC model:

```asm
get_x:
        mov     rax, [rip + x@GOTPCREL]  # 1: load the address of x from the GOT
        mov     eax, [rax]               # 2: load the value of x
        ret
```

The PIC version needs two memory accesses instead of one. This is the
usual price of position independence for global symbols.

#### Medium Models

**Figure: Absolute Load and Store (Medium Model)**

The declarations are the same as in the small model figure:

```c
extern int src[65536];      /* .extern src              */
extern int dst[65536];      /* .extern dst              */
extern int *ptr;            /* .extern ptr              */
static int lsrc[65536];     /* .local lsrc              */
                            /* .comm  lsrc,262144,4     */
static int ldst[65536];     /* .local ldst              */
                            /* .comm  ldst,262144,4     */
static int *lptr;           /* .local lptr              */
                            /* .comm  lptr,8,8          */
```

> **Note:** The linker is responsible for placing the symbol in the
> large data section.

The arrays are large, so they live in the large data sections. Their
addresses do not fit in 32 bits. The code must use `movabs`:

```asm
        .text
# dst[0] = src[0];
        movabs  eax, [src]           # load through a 64-bit absolute address
        movabs  [dst], eax           # store through a 64-bit absolute address

# ptr = dst;
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET dst
        mov     [rip + ptr], rdx

# *ptr = src[0];
        mov     rdx, [rip + ptr]
        movabs  eax, [src]
        mov     [rdx], eax

# ldst[0] = lsrc[0];
        movabs  eax, [lsrc]
        movabs  [ldst], eax

# lptr = ldst;
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET ldst
        movabs  [lptr], rdx

# *lptr = lsrc[0];
        mov     rdx, [rip + lptr]
        movabs  eax, [lsrc]
        mov     [rdx], eax
```

Here `movabs eax, [src]` is the special form of `mov` that holds a full
64-bit absolute address inside the instruction. This form only works
with the accumulator register (`al`, `ax`, `eax`, `rax`). The small
pointer `ptr` still lives in the regular data section, so normal
`rip`-relative access works for it.

**Figure: Position-Independent Load and Store (Medium PIC Model)**

The declarations are the same again. For the external symbols, the code
is the same as in the small PIC model:

```asm
        .text
# dst[0] = src[0];
        mov     rax, [rip + src@GOTPCREL]
        mov     edx, [rax]
        mov     rax, [rip + dst@GOTPCREL]
        mov     [rax], edx

# ptr = dst;
        mov     rax, [rip + ptr@GOTPCREL]
        mov     rdx, [rip + dst@GOTPCREL]
        mov     [rax], rdx

# *ptr = src[0];
        mov     rax, [rip + ptr@GOTPCREL]
        mov     rdx, [rax]
        mov     rax, [rip + src@GOTPCREL]
        mov     eax, [rax]
        mov     [rdx], eax
```

**Figure: Position-Independent Load and Store (Medium PIC Model), continued**

For the large local data, the code loads the 64-bit offset of the
symbol from the GOT base with `movabs`, and adds the GOT pointer in
`r15`:

```asm
# ldst[0] = lsrc[0];
        movabs  rax, OFFSET lsrc@GOTOFF
        mov     eax, [rax + r15]
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET ldst@GOTOFF
        mov     [rdx + r15], eax

# lptr = ldst;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET ldst@GOTOFF
        add     rax, r15
        mov     [rip + lptr], rax

# *lptr = lsrc[0];
        movabs  rax, OFFSET lsrc@GOTOFF
        mov     eax, [rax + r15]
        mov     rdx, [rip + lptr]
        mov     [rdx], eax
```

#### Large Models

To access data at any position in the 64-bit address space, the code
must again compute the address explicitly, like in the medium code
model.

> **Note:** Sometimes the compiler can see, at code generation time,
> that the address of a global data object resolves within 2GB. Then it
> can use the `rip`-relative addressing mode instead. See the figure
> "Faster Absolute Global Data Load and Store".

**Figure: Absolute Global Data Load and Store**

The declarations:

```c
static int src;             /* Lsrc: .long              */
static int dst;             /* Ldst: .long              */
extern int *ptr;            /*       .extern ptr        */
```

The code:

```asm
# dst = src;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lsrc     # R_X86_64_64
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET Ldst     # R_X86_64_64
        mov     ecx, [rax]
        mov     [rdx], ecx

# ptr = &dst;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET ptr      # R_X86_64_64
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET Ldst     # R_X86_64_64
        mov     [rax], rdx

# *ptr = src;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lsrc     # R_X86_64_64
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET ptr      # R_X86_64_64
        mov     ecx, [rax]
        mov     rdx, [rdx]
        mov     [rdx], ecx
```

The comment after each `movabs` names the relocation that the assembler
emits for it.

**Figure: Faster Absolute Global Data Load and Store**

If `Lsrc` is known to be within 2GB of the code, the last statement can
be shorter:

```asm
# *ptr = src;
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET ptr      # R_X86_64_64
        mov     ecx, [rip + Lsrc]
        mov     rdx, [rdx]
        mov     [rdx], ecx
```

For position-independent code, access to static and external global
data assumes that the GOT address is stored in a dedicated register.
These examples assume it is in `r15` (see the section
"Position-Independent Function Prologue").

> **Note:** Sometimes the compiler can see, at code generation time,
> that the address of a global data object resolves within 2GB. Then it
> can use the `rip`-relative addressing mode instead. See the figure
> "Faster Position-Independent Global Data Load and Store".

**Figure: Position-Independent Global Data Load and Store**

The declarations are the same as in the absolute figure above.

```asm
# dst = src;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lsrc@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET Ldst@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        mov     ecx, [rax + r15]
        mov     [rdx + r15], ecx

# ptr = &dst;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET ptr@GOT       # R_X86_64_GOT64
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET Ldst@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        mov     rax, [rax + r15]
        lea     rcx, [rdx + r15]
        mov     [rax], rcx

# *ptr = src;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lsrc@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET ptr@GOT       # R_X86_64_GOT64
        mov     ecx, [rax + r15]
        mov     rdx, [rdx + r15]
        mov     [rdx], ecx
```

**Figure: Faster Position-Independent Global Data Load and Store**

```asm
# *ptr = src;
        movabs  rdx, OFFSET ptr@GOT       # R_X86_64_GOT64
        mov     ecx, [rip + Lsrc]
        mov     rdx, [rdx + r15]
        mov     [rdx], ecx
```

### Function Calls

#### Small and Medium Models

**Figure: Position-Independent Direct Function Call (Small and Medium Model)**

```c
extern void function ();    /* .globl function          */

function ();
```

```asm
        call    function@PLT
```

**Figure: Position-Independent Indirect Function Call**

```c
extern void (*ptr) ();      /* .globl ptr, name         */
extern void name ();
```

```asm
# ptr = name;
        mov     rax, [rip + ptr@GOTPCREL]
        mov     rdx, [rip + name@GOTPCREL]
        mov     [rax], rdx

# (*ptr)();
        mov     rax, [rip + ptr@GOTPCREL]
        call    [rax]
```

Here `call [rax]` is an indirect call: the processor loads the target
address from the memory that `rax` points to, and calls it.

#### Large Models

In general, you cannot assume that a function is within 2GB. So the
code must compute the target address explicitly. The address can then
reach the whole 64-bit address space.

**Figure: Absolute Direct and Indirect Function Call**

The declarations:

```c
static void (*ptr) (void);  /* Lptr: .quad              */
extern void foo (void);     /*       .globl foo         */
static void bar (void);     /* Lbar: ...                */
```

The code:

```asm
# foo ();
        movabs  r11, OFFSET foo      # R_X86_64_64
        call    r11
# bar ();
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Lbar     # R_X86_64_64
        call    r11

# ptr = foo;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lptr     # R_X86_64_64
        movabs  r11, OFFSET foo      # R_X86_64_64
        mov     [rax], r11
# ptr = bar;
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Lbar     # R_X86_64_64
        mov     [rax], r11

# (*ptr) ();
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Lptr     # R_X86_64_64
        call    [r11]
```

`call r11` calls the address that is in the register. `call [r11]`
calls the address stored in memory at the location in `r11`.

And here is the same for position-independent objects:

> **Note:** See the part "Implementation Advice" below for some
> optimizations.

**Figure: Position-Independent Direct and Indirect Function Call**

The declarations are the same as in the absolute figure above.

```asm
# foo ();
        movabs  r11, OFFSET foo@PLTOFF    # R_X86_64_PLTOFF64
        call    [r11 + r15]
# bar ();
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Lbar@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        lea     r11, [r11 + r15]
        call    r11

# ptr = foo;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lptr@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        movabs  r11, OFFSET foo@PLTOFF    # R_X86_64_PLTOFF64
        lea     r11, [r11 + r15]
        mov     [rax + r15], r11
# ptr = bar;
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Lbar@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        lea     r11, [r11 + r15]
        mov     [rax + r15], r11

# (*ptr) ();
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Lptr@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        call    [r11 + r15]
```

#### Implementation Advice

Sometimes the compiler can prove certain conditions at code generation
time. Then it can generate faster or smaller code than the large model
normally requires. The cases are:

**(Absolute) The target of the function call is within 2GB.** A direct
call or `rip`-relative addressing can be used:

```asm
# bar ();
        call    Lbar

# ptr = bar;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lptr     # R_X86_64_64
        lea     r11, [rip + Lbar]
        mov     [rax], r11
```

**(PIC) The base of the GOT is within 2GB.** An indirect call through
the GOT entry can be implemented like this:

```asm
# foo ();
        call    [rip + foo@GOTPCREL] # R_X86_64_GOTPCREL
```

**(PIC) The base of the PLT is within 2GB.** The PLT entry can be
referenced relative to `rip`:

```asm
# ptr = foo;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lptr@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        lea     r11, [rip + foo@PLT]      # R_X86_64_PLT32
        mov     [rax + r15], r11
```

**(PIC) The target of the function call is within 2GB**, and the target
is not global or is bound locally. Then a direct call to the symbol can
be used, or the symbol can be referenced relative to `rip`:

```asm
# bar ();
        call    Lbar

# ptr = bar;
        movabs  rax, OFFSET Lptr@GOTOFF   # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        lea     r11, [rip + Lbar]
        mov     [rax + r15], r11
```

### Branching

#### Small and Medium Models

All labels are within 2GB. So branches need no special care. The full
AMD64 ISA is usable.

**Example:** A simple `switch` statement in the small model can use a
compact jump table. A jump table is an array of addresses or offsets;
the code picks one entry and jumps to it.

```c
int classify(int x)
{
    switch (x) {
    case 0:  return 10;
    case 1:  return 20;
    case 2:  return 30;
    default: return -1;
    }
}
```

A compiler can produce this Intel-syntax code. It stores 32-bit offsets
relative to the table, so the table also works in the small PIC model:

```asm
classify:
        cmp     edi, 2               # x is in edi (first integer argument)
        ja      .Ldefault            # unsigned check: x < 0 or x > 2
        mov     eax, edi
        lea     rcx, [rip + .Ltable] # address of the jump table
        movsxd  rax, DWORD PTR [rcx + rax*4]  # load the 32-bit offset for case x
        add     rax, rcx             # offset + table base = target address
        jmp     rax
        .section .rodata
        .align  4
.Ltable:
        .long   .Lcase0 - .Ltable
        .long   .Lcase1 - .Ltable
        .long   .Lcase2 - .Ltable
        .previous
.Lcase0:
        mov     eax, 10
        ret
.Lcase1:
        mov     eax, 20
        ret
.Lcase2:
        mov     eax, 30
        ret
.Ldefault:
        mov     eax, -1
        ret
```

#### Large Models

Functions can, in theory, be up to 16EB long. The maximum 32-bit
displacement of conditional and unconditional branches in the AMD64 ISA
is then not enough to reach the branch target. So the code computes the
branch target address explicitly.

> **Note:** If the compiler can see, at code generation time, that the
> target addresses are within 2GB, the branch target addresses may
> instead be computed implicitly. See the figure "Implicit Calculation
> of Target Address".

For absolute objects:

**Figure: Absolute Branching Code**

```asm
# if (!a)
# {
        test    eax, eax
        jnz     1f
        movabs  r11, OFFSET 2f       # R_X86_64_64
        jmp     r11
#    ...
1:      ...
# }
2:
```

```asm
# goto Label;
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Label    # R_X86_64_64
        jmp     r11
# ...
        ...
# Label:
Label:
```

**Figure: Implicit Calculation of Target Address**

```asm
# if (!a)
# {
        test    eax, eax
        jz      2f
#    ...
1:      ...
# }
2:
```

```asm
# goto Label;
        jmp     Label
# ...
        ...
# Label:
Label:
```

For position-independent objects:

**Figure: Position-Independent Branching Code**

```asm
# if (!a)
# {
        test    eax, eax
        jnz     1f
        movabs  r11, OFFSET 2f@GOTOFF     # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        lea     r11, [r11 + r15]
        jmp     r11
1:      ...
#    ...
2:
# }
```

```asm
# goto Label;
        movabs  r11, OFFSET Label@GOTOFF  # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        lea     r11, [r11 + r15]
        jmp     r11
# ...
Label:
```

For absolute objects, the implementation of the `switch` statement is:

**Figure: Absolute Switch Code**

```asm
# switch (a)
# {
        cmp     eax, 0
        jl      .Ldefault
        cmp     eax, 2
        jg      .Ldefault
        movabs  r11, OFFSET .Ltable  # R_X86_64_64
        jmp     [r11 + rax*8]
        .section .lrodata,"aLM",@progbits,8
        .align  8
.Ltable:
        .quad   .Lcase0              # R_X86_64_64
        .quad   .Ldefault            # R_X86_64_64
        .quad   .Lcase2              # R_X86_64_64
        .previous
#    default:
.Ldefault:
#       ...
        ...
#    case 0:
.Lcase0:
#       ...
        ...
#    case 2:
.Lcase2:
#       ...
        ...
# }
```

When building position-independent objects, the `switch` statement
implementation changes to:

**Figure: Position-Independent Switch Code**

```asm
# switch (a)
# {
        cmp     eax, 0
        jl      .Ldefault
        cmp     eax, 2
        jg      .Ldefault
        movabs  r11, OFFSET .Ltable@GOTOFF  # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        lea     r11, [r11 + r15]
        mov     r11, [r11 + rax*8]
        lea     r11, [r11 + r15]
        jmp     r11
        .section .lrodata,"aLM",@progbits,8
        .align  8
.Ltable:
        .quad   .Lcase0@GOTOFF       # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        .quad   .Ldefault@GOTOFF     # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        .quad   .Lcase2@GOTOFF       # R_X86_64_GOTOFF64
        .previous
#    default:
.Ldefault:
#       ...
        ...
#    case 0:
.Lcase0:
#       ...
        ...
#    case 2:
.Lcase2:
#       ...
        ...
# }
```

> **Note:** The jump table is emitted in a different section. This way
> it occupies cache lines that hold no instruction bytes. This avoids
> thrashing in exclusive cache subsystems.

### Variable Argument Lists

Some otherwise portable C programs depend on the argument passing
scheme. They silently assume that all arguments are passed on the
stack, and that the arguments appear on the stack in increasing order.
Programs with these assumptions were never portable, but they have
worked on many implementations. They do not work on the AMD64
architecture, because some arguments are passed in registers. Portable
C programs must use the header file `<stdarg.h>` to handle variable
argument lists.

When the program calls a function that takes variable arguments, `al`
must be set to the total number of floating point parameters that are
passed to the function in vector registers.

> **Note:** This implies that the only legal values for `al` when
> calling a function with a variable argument list are 0 to 8
> (inclusive).

When a `__m256` or `__m512` value is passed as a variable argument, it
should always be passed on the stack. Only named `__m256` and `__m512`
arguments may be passed in registers, as specified in the section
"Function Calling Sequence".

**Figure: Parameter Passing Example with Variable-Argument List**

```c
int a, b;
long double ld;
double m, n;
__m256 u, y;
__m512 v, z;

extern void func (int a, double m, __m256 u, __m512 v, ...);

func (a, m, u, v, b, ld, y, z, n);
```

**Figure: Register Allocation Example for Variable-Argument List**

| General Purpose Registers | Floating Point Registers | Stack Frame Offset |
|---|---|---|
| `rdi`: `a` | `xmm0`: `m` | `0:` `ld` |
| `rsi`: `b` | `ymm1`: `u` | `32:` `y` |
| `rax`: 3 | `zmm2`: `v` | |
| | `xmm3`: `n` | |

In this call, three floating point values (`m`, `u`, `v`) travel in
vector registers. So `rax` gets the value 3 (only `al` matters). The
values `ld`, `y` and `z` go on the stack: `ld` because `long double`
always goes in memory, `y` and `z` because unnamed `__m256` and
`__m512` arguments must go on the stack. The last argument `n` still
fits in `xmm3`.

**Example:** Look at this simple call of `printf`:

```c
int i = 3;
double d = 1.5;
printf("%d %f", i, d);
```

`printf` is a variadic function: only the format string is a named
argument. The call becomes:

```asm
        lea     rdi, [rip + .Lfmt]   # 1st integer argument: address of "%d %f"
        mov     esi, 3               # 2nd integer argument: i
        movsd   xmm0, [rip + .Lval]  # 1st floating point argument: d
        mov     al, 1                # one vector register (xmm0) is used
        call    printf@PLT

        .section .rodata
.Lfmt:  .string "%d %f"
        .align  8
.Lval:  .double 1.5
        .previous
```

The pointer to the format string goes in `rdi`. The `int` value goes in
`esi`. The `double` goes in `xmm0`. Because exactly one vector register
carries an argument, `al` is set to 1. If the call had no floating
point arguments, the caller would set `al` to 0, for example with
`xor eax, eax`.

#### The Register Save Area

The prologue of a function that takes a variable argument list, and
that is known to call the macro `va_start`, must save the argument
registers in the *register save area*. Each argument register has a
fixed offset in the register save area, as the figure "Register Save
Area" shows.

Only registers that might pass arguments need to be saved. The other
registers are not accessed there and can be used for other purposes. If
a function is known to never accept arguments passed in registers, the
register save area may be left out completely.

> **Note:** The compiler can determine this fact in two ways: by
> looking at the types used with the `va_arg` macro, or by seeing that
> the named arguments already use up all the argument registers.

The prologue should use `al` to avoid saving XMM registers when it is
not needed. This is especially important for integer-only programs,
because it avoids the initialization of the XMM unit.

**Figure: Register Save Area**

| Register | Offset |
|---|---|
| `rdi` | 0 |
| `rsi` | 8 |
| `rdx` | 16 |
| `rcx` | 24 |
| `r8` | 32 |
| `r9` | 40 |
| `xmm0` | 48 |
| `xmm1` | 64 |
| ... | |
| `xmm7` | 160 |

Each general purpose register takes 8 bytes. Each XMM register takes 16
bytes. The whole area is 176 bytes (6 * 8 + 8 * 16).

**Example:** Here is a register save prologue for a variadic function.
It saves the six general purpose argument registers. Then it tests `al`
and skips the XMM saves when no vector register holds an argument:

```asm
func:
        push    rbp
        mov     rbp, rsp
        sub     rsp, 192             # space for the register save area
        mov     [rsp], rdi           # offset 0
        mov     [rsp + 8], rsi       # offset 8
        mov     [rsp + 16], rdx      # offset 16
        mov     [rsp + 24], rcx      # offset 24
        mov     [rsp + 32], r8       # offset 32
        mov     [rsp + 40], r9       # offset 40
        test    al, al               # did any argument use a vector register?
        je      .Lnosave             # if al == 0, skip the XMM saves
        movaps  [rsp + 48], xmm0     # offset 48
        movaps  [rsp + 64], xmm1     # offset 64
        movaps  [rsp + 80], xmm2     # offset 80
        movaps  [rsp + 96], xmm3     # offset 96
        movaps  [rsp + 112], xmm4    # offset 112
        movaps  [rsp + 128], xmm5    # offset 128
        movaps  [rsp + 144], xmm6    # offset 144
        movaps  [rsp + 160], xmm7    # offset 160
.Lnosave:
        ...
```

#### The va_list Type

The `va_list` type is an array that contains a single element of one
structure. The structure contains all the information that the
`va_arg` macro needs. The figure below gives the C definition of the
`va_list` type.

**Figure: va_list Type Declaration**

```c
typedef struct {
   unsigned int gp_offset;
   unsigned int fp_offset;
   void *overflow_arg_area;
   void *reg_save_area;
} va_list[1];
```

#### The va_start Macro

The `va_start` macro initializes the structure like this:

- `reg_save_area`: This element points to the start of the register
  save area.
- `overflow_arg_area`: This pointer is used to fetch arguments passed
  on the stack. It is initialized with the address of the first
  argument passed on the stack, if there is one. After that, it is
  always updated to point to the start of the next argument on the
  stack.
- `gp_offset`: This element holds the offset in bytes from
  `reg_save_area` to the place where the next available general purpose
  argument register is saved. When all argument registers are used up,
  it is set to the value 48 (6 * 8).
- `fp_offset`: This element holds the offset in bytes from
  `reg_save_area` to the place where the next available floating point
  argument register is saved. When all argument registers are used up,
  it is set to the value 176 (6 * 8 + 8 * 16).

#### The va_arg Macro

The algorithm for the generic `va_arg(l, type)` implementation is:

1. Determine whether `type` may be passed in registers. If not, go to
   step 7.
2. Compute `num_gp`. This is the number of general purpose registers
   needed to pass `type`. Also compute `num_fp`, the number of floating
   point registers needed.
3. Check whether the argument still fits into the saved registers. If
   `l->gp_offset > 48 - num_gp * 8`, or if
   `l->fp_offset > 176 - num_fp * 16`, go to step 7.
4. Fetch `type` from `l->reg_save_area`, at the offset `l->gp_offset`
   and/or `l->fp_offset`. This may require a copy to a temporary
   location. That happens when the parameter is passed in different
   register classes, or when it needs an alignment greater than 8 for
   general purpose registers or greater than 16 for XMM registers.
5. Set `l->gp_offset = l->gp_offset + num_gp * 8` and
   `l->fp_offset = l->fp_offset + num_fp * 16`.
6. Return the fetched `type`.
7. Align `l->overflow_arg_area` upwards to a 16 byte boundary if the
   alignment needed by `type` exceeds an 8 byte boundary.
8. Fetch `type` from `l->overflow_arg_area`.
9. Set `l->overflow_arg_area` to
   `l->overflow_arg_area + sizeof(type)`.
10. Align `l->overflow_arg_area` upwards to an 8 byte boundary.
11. Return the fetched `type`.

The `va_arg` macro is usually a compiler builtin. The compiler expands
it in a simplified form for each particular type. The figure below is a
sample implementation of the `va_arg` macro for `int`. In this code,
a name like `l->gp_offset` stands for the memory operand that accesses
this field of the `va_list` structure.

**Figure: Sample Implementation of va_arg(l, int)**

```asm
        mov     eax, l->gp_offset
        cmp     eax, 48                   # is a register available?
        jae     stack                     # if not, use the stack
        lea     edx, [rax + 8]            # next available register
        add     rax, l->reg_save_area     # address of saved register
        mov     l->gp_offset, edx         # update gp_offset
        jmp     fetch
stack:  mov     rax, l->overflow_arg_area # address of stack slot
        lea     rdx, [rax + 8]            # next available stack slot
        mov     l->overflow_arg_area, rdx # update
fetch:  mov     eax, [rax]                # load argument
```

## DWARF Definition

This section defines the DWARF debugging format for the x86-64 processor
family. DWARF is short for "Debug With Arbitrary Record Format". The x86-64
ABI does not choose one required debug format. But every system that does
implement DWARF on x86-64 must use the definitions from this section.

> **Note:** The structure of this section is similar to the PowerPC psABI.

DWARF is a specification made for symbolic, source-level debugging. This
means a debugger can show your program in terms of source lines, variable
names, and types. The format does not favor the design of any one compiler
or debugger. For more information on DWARF, see the *DWARF Debugging Format
Standard*, available at: http://www.dwarfstd.org/.

### DWARF Release Number

The DWARF definition needs some machine-specific definitions. The register
number mapping must be specified for the x86-64 registers. Also, starting
with version 3, the DWARF specification requires processor-specific address
class codes to be defined.

### DWARF Register Number Mapping

DWARF does not use register names like `rax` or `rbp`. It uses small
numbers instead. Every tool that reads or writes DWARF data for x86-64 must
use the same numbers. The table below shows the full mapping for the x86-64
processor family.

> **Note:** The table gives a register number to the "Return Address" (RA),
> even though the return address is stored in memory at `[rsp]` and not in
> a physical register.

> **Note:** This document does not define mappings for privileged
> registers. A *privileged register* is a register that only the operating
> system kernel may use.

**Table: DWARF Register Number Mapping**

| Register Name | Number | Abbreviation |
|---|---|---|
| General Purpose Register RAX | 0 | `rax` |
| General Purpose Register RDX | 1 | `rdx` |
| General Purpose Register RCX | 2 | `rcx` |
| General Purpose Register RBX | 3 | `rbx` |
| General Purpose Register RSI | 4 | `rsi` |
| General Purpose Register RDI | 5 | `rdi` |
| Frame Pointer Register RBP | 6 | `rbp` |
| Stack Pointer Register RSP | 7 | `rsp` |
| Extended Integer Registers 8-15 | 8-15 | `r8`-`r15` |
| Return Address RA | 16 | |
| Vector Registers 0-7 | 17-24 | `xmm0`-`xmm7` |
| Extended Vector Registers 8-15 | 25-32 | `xmm8`-`xmm15` |
| Floating Point Registers 0-7 | 33-40 | `st0`-`st7` |
| MMX Registers 0-7 | 41-48 | `mm0`-`mm7` |
| Flag Register | 49 | `rFLAGS` |
| Segment Register ES | 50 | `es` |
| Segment Register CS | 51 | `cs` |
| Segment Register SS | 52 | `ss` |
| Segment Register DS | 53 | `ds` |
| Segment Register FS | 54 | `fs` |
| Segment Register GS | 55 | `gs` |
| Reserved | 56-57 | |
| FS Base address | 58 | `fs.base` |
| GS Base address | 59 | `gs.base` |
| Reserved | 60-61 | |
| Task Register | 62 | `tr` |
| LDT Register | 63 | `ldtr` |
| 128-bit Media Control and Status | 64 | `mxcsr` |
| x87 Control Word | 65 | `fcw` |
| x87 Status Word | 66 | `fsw` |
| Upper Vector Registers 16-31 | 67-82 | `xmm16`-`xmm31` |
| Reserved | 83-117 | |
| Vector Mask Registers 0-7 | 118-125 | `k0`-`k7` |
| Reserved | 126-129 | |
| APX Integer Registers 16-31 | 130-145 | `r16`-`r31` |
| Tile Registers 0-7 | 146-153 | `tmm0`-`tmm7` |
| Tile Control Register | 154 | `tilecfg` |

**Example:** How a debugger uses the number for `rbp`. Look at this small C
function, built with a frame pointer:

```c
long twice(long x)
{
    return 2 * x;
}
```

The compiler produces this assembly with CFI directives. CFI is short for
"Call Frame Information". These directives create the DWARF unwind data:

```asm
twice:
        push  rbp
        .cfi_def_cfa_offset 16
        .cfi_offset 6, -16        # register 6 is rbp in DWARF
        mov   rbp, rsp
        .cfi_def_cfa_register 6   # from now on, use rbp for the CFA
        lea   rax, [rdi + rdi]
        pop   rbp
        .cfi_def_cfa 7, 8         # back to: CFA = rsp + 8
        ret
```

The directive `.cfi_offset 6, -16` does not store the name "rbp". It
stores the number 6. When a debugger later reads this rule, it looks up
number 6 in the table above. The table says: number 6 is `rbp`. So the
debugger knows that the saved value of `rbp` lies 16 bytes below the CFA.
In the same way, number 7 in `.cfi_def_cfa 7, 8` means `rsp`.

## Stack Unwind Algorithm

*Stack unwinding* means walking back through the chain of function calls.
The unwinder starts in the current function. It then computes the state of
the caller: where the caller's code continues, and what values the caller's
registers had. It repeats this, frame by frame, until it reaches the frame
it wants.

Stack frames on x86-64 are not self descriptive. This means you cannot
look at the stack alone and know where each frame starts and ends. So when
stack unwinding is needed (for example for exception handling), the
compiler must generate extra unwind information.

This information is stored in an allocatable section named `.eh_frame`.
"Allocatable" means the section is loaded into memory when the program
runs. The format of `.eh_frame` is identical to the `.debug_frame` section
defined by the DWARF debug information standard (see the *DWARF Debugging
Information Format*), with the extensions listed later in this section.

The unwind information uses two kinds of records:

- A *CIE* (Common Information Entry) holds data that many functions share.
- An *FDE* (Frame Description Entry) holds the data for one function. Each
  FDE points to its CIE.

The central idea is the *CFA*, the Canonical Frame Address. The CFA is a
fixed reference address for one stack frame. On x86-64, the CFA is
normally the value that `rsp` had in the caller, just before the `call`
instruction. Because `call` pushes an 8-byte return address, the CFA at
function entry equals `rsp + 8`. All unwind rules are written relative to
the CFA. For example: "the return address is stored at CFA - 8", or "the
saved `rbp` is stored at CFA - 16".

The unwinder works step by step, one frame at a time:

1. Take the current instruction address (`rip`).
2. Search `.eh_frame` for the FDE that covers this address.
3. Read the CIE and FDE instructions up to the current address. This gives
   a rule for the CFA (for example "CFA = rsp + 16") and a rule for each
   saved register (for example "register 6 saved at CFA - 16").
4. Compute the CFA from the current register values.
5. Restore the saved registers by reading memory at the rule addresses.
6. Load the return address (DWARF register 16, stored at CFA - 8). This is
   the new `rip`.
7. Set the new `rsp` to the CFA. Now the unwinder stands in the caller's
   frame, and the loop starts again at step 1.

**Example:** Unwinding one frame with real numbers. Suppose the unwinder
stops inside a function. The current registers are:

```
rsp = 0x7fffffffe000
rip = 0x401234  (somewhere inside the function body)
```

The FDE for address `0x401234` gives two rules:

```
CFA = rsp + 16
return address (RA) at CFA - 8
```

The unwinder now does the arithmetic:

```
CFA           = 0x7fffffffe000 + 16 = 0x7fffffffe010
address of RA = 0x7fffffffe010 - 8  = 0x7fffffffe008
new rip       = the 8 bytes stored at 0x7fffffffe008
new rsp       = CFA                 = 0x7fffffffe010
```

The unwinder reads the return address from memory at `0x7fffffffe008`.
That value becomes the new `rip`. The new `rsp` is the CFA itself,
`0x7fffffffe010`. The unwinder is now in the caller's frame and can repeat
the same steps there.

The `.eh_frame` format extends the DWARF `.debug_frame` format in the
following ways:

**Position independence.** Position independent code should not need
relocations at load time. A *relocation* is a fix-up of an address when
the program is loaded. To avoid such fix-ups, the FDE stores its CIE
offset pointer relative to the start of the CIE table entry, not as an
absolute address. Frames that use this extension of the DWARF standard
must set the CIE identifier tag to 1.

**Outgoing arguments area delta.** A function can build the arguments for
a call with `push` instructions. This creates a temporary outgoing
arguments area at the end of the stack. The operation `GNU_ARGS_SIZE`
(`0x2e`) records the size of this area. The operation takes a single
`uleb128` argument that gives the current size. A `uleb128` is an
unsigned integer in a compact, variable-length encoding. The unwinder
uses this size to adjust the stack frame when it jumps into the exception
handler of the function after unwinding the stack frame. Also, the CIE
Augmentation must contain an exact specification of the pointer encoding
used. It is recommended to use a PC relative encoding whenever possible,
and to adjust the size according to the code model used.

**CIE Augmentations.** The CIE header stores an augmentation string. This
string describes the format of the augmentation field. The string may
contain the following characters:

- `z` - A `uleb128` value is present. It gives the size of the
  augmentation section. This lets a reader skip the whole augmentation
  easily.

- `L` - An LSDA pointer is present in the FDE augmentation, and this
  character gives its encoding. LSDA is short for "Language Specific Data
  Area"; exception handling uses it. The data field is a single byte that
  specifies how pointers are encoded. The byte is a mask of the values in
  the table below.

- `R` - The FDE code pointers use a non-default pointer encoding. The
  encoding is one byte, in the same format as for the `L` character.

- `P` - A language *personality routine* is present in the CIE
  augmentation. The personality routine is the function that handles
  language-specific exception work during unwinding. The encoding is one
  byte, in the same format as for the `L` character. After this byte
  follows a pointer to the personality function, encoded in the specified
  way.

**Table: Pointer Encoding Specification Byte**

| Mask | Meaning |
|---|---|
| 0x1 | Values are stored as `uleb128` or `sleb128` type (according to flag 0x8) |
| 0x2 | Values are stored as 2 bytes wide integers (`udata2` or `sdata2`) |
| 0x3 | Values are stored as 4 bytes wide integers (`udata4` or `sdata4`) |
| 0x4 | Values are stored as 8 bytes wide integers (`udata8` or `sdata8`) |
| 0x8 | Values are signed |
| 0x10 | Values are PC relative |
| 0x20 | Values are text section relative |
| 0x30 | Values are data section relative |
| 0x40 | Values are relative to the start of function |

The default DWARF pointer encoding (direct 4-byte absolute pointers) is
represented by the value 0.

When the augmentation is present, the first character in the string must
always be `z`. This allows a reader to skip the whole augmentation
information easily.

**Example:** Reading an encoding byte. Many compilers emit the
augmentation string `zR` with the encoding byte `0x1b` for the FDE code
pointers. Split `0x1b` into the mask values from the table:

```
0x1b = 0x3 + 0x8 + 0x10
0x3  -> values are 4 bytes wide
0x8  -> values are signed  (so the type is sdata4)
0x10 -> values are PC relative
```

So each FDE code pointer is a signed 4-byte offset from the place where
the pointer itself is stored. This encoding needs no load time
relocations, which is good for position independent code.

To make work with the unwind tables simpler, the runtime library provides
a higher level API for the stack unwinding mechanism. For details, see the
section "Unwind Library Interface" in the chapter about libraries.

# 4. Object Files

An *object file* is the output of a compiler or assembler. It holds code,
data, and control information in the ELF format. ELF means "Executable and
Linking Format". The linker reads object files and joins them into a
program or a shared library. This chapter explains the x86-64 rules for
object files: the ELF header, the sections, the symbol table, and the
relocations.

## ELF Header

Every ELF file starts with the *ELF header*. The header describes the whole
file. It says what kind of file it is, for which machine it was built, and
where the other parts of the file are.

### Machine Information

#### Programming Model

As the introduction chapter explains, binaries that use the x86-64
instruction set can follow one of two programming models:

- **ILP32**: `int`, `long`, and pointers are all 32 bits.
- **LP64**: `int` is 32 bits, but `long` and pointers are 64 bits.

This specification describes binaries for both models.

#### File Class

The byte `e_ident[EI_CLASS]` in the ELF header gives the *file class*. The
file class says if the file uses 32-bit or 64-bit structures.

- For x86-64 ILP32 objects, `e_ident[EI_CLASS]` must be `ELFCLASS32`.
- For x86-64 LP64 objects, `e_ident[EI_CLASS]` must be `ELFCLASS64`.

#### Data Encoding

The byte `e_ident[EI_DATA]` gives the *data encoding*. It says in which
byte order the file stores numbers. x86-64 objects use `ELFDATA2LSB`. This
means little-endian: the least significant byte comes first.

> **Note:** The byte `e_ident[EI_OSABI]` selects the operating system ABI.
> For plain System V objects this byte is `ELFOSABI_NONE` (value 0), also
> called `ELFOSABI_SYSV`. Tools like `readelf` show it as
> "UNIX - System V".

#### Processor Identification

The `e_machine` member of the ELF header identifies the processor. For
x86-64 objects it must have the value `EM_X86_64`.

> **Note:** The numeric value of `EM_X86_64` is 62.

**Example:** Here is the ELF header of a tiny LP64 object file, as printed
by `readelf -h tiny.o`. The explanation for each line follows.

```
ELF Header:
  Magic:   7f 45 4c 46 02 01 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00
  Class:                             ELF64
  Data:                              2's complement, little endian
  Version:                           1 (current)
  OS/ABI:                            UNIX - System V
  ABI Version:                       0
  Type:                              REL (Relocatable file)
  Machine:                           Advanced Micro Devices X86-64
  Version:                           0x1
  Entry point address:               0x0
  Start of program headers:          0 (bytes into file)
  Start of section headers:          544 (bytes into file)
  Flags:                             0x0
  Size of this header:               64 (bytes)
  Size of program headers:           0 (bytes)
  Number of program headers:         0
  Size of section headers:           64 (bytes)
  Number of section headers:         12
  Section header string table index: 11
```

Line by line:

- **Magic**: the first 4 bytes are `7f 45 4c 46`. That is `0x7f` plus the
  letters "ELF". Every ELF file starts with these bytes. The 5th byte is
  `e_ident[EI_CLASS]`: `02` means `ELFCLASS64`, so this is an LP64 object.
  The 6th byte is `e_ident[EI_DATA]`: `01` means `ELFDATA2LSB`
  (little-endian). The 7th byte is the ELF version (1). The 8th byte is
  `e_ident[EI_OSABI]`: `00` means `ELFOSABI_NONE` (System V).
- **Type**: `REL` means a relocatable object file, not a finished program.
- **Machine**: this is `e_machine`. The value is `EM_X86_64` (62), which
  `readelf` prints as "Advanced Micro Devices X86-64".
- **Entry point address**: 0, because an object file has no start address.
- **Number of program headers**: 0, because a relocatable file needs no
  program header table. Only executables and shared objects need one.
- The last lines describe the section header table: where it starts, how
  big each entry is, how many entries exist, and which entry holds the
  section name strings.

### Number of Program Headers

A *program header* describes a segment of the file that the system loads
into memory. The `e_phnum` member of the ELF header holds the number of
entries in the program header table. The product of `e_phentsize` and
`e_phnum` gives the size of the table in bytes. If a file has no program
header table, `e_phnum` holds the value zero.

A special rule exists for very large files. If the number of program
headers is greater than or equal to `PN_XNUM` (0xffff), then `e_phnum`
holds the value `PN_XNUM` (0xffff). The real number of program header
table entries is then stored in the `sh_info` field of the section header
at index 0. In all other cases, the `sh_info` member of that first section
header entry holds the value zero.

**Example:** Imagine a core dump file with 70000 memory segments. The
number 70000 (0x11170) does not fit the rule "less than 0xffff". So the
producer of the file writes 0xffff (`PN_XNUM`) into `e_phnum`. It writes
the real count, 70000, into `sh_info` of section header 0. A reader first
checks `e_phnum`. It sees 0xffff, so it knows it must read `sh_info` of
section header 0 to get the true count. For a normal program with, say, 13
program headers, `e_phnum` is simply 13, and `sh_info` of section header 0
is 0.

## Sections

A *section* is a named block inside an object file. Examples are `.text`
for code and `.data` for writable data. Each section has a header with a
type, flags (attributes), a size, and other fields.

### Section Attribute Flags

Object files can use different *code models*. A code model limits how far
apart code and data can be in memory (see the chapter about code models
for details). To link object files with different code models, the linker
must know which sections may grow larger than 2GB and which may not. For
this, the ABI defines a processor-specific flag for the `sh_flags` field
of a section header.

**Table: x86-64 specific section header flag, sh_flags**

| Name | Value |
|---|---|
| `SHF_X86_64_LARGE` | `0x10000000` |

- **`SHF_X86_64_LARGE`**: If a section does *not* set this flag, it may
  not hold more than 2GB. Objects that use smaller code models can freely
  refer to such a section. If a section sets this flag, only objects that
  use larger code models can refer to it. For example, a medium code model
  object can refer to data in a section with this flag, and also to data
  in a section without it. A small code model object can refer only to
  code in a section that does not set this flag.

In simple words: the flag marks "large" sections. Small code model code
cannot reach them, so only large-model-aware code may use them.

**Example:** A program stores a huge lookup table of 3GB:

```c
static char big_table[3ULL * 1024 * 1024 * 1024];
```

3GB is more than 2GB, so this array cannot live in the normal `.bss`
section. A compiler using the medium code model places it in the `.lbss`
section instead. The `.lbss` section sets the `SHF_X86_64_LARGE` flag. A
small code model object linked into the same program never touches
`.lbss`, so the link still works. The small object keeps using the normal
`.bss`, which stays below 2GB.

### Section Types

The `sh_type` field of a section header gives the section type. The ABI
defines one processor-specific type.

**Table: Section header types**

| sh_type name | Value |
|---|---|
| `SHT_X86_64_UNWIND` | `0x70000001` |

- **`SHT_X86_64_UNWIND`**: This section contains unwind function table
  entries for stack unwinding. *Stack unwinding* means walking back
  through the active function calls, for example when a C++ exception is
  thrown. The contents are described in the section "EH_FRAME Sections"
  below.

### Special Sections

Some sections have fixed names and fixed meanings. The following table
lists the x86-64 special sections.

**Table: Special sections**

| Name | Type | Attributes |
|---|---|---|
| `.got` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_WRITE` |
| `.plt` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_EXECINSTR` |
| `.eh_frame` | `SHT_X86_64_UNWIND` | `SHF_ALLOC` |

- **`.got`**: This section holds the *global offset table* (GOT). The GOT
  is a table of addresses. Code reads addresses from it instead of using
  hard-coded addresses.
- **`.plt`**: This section holds the *procedure linkage table* (PLT). The
  PLT is a table of small code stubs used to call functions in shared
  libraries.
- **`.eh_frame`**: This section holds the unwind function table. The
  contents are described in the section "EH_FRAME Sections" below.

A system that supports the large code model uses the additional sections
in the next table.

**Table: Additional special sections for the medium and large code models**

| Name | Type | Attributes |
|---|---|---|
| `.lbss` | `SHT_NOBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_WRITE+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |
| `.ldata` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_WRITE+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |
| `.ldata1` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_WRITE+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |
| `.lgot` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_WRITE+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |
| `.lplt` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_EXECINSTR+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |
| `.lrodata` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |
| `.lrodata1` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |
| `.ltext` | `SHT_PROGBITS` | `SHF_ALLOC+SHF_EXECINSTR+SHF_X86_64_LARGE` |

These are the "large" twins of the normal sections. For example, `.ldata`
is the large version of `.data`, and `.ltext` is the large version of
`.text`.

To make static linking of objects with different code models possible,
the ABI suggests this section order:

- **`.plt` `.init` `.fini` `.text` `.got` `.rodata` `.rodata1` `.data`
  `.data1` `.bss`**: These sections together can have a combined size of
  up to 2GB.
- **`.lplt` `.ltext` `.lgot` `.lrodata` `.lrodata1` `.ldata` `.ldata1`
  `.lbss`**: These sections, plus all the sections above, can have a
  combined size of up to 16EB (exabytes).

In simple words: all the "small" sections come first and stay under 2GB.
The "large" sections come after them and can use the full 64-bit address
space.

### EH_FRAME Sections

The *call frame information* is the data needed to unwind the stack. It
is stored in one or more ELF sections of type `SHT_X86_64_UNWIND`. In the
simplest case there is one such section per object file, and its name is
`.eh_frame`.

An `.eh_frame` section consists of one or more subsections. Each
subsection contains one CIE (Common Information Entry) followed by a
varying number of FDEs (Frame Descriptor Entry). An FDE corresponds to an
explicit or compiler-generated function in a compilation unit. All FDEs
can access the CIE that begins their subsection for shared data. If the
code of a function is not one contiguous block, there is a separate FDE
for each contiguous piece.

If an object file contains C++ template instantiations, there shall be a
separate CIE immediately before each FDE that corresponds to an
instantiation.

With the preferred encoding specified below, the `.eh_frame` section can
be fully resolved at link time. So it can become part of the text
segment, which is read-only.

The term `EH_PE` encoding below refers to the pointer encoding specified
in the enhanced LSB, Chapter 7, for `Eh_Frame_Hdr`. The pointer encoding
tables appear at the end of this section.

Two number formats appear in the tables below:

- *uleb128* is an unsigned number stored in a variable number of bytes.
  Small numbers need only one byte.
- *sleb128* is the signed version of the same format.

**Table: Common Information Entry (CIE)**

| Field | Length (byte) | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Length | 4 | Length of the CIE (not including this 4-byte field) |
| CIE id | 4 | Value 0 for `.eh_frame` (used to distinguish CIEs and FDEs when scanning the section) |
| Version | 1 | Value one (1) |
| CIE Augmentation String | string | Null-terminated string. Legal values are "" (empty), or 'z' optionally followed by single occurrences of 'P', 'L', or 'R' in any order. The characters in the string dictate the content of the Augmentation Section field below. Each character has one or two associated operands in that section (see the table "CIE augmentation section content"). The operand order depends on the position in the string ('z' must be first) |
| Code Align Factor | uleb128 | To be multiplied with the "Advance Location" instructions in the Call Frame Instructions |
| Data Align Factor | sleb128 | To be multiplied with all offsets in the Call Frame Instructions |
| Ret Address Reg | 1/uleb128 | A "virtual" register representation of the return address. In DWARF V2 this is one byte; otherwise it is a uleb128. It is one byte in gcc 3.3.x |
| Optional CIE Augmentation Section | varying | Present if the Augmentation String field above is not empty. See the table "CIE augmentation section content" |
| Optional Call Frame Instructions | varying | |

**Table: CIE augmentation section content**

| Char | Operands | Length (byte) | Description |
|---|---|---|---|
| z | size | uleb128 | Length of the remainder of the Augmentation Section |
| P | personality_enc | 1 | Encoding specifier - preferred value is a pc-relative, signed 4-byte value |
| | personality routine | (encoded) | Encoded pointer to the personality routine (actually to the PLT entry for the personality routine) |
| R | code_enc | 1 | Non-default encoding for the code pointers (the FDE members `initial_location` and `address_range`, and the operand for `DW_CFA_set_loc`) - preferred value is pc-relative, signed 4-byte |
| L | lsda_enc | 1 | FDE augmentation bodies may contain LSDA pointers. If so, they are encoded as specified here - preferred value is pc-relative, signed 4-byte, possibly indirect through a GOT entry |

The *personality routine* is a function that a language runtime uses to
handle an exception for one frame. The *LSDA* (Language Specific Data
Area) is extra data for that routine, for example the list of C++ catch
handlers.

**Table: Frame Descriptor Entry (FDE)**

| Field | Length (byte) | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Length | 4 | Length of the FDE (not including this 4-byte field) |
| CIE pointer | 4 | Distance from this field to the nearest preceding CIE (the value is subtracted from the current address). This value can never be zero. So it can be used to distinguish CIEs and FDEs when scanning the `.eh_frame` section |
| Initial Location | var | Reference to the function code that corresponds to this FDE. If 'R' is missing from the CIE Augmentation String, the field is an 8-byte absolute pointer. Otherwise, the corresponding `EH_PE` encoding from the CIE Augmentation Section is used to interpret the reference |
| Address Range | var | Size of the function code that corresponds to this FDE. If 'R' is missing from the CIE Augmentation String, the field is an 8-byte unsigned number. Otherwise, the size is determined by the corresponding `EH_PE` encoding from the CIE Augmentation Section (the value is always absolute) |
| Optional FDE Augmentation Section | var | Present if the CIE Augmentation String is non-empty. See the table "FDE augmentation section content" |
| Optional Call Frame Instructions | var | |

**Table: FDE augmentation section content**

| Char | Operands | Length (byte) | Description |
|---|---|---|---|
| z | length | uleb128 | Length of the remainder of the Augmentation Section |
| L | LSDA | var | LSDA pointer, encoded in the format given by the corresponding operand in the CIE's augmentation body (only present if length > 0) |

The optional call frame instruction area has no length field of its own.
You must compute its existence and size from the overall entry size and
from the offset you reached while scanning the earlier fields of the CIE
or FDE.

The overall size of an `.eh_frame` section is given in the ELF section
header. The only way to find the number of entries is to scan the section
to the end and count the entries as you meet them.

The following two tables give the `EH_PE` pointer encodings, as defined
by the LSB. One encoding byte combines two parts. The lower 4 bits give
the value format. The upper 4 bits say how to apply the value.

**Table: Pointer encoding: value format (lower 4 bits)**

| Name | Value | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `DW_EH_PE_absptr` | `0x00` | Pointer with the natural pointer size |
| `DW_EH_PE_uleb128` | `0x01` | Unsigned LEB128 value |
| `DW_EH_PE_udata2` | `0x02` | Unsigned 2-byte value |
| `DW_EH_PE_udata4` | `0x03` | Unsigned 4-byte value |
| `DW_EH_PE_udata8` | `0x04` | Unsigned 8-byte value |
| `DW_EH_PE_sleb128` | `0x09` | Signed LEB128 value |
| `DW_EH_PE_sdata2` | `0x0a` | Signed 2-byte value |
| `DW_EH_PE_sdata4` | `0x0b` | Signed 4-byte value |
| `DW_EH_PE_sdata8` | `0x0c` | Signed 8-byte value |

**Table: Pointer encoding: application (upper 4 bits)**

| Name | Value | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `DW_EH_PE_absptr` | `0x00` | The value is used as it is |
| `DW_EH_PE_pcrel` | `0x10` | The value is relative to the address of the encoded field itself |
| `DW_EH_PE_textrel` | `0x20` | The value is relative to the start of the text section |
| `DW_EH_PE_datarel` | `0x30` | The value is relative to the start of the data area (for `.eh_frame_hdr`: relative to the start of that section) |
| `DW_EH_PE_funcrel` | `0x40` | The value is relative to the start of the function |
| `DW_EH_PE_aligned` | `0x50` | The value is aligned to the pointer size |
| `DW_EH_PE_indirect` | `0x80` | Extra flag bit: the computed value is the address where the real value is stored |
| `DW_EH_PE_omit` | `0xff` | No value is present |

For example, the preferred encoding `0x1b` combines `DW_EH_PE_pcrel`
(`0x10`) with `DW_EH_PE_sdata4` (`0x0b`). It means: a signed 4-byte
value, relative to the place where the value is stored.

**Example:** Here is a complete, minimal `.eh_frame` section, 48 bytes
long, shown as a hexdump with one field per line. It holds one CIE and
one FDE. The FDE describes a small leaf function of 11 bytes.

```
Offset  Bytes         Meaning
------  -----------   -----------------------------------------------
CIE:
0x0000  14 00 00 00   Length = 0x14 = 20 bytes (after this field)
0x0004  00 00 00 00   CIE id = 0: this entry is a CIE
0x0008  01            Version = 1
0x0009  7a 52 00      Augmentation string "zR", null-terminated
0x000c  01            Code align factor = 1 (uleb128)
0x000d  78            Data align factor = -8 (sleb128)
0x000e  10            Return address register = 16 (the RA column)
0x000f  01            'z' operand: 1 more byte of augmentation data
0x0010  1b            'R' operand: code pointers use encoding 0x1b
                      (DW_EH_PE_pcrel + DW_EH_PE_sdata4)
0x0011  0c 07 08      DW_CFA_def_cfa: CFA = rsp + 8
0x0014  90 01         DW_CFA_offset: RA saved at CFA - 8
                      (offset 1 * data align factor -8 = -8)
0x0016  00 00         Padding (DW_CFA_nop)
FDE:
0x0018  14 00 00 00   Length = 0x14 = 20 bytes (after this field)
0x001c  1c 00 00 00   CIE pointer: 0x001c - 0x1c = 0x0000, the CIE.
                      Not zero, so this entry is an FDE
0x0020  d8 fe ff ff   Initial location: pc-relative signed 4-byte
                      value -0x128. Function start = 0x0020 - 0x128
                      (as a section offset; the linker fixes it)
0x0024  0b 00 00 00   Address range: the function is 11 bytes long
0x0028  00            'z' operand: 0 bytes of FDE augmentation data
0x0029  00 00 00 00   Padding (DW_CFA_nop)
0x002d  00 00 00      Padding (DW_CFA_nop)
```

How a consumer reads this: it starts at offset 0. The second field is 0,
so the first entry is a CIE. The augmentation string is "zR", so one
augmentation length byte and one encoding byte follow the return address
register field. The next entry starts at 0x0018. Its second field is
0x1c, not 0, so it is an FDE. Subtracting 0x1c from the field address
0x001c gives 0, the offset of the matching CIE. The CIE said "encoding
0x1b", so the FDE's initial location is a signed 4-byte pc-relative
value. The scan then continues at offset 0x0030, which is the end of the
section, so there are exactly two entries.

## Symbol Table

The *symbol table* of an object file lists the names the file defines and
the names it needs from other files. Each entry describes one symbol: its
name, its value, its size, its type, and its binding. The type and
binding share the `st_info` field: the low 4 bits hold the type
(`STT_FUNC`, `STT_OBJECT`, and so on), and the high 4 bits hold the
binding (`STB_LOCAL`, `STB_GLOBAL`, `STB_WEAK`). If the `st_other` field
is present, its low 2 bits hold the symbol visibility, for example
`STV_DEFAULT` or `STV_HIDDEN`.

The discussion of "Function Addresses" in the chapter about program
loading and dynamic linking defines some special values for symbol table
fields.

The `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbol type is optional. It is the same as `STT_FUNC`
except that the symbol always points to a function or piece of executable
code that takes no arguments and returns a function pointer. If a
relocation refers to an `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbol, the evaluation of that
relocation is delayed until load time. The value used in the relocation
is the function pointer returned by a call of the `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbol.

> **Note:** `STT_GNU_IFUNC` is specified in the document "Linux
> Extensions to gABI", available at
> https://gitlab.com/x86-psABIs/Linux-ABI

The purpose of the `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbol type is to let the run-time
select between multiple versions of the implementation of a specific
function. In general, the selection looks at the currently available
hardware and picks the most appropriate version.

**Example:** Imagine a C library that ships three versions of `memcpy`:
a plain version, an SSE2 version, and an AVX2 version. The library marks
the public symbol `memcpy` as `STT_GNU_IFUNC`. The symbol points to a
small resolver function:

```c
static void *memcpy_resolver(void) {
    if (cpu_has_avx2()) return memcpy_avx2;
    if (cpu_has_sse2()) return memcpy_sse2;
    return memcpy_plain;
}
```

When a program that calls `memcpy` is loaded, the dynamic linker sees a
relocation against the `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbol. It calls the resolver
once. The resolver checks the CPU and returns, for example, the address
of `memcpy_avx2`. The dynamic linker writes that address into the GOT.
From then on, every call to `memcpy` goes straight to the AVX2 version,
with no extra cost per call.

## Relocation

A *relocation* is an instruction to the linker or the dynamic linker. It
says: "patch this place in the file with this computed value". The
compiler emits relocations for every address it cannot know yet.

### Relocation Types

A relocation patches a field of a fixed width. The next figure shows the
allowed relocatable fields.

**Figure: Relocatable fields**

```
  7        0
 +----------+
 |  word8   |
 +----------+

 15                    0
 +---------------------+
 |       word16        |
 +---------------------+

 31                                            0
 +---------------------------------------------+
 |                   word32                    |
 +---------------------------------------------+

 63                                                                    0
 +---------------------------------------------------------------------+
 |                                word64                               |
 +---------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

- *word8*: an 8-bit field that occupies 1 byte.
- *word16*: a 16-bit field that occupies 2 bytes, with arbitrary byte
  alignment. These values use the same byte order as other word values in
  the x86-64 architecture.
- *word32*: a 32-bit field that occupies 4 bytes, with arbitrary byte
  alignment. These values use the same byte order as other word values in
  the x86-64 architecture.
- *word64*: a 64-bit field that occupies 8 bytes, with arbitrary byte
  alignment. These values use the same byte order as other word values in
  the x86-64 architecture.
- *wordclass*: this means *word64* for LP64 and *word32* for ILP32.

The calculations in the relocation table below use these symbols:

- **A**: the *addend* used to compute the value of the relocatable field.
  The addend is an extra constant stored with the relocation.
- **B**: the *base address* at which a shared object is loaded into
  memory during execution. Generally, a shared object is built with a 0
  base virtual address, but the execution address is different.
- **G**: the offset into the global offset table where the address of the
  relocation entry's symbol will sit during execution.
- **GOT**: the address of the global offset table.
- **L**: the place (section offset or address) of the procedure linkage
  table entry for a symbol.
- **P**: the place (section offset or address) of the storage unit being
  relocated (computed with `r_offset`).
- **S**: the value of the symbol whose index sits in the relocation
  entry.
- **Z**: the size of the symbol whose index sits in the relocation entry.

The x86-64 LP64 ABI uses only `Elf64_Rela` relocation entries, which have
explicit addends. The `r_addend` member serves as the relocation addend.

The x86-64 ILP32 ABI uses only `Elf32_Rela` relocation entries in
relocatable files. Executable files or shared objects may use either
`Elf32_Rela` or `Elf32_Rel` relocation entries.

**Table: Relocation types**

| Name | Value | Field | Calculation |
|---|---|---|---|
| `R_X86_64_NONE` | 0 | none | none |
| `R_X86_64_64` | 1 | *word64* | `S + A` |
| `R_X86_64_PC32` | 2 | *word32* | `S + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_GOT32` | 3 | *word32* | `G + A` |
| `R_X86_64_PLT32` | 4 | *word32* | `L + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_COPY` | 5 | none | none |
| `R_X86_64_GLOB_DAT` | 6 | *wordclass* | `S` |
| `R_X86_64_JUMP_SLOT` | 7 | *wordclass* | `S` |
| `R_X86_64_RELATIVE` | 8 | *wordclass* | `B + A` |
| `R_X86_64_GOTPCREL` | 9 | *word32* | `G + GOT + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_32` | 10 | *word32* | `S + A` |
| `R_X86_64_32S` | 11 | *word32* | `S + A` |
| `R_X86_64_16` | 12 | *word16* | `S + A` |
| `R_X86_64_PC16` | 13 | *word16* | `S + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_8` | 14 | *word8* | `S + A` |
| `R_X86_64_PC8` | 15 | *word8* | `S + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_DTPMOD64` | 16 | *word64* | |
| `R_X86_64_DTPOFF64` | 17 | *word64* | |
| `R_X86_64_TPOFF64` | 18 | *word64* | |
| `R_X86_64_TLSGD` | 19 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_TLSLD` | 20 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_DTPOFF32` | 21 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_GOTTPOFF` | 22 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_TPOFF32` | 23 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_PC64` * | 24 | *word64* | `S + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_GOTOFF64` * | 25 | *word64* | `S + A - GOT` |
| `R_X86_64_GOTPC32` | 26 | *word32* | `GOT + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_SIZE32` | 32 | *word32* | `Z + A` |
| `R_X86_64_SIZE64` * | 33 | *word64* | `Z + A` |
| `R_X86_64_GOTPC32_TLSDESC` | 34 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_TLSDESC_CALL` | 35 | none | |
| `R_X86_64_TLSDESC` | 36 | *word64* x 2 | |
| `R_X86_64_IRELATIVE` | 37 | *wordclass* | `indirect (B + A)` |
| `R_X86_64_RELATIVE64` ** | 38 | *word64* | `B + A` |
| Deprecated | 39 | | |
| Deprecated | 40 | | |
| `R_X86_64_GOTPCRELX` | 41 | *word32* | `G + GOT + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX` | 42 | *word32* | `G + GOT + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTPCRELX` | 43 | *word32* | `G + GOT + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTTPOFF` | 44 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTPC32_TLSDESC` | 45 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_5_GOTPCRELX` | 46 | *word32* | `G + GOT + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_5_GOTTPOFF` | 47 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_5_GOTPC32_TLSDESC` | 48 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_6_GOTPCRELX` | 49 | *word32* | `G + GOT + A - P` |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_6_GOTTPOFF` | 50 | *word32* | |
| `R_X86_64_CODE_6_GOTPC32_TLSDESC` | 51 | *word32* | |

`*` This relocation is used only for LP64.

`**` This relocation appears only in ILP32 executable files or shared
objects.

The special semantics for most of these relocation types are identical to
those used for the Intel386 ABI.

> **Note:** Even though the x86-64 architecture supports IP-relative
> addressing modes, a GOT is still required. The reason is that the
> static linker cannot know the offset from a particular instruction to a
> particular data item in a shared library.

> **Note:** The x86-64 architecture assumes that offsets into the GOT are
> 32-bit values, not 64-bit values. So a maximum of 2^32 / 8 = 2^29
> entries fit into the GOT. That should be more than enough for most
> programs. If it is not enough, the linker could create multiple GOTs.
> Because the offsets are 32-bit, loads of global data do not need to
> load the offset into a displacement register. The "base plus immediate
> displacement" addressing form can be used.

**Example:** A concrete walk-through of `R_X86_64_PC32`. The compiler
translates this C code:

```c
extern int x;
int get_x(void) { return x; }
```

into this instruction:

```asm
mov eax, [rip + x]      ; load the 4-byte value of x
```

The instruction is 6 bytes: `8b 05` followed by a 4-byte displacement.
The displacement field starts 2 bytes after the instruction start. The
assembler does not know where `x` will live, so it emits a relocation on
that field:

```
Offset in .text   Type            Symbol   Addend
0x2               R_X86_64_PC32   x        -4
```

Why is the addend -4? The formula `S + A - P` uses P, the address of the
4-byte field itself. But the CPU adds the displacement to `rip`, which at
that moment points to the *end* of the instruction. The end is 4 bytes
after the field. The addend -4 corrects exactly this difference.

Now the math with example addresses. Suppose the linker places the
instruction at 0x401000, so the field is at P = 0x401002. Suppose it
places `x` at S = 0x404028. The addend is A = -4. The linker computes:

```
value = S + A - P
      = 0x404028 + (-4) - 0x401002
      = 0x3022
```

It writes 0x3022 into the field. At run time, after fetching the
instruction, `rip` = 0x401006. The CPU computes 0x401006 + 0x3022 =
0x404028. That is exactly the address of `x`. The relocation is correct.

**Example:** One worked example for an absolute relocation and one for a
GOT relocation, with the same addresses as above (S = 0x404028 for `x`,
GOT at 0x403fe8, and the GOT slot of `x` at offset G = 0x18):

- `R_X86_64_64`, calculation `S + A`. The compiler emits
  `.quad x + 16` in `.data`. Here A = 16. The linker writes
  S + A = 0x404028 + 16 = 0x404038 into the 8-byte field. No P appears:
  the value does not depend on where the field itself is.
- `R_X86_64_GOTPCREL`, calculation `G + GOT + A - P`. The compiler emits
  `mov rax, [rip + x@GOTPCREL]` with A = -4 and the field at
  P = 0x401002. The linker computes 0x18 + 0x403fe8 - 4 - 0x401002 =
  0x2ffa. At run time, `rip` (0x401006) plus 0x2ffa gives 0x404000, the
  GOT slot that holds the address of `x`. The instruction loads that
  address into `rax`.

The `R_X86_64_GOTPCREL` relocation has different semantics from the
`R_X86_64_GOT32` relocation or the equivalent i386 `R_386_GOTPC`
relocation. The x86-64 architecture has an addressing mode relative to
the instruction pointer. So one single instruction can load an address
from the GOT. The calculation done by the `R_X86_64_GOTPCREL` relocation
gives the difference between two places: the location in the GOT where
the symbol's address is stored, and the location where the relocation is
applied.

For an occurrence of `name@GOTPCREL` in the following assembler
instructions:

```asm
call  [rip + name@GOTPCREL]
jmp   [rip + name@GOTPCREL]
mov   reg, [rip + name@GOTPCREL]
test  [rip + name@GOTPCREL], reg
binop reg, [rip + name@GOTPCREL]
```

where `binop` is one of the instructions `adc`, `add`, `and`, `cmp`,
`or`, `sbb`, `sub`, `xor`, the following `GOTPCRELX` relocations should
be generated (see also the section "Optimize GOTPCRELX Relocations" in
the chapter about linker optimization):

- `R_X86_64_GOTPCRELX`: the instruction starts 2 bytes before the
  relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX`: the instruction starts 3 bytes before the
  relocation offset, with the `REX` prefix.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTPCRELX`: the instruction starts 4 bytes before the
  relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_5_GOTPCRELX`: the instruction starts 5 bytes before the
  relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_6_GOTPCRELX`: the instruction starts 6 bytes before the
  relocation offset.

The `R_X86_64_32` and `R_X86_64_32S` relocations truncate the computed
value to 32 bits. The linker must verify the result. For `R_X86_64_32`,
the generated value must zero-extend to the original 64-bit value. For
`R_X86_64_32S`, it must sign-extend to the original 64-bit value. In
simple words: the address must really fit in 32 bits, either as an
unsigned number (`R_X86_64_32`) or as a signed number (`R_X86_64_32S`).
Otherwise the linker reports an error.

A program or object file that uses the relocations `R_X86_64_8`,
`R_X86_64_16`, `R_X86_64_PC16`, or `R_X86_64_PC8` does not conform to
this ABI. These relocations are only listed for documentation purposes.
The `R_X86_64_16` and `R_X86_64_8` relocations truncate the computed
value to 16 bits and 8 bits, respectively.

The relocations `R_X86_64_DTPMOD64`, `R_X86_64_DTPOFF64`,
`R_X86_64_TPOFF64`, `R_X86_64_TLSGD`, `R_X86_64_TLSLD`,
`R_X86_64_DTPOFF32`, `R_X86_64_GOTTPOFF`, and `R_X86_64_TPOFF32` are
listed for completeness. They are part of the Thread-Local Storage (TLS)
ABI extensions. They are documented in the document "ELF Handling for
Thread-Local Storage".

> **Note:** That document is currently available at
> http://www.akkadia.org/drepper/tls.pdf

The following `GOTTPOFF` relocations should be generated:

- `R_X86_64_GOTTPOFF`: the instruction starts 3 bytes before the
  relocation offset, with the `REX` byte.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTTPOFF`: the instruction starts 4 bytes before the
  relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_5_GOTTPOFF`: the instruction starts 5 bytes before the
  relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_6_GOTTPOFF`: the instruction starts 6 bytes before the
  relocation offset.

Linker optimization must take the different instruction encodings into
account.

The relocations `R_X86_64_GOTPC32_TLSDESC`, `R_X86_64_TLSDESC_CALL`, and
`R_X86_64_TLSDESC` are also used for Thread-Local Storage. But they are
not documented in that document as of this writing. A description can be
found in the document "Thread-Local Storage Descriptors for IA32 and
AMD64/EM64T".

> **Note:** That document is currently available at
> http://www.fsfla.org/~lxoliva/writeups/TLS/RFC-TLSDESC-x86.txt

The following `GOTPC32_TLSDESC` relocations should be generated:

- `R_X86_64_GOTPC32_TLSDESC`: the instruction starts 3 bytes before the
  relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTPC32_TLSDESC`: the instruction starts 4 bytes
  before the relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_5_GOTPC32_TLSDESC`: the instruction starts 5 bytes
  before the relocation offset.
- `R_X86_64_CODE_6_GOTPC32_TLSDESC`: the instruction starts 6 bytes
  before the relocation offset.

Linker optimization must take the different instruction encodings into
account.

To make this document self-contained, a description of the TLS
relocations follows.

*Thread-local storage* (TLS) gives each thread its own copy of certain
variables. The *thread pointer* points to the TLS area of the current
thread. The `fs` segment register implements the thread pointer. The
linear address of the thread pointer is stored at offset 0 relative to
the `fs` segment register. The following code loads the thread pointer
into the `rax` register:

```asm
mov rax, fs:[0]
```

`R_X86_64_DTPMOD64` resolves to the index of the dynamic thread vector
entry that points to the base address of the TLS block of the module that
defines the referenced symbol. The *dynamic thread vector* is a run-time
table with one entry per loaded module. `R_X86_64_DTPOFF64` and
`R_X86_64_DTPOFF32` compute the offset from the pointer in that entry to
the referenced symbol. The linker generates such relocations in adjacent
GOT entries, in response to `R_X86_64_TLSGD` and `R_X86_64_TLSLD`
relocations. If the referenced symbol binds locally, the linker can
compute the offset itself. Then the relocations `R_X86_64_64` and
`R_X86_64_32` may be used instead. Otherwise, such relocations always
come in pairs: the `R_X86_64_DTPOFF64` relocation applies to the word64
directly after the word64 with the matching `R_X86_64_DTPMOD64`
relocation.

`R_X86_64_TPOFF64` and `R_X86_64_TPOFF32` resolve to the offset from the
thread pointer to a thread-local variable. The first one is generated in
response to `R_X86_64_GOTTPOFF`. `R_X86_64_GOTTPOFF` itself resolves to
a PC-relative address of a GOT entry that contains such a 64-bit offset.
