`R_X86_64_TLSGD` and `R_X86_64_TLSLD` both resolve to PC-relative
offsets to a `DTPMOD` GOT entry. The difference between them is the
content of the next GOT entry. For `R_X86_64_TLSGD`, the following GOT
entry contains the offset of the referenced symbol inside its TLS block.
For `R_X86_64_TLSLD`, the following GOT entry contains the offset for
the base address of the TLS block. The idea is: adding this offset to
the result of `R_X86_64_DTPMOD32` for a symbol should give the same
value as the result of `R_X86_64_DTPMOD64` for the same symbol.

`R_X86_64_TLSDESC` resolves to a pair of word64 values, called a TLS
Descriptor. The first word64 is a pointer to a function. The second
word64 is an argument. The function receives a pointer to this pair of
entries in `rax`. Using the argument in the second entry, it must compute
the offset from the thread pointer to the symbol referenced in the
relocation. It must return this offset in `rax`. It must not modify any
registers other than the processor flags. `R_X86_64_GOTPC32_TLSDESC`
resolves to the PC-relative address of a TLS descriptor for the named
symbol. `R_X86_64_TLSDESC_CALL` must annotate the instruction that calls
the TLS Descriptor resolver function. This annotation enables relaxation
of that instruction by the linker.

`R_X86_64_IRELATIVE` is similar to `R_X86_64_RELATIVE`, with one extra
step. The address that a plain `R_X86_64_RELATIVE` relocation would
produce is treated as the address of a function that takes no arguments.
The dynamic linker calls that function. The value used in the relocation
is the program address that the function returns.

One use of the `R_X86_64_IRELATIVE` relocation is to avoid name lookup
for locally defined `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbols at load time. Support for
this relocation is optional. But if `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbols are used, it
is required.

### Large Models

The large code model needs a PLT and a GOT that can grow beyond 2GB. For
this, extra relocation types with full 64-bit fields are needed. The next
table lists them.

**Table: Large model relocation types**

| Name | Value | Field | Calculation |
|---|---|---|---|
| `R_X86_64_GOT64` | 27 | word64 | `G + A` |
| `R_X86_64_GOTPCREL64` | 28 | word64 | `G + GOT - P + A` |
| `R_X86_64_GOTPC64` | 29 | word64 | `GOT - P + A` |
| Deprecated | 30 | | |
| `R_X86_64_PLTOFF64` | 31 | word64 | `L - GOT + A` |

**Example:** In the large code model, the compiler cannot use a 32-bit
displacement to reach the GOT. So it first loads the full 64-bit GOT
address into a register, and then adds 64-bit offsets:

```asm
    lea    r11, [rip + next]     ; address of the label "next"
next:
    movabs r15, OFFSET _GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_
                                 ; R_X86_64_GOTPC64: GOT - P + A
    lea    r15, [r11 + r15]      ; r15 = address of the GOT
    movabs rax, OFFSET x@GOT     ; R_X86_64_GOT64: G + A
    mov    rax, [r15 + rax]      ; load the address of x from the GOT
```

The `movabs` with `_GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_` uses `R_X86_64_GOTPC64`. It
stores the 64-bit distance from the patched field to the GOT (the addend
corrects for the label position). Adding the run-time address in `r11`
turns that distance into the absolute GOT address. The second `movabs`
uses `R_X86_64_GOT64` to get the 64-bit offset of the GOT slot of `x`.
The sum `r15 + rax` is the address of the slot, even if the GOT is
larger than 2GB. For a function call, `R_X86_64_PLTOFF64` (`L - GOT +
A`) gives the 64-bit distance from the GOT to the PLT entry of the
function in the same way.

# 5. Program Loading and Dynamic Linking

This chapter explains two things. First, how the system loads a
program file into memory. Second, how the *dynamic linker* connects
the program with shared objects while the program runs.

Two terms appear many times in this chapter:

- The *link editor* (also called the static linker, for example
  `ld`) builds an executable file or a shared object file from
  object files. It runs at build time.
- The *dynamic linker* (for example `ld.so`) loads shared objects
  into memory and resolves symbols. It runs at program start and
  while the program runs.

## Program Loading

Program loading is the process of mapping file segments to virtual
memory segments. A *segment* is a block of the file that the system
maps into memory. A *virtual address* is an address inside the
private address space of a process.

The mapping must be efficient. So executable files and shared object
files must have segments whose file offsets and virtual addresses
are congruent modulo the page size. In simple words: divide the file
offset by the page size and keep the remainder. Divide the virtual
address by the page size and keep the remainder. Both remainders
must be equal. Then the system can map whole file pages directly
onto whole memory pages.

**Example:** The next two tables show an executable file and its
process image. In this example the page size is 4096 bytes
(0x1000). The text segment holds the code. The data segment holds
the variables with start values. The uninitialized data (`.bss`)
holds variables that start as zero.

**Figure: Example executable file**

| File offset | File contents | Virtual address |
|---|---|---|
| `0` | ELF header | |
| | Program header table | |
| | Other information | |
| `0x100` | Text segment (`0x2be00` bytes) | `0x400100` |
| `0x2bf00` | Data segment (`0x4e00` bytes) | `0x42cf00` |
| `0x30d00` | Other information | |

Check the congruence rule with these numbers. The text segment:
`0x100` modulo `0x1000` is `0x100`, and `0x400100` modulo `0x1000`
is also `0x100`. The data segment: `0x2bf00` modulo `0x1000` is
`0xf00`, and `0x42cf00` modulo `0x1000` is also `0xf00`. So the
rule holds for both segments.

**Figure: Example loaded process image segments**

| Virtual address range | Contents | Segment | Permissions |
|---|---|---|---|
| `0x400000` - `0x4000ff` | header padding | text | read, execute |
| `0x400100` - `0x42beff` | text segment | text | read, execute |
| `0x42bf00` - `0x42bfff` | data padding | text | read, execute |
| `0x42c000` - `0x42ceff` | text padding | data | read, write |
| `0x42cf00` - `0x431cff` | initialized data | data | read, write |
| `0x431d00` - `0x431fff` | uninitialized data (zero) | data | read, write |
| `0x432000` - `0x432dff` | uninitialized data (zero) | data | read, write |

To save space, one file page may hold the last page of the text
segment and the first page of the data segment. In the example
above, the file page at offset `0x2b000` holds the end of text and
the beginning of data. The system maps this region of the file
twice: at one virtual address for text (`0x42b000`), and at a
different virtual address for data (`0x42c000`). The last data page
may also contain file information that the running process does not
need.

Logically, the system enforces the memory permissions as if each
segment were complete and separate. The system adjusts the
segments' addresses so that each logical page in the address space
has a single set of permissions.

The end of the data segment needs special handling for
uninitialized data. The system defines uninitialized data to begin
with zero values. So if the file's last data page includes
information that is not in the logical memory page, the system must
set that extra area to zero. It must not keep the unknown contents
of the executable file there. "Impurities" in the other three page
areas (the header padding, the data padding at the end of the text
segment, and the text padding at the start of the data segment) are
not logically part of the process image. The standard does not say
if the system removes them or not.

One part of segment loading differs between executable files and
shared objects. Executable file segments usually contain absolute
code (see the section "Coding Examples"). Absolute code only works
at one fixed address. For the process to run correctly, the
segments must sit at the virtual addresses that the link editor
used to build the executable file. So the system uses the
`p_vaddr` values unchanged as virtual addresses.

Shared object segments, on the other hand, usually contain
position-independent code. Position-independent code works at any
load address. So the virtual address of a segment can change from
one process to another. The program still runs correctly. The
system chooses virtual addresses for each process, but it keeps the
relative positions of the segments. Position-independent code uses
relative addressing between segments. So the difference between
virtual addresses in memory must match the difference between
virtual addresses in the file.

**Example:** Imagine the shared object `libfoo.so` has a text
segment at file address `0x0` and a data segment at file address
`0x200000`. The difference is `0x200000`. Process A loads the
library at `0x7f11aa000000`. Then the data segment must sit at
`0x7f11aa200000`. Process B loads the same library at
`0x7f22bb000000`. Then the data segment must sit at
`0x7f22bb200000`. In both processes the difference stays
`0x200000`, so the relative addressing in the code still works.

### Program Header

The program header table describes the segments of the file. The
x86-64 ABI defines the following program header types:

**Table: Program Header Types**

| Name | Value |
|---|---|
| `PT_GNU_EH_FRAME` | `0x6474e550` |
| `PT_SUNW_EH_FRAME` | `0x6474e550` |
| `PT_SUNW_UNWIND` | `0x6464e550` |

- `PT_GNU_EH_FRAME`, `PT_SUNW_EH_FRAME` and `PT_SUNW_UNWIND`: The
  segment contains the stack unwind tables. See the section about
  the `.eh_frame` unwind sections in this document.

> **Note:** The values for these program headers sit in the
> `PT_LOOS` to `PT_HIOS` range (the range for operating system
> specific values). This matches the existing GNU implementation.
> New operating systems that want to agree on these program headers
> should also add them to their own OS specific range.

## Dynamic Linking

Dynamic linking means that the system finishes the linking process
at run time. The dynamic linker loads the shared objects that the
program needs. It also fills in the addresses of functions and
variables that live in other objects.

### Dynamic Section

Dynamic section entries give information to the dynamic linker.
Some of this information is processor-specific. This includes the
meaning of some entries in the dynamic structure.

The following optional x86-64 Dynamic Array Tags are defined:

**Table: Optional Dynamic Array Tags, d_tag**

| Name | Value | d_un |
|---|---|---|
| `DT_X86_64_PLT` | `DT_LOPROC + 0` | `d_ptr` |
| `DT_X86_64_PLTSZ` | `DT_LOPROC + 1` | `d_val` |
| `DT_X86_64_PLTENT` | `DT_LOPROC + 3` | `d_val` |

- `DT_X86_64_PLT`: The address of the procedure linkage table.
- `DT_X86_64_PLTSZ`: The total size, in bytes, of the procedure
  linkage table.
- `DT_X86_64_PLTENT`: The size, in bytes, of one procedure linkage
  table entry. This size is always a power of 2.

A file provides `DT_X86_64_PLT`, `DT_X86_64_PLTSZ` and
`DT_X86_64_PLTENT` only if all of the following points are true:

- The procedure linkage table entries transfer control to external
  functions with an indirect branch. The branch goes over the
  matching entry in the global offset table. The `r_offset` field
  of the `R_X86_64_JUMP_SLOT` relocation references that global
  offset table entry. The `r_addend` field of the
  `R_X86_64_JUMP_SLOT` relocation stores the offset of the indirect
  branch inside the matching procedure linkage table entry.
- All such procedure linkage table entries have the same layout.
  Each entry starts with an indirect branch over the global offset
  table entry. An `ENDBR64` instruction may come before the
  indirect branch. Replacing the indirect branch with a direct
  branch must not change the behavior of the program.
- The only entry point of a procedure linkage table entry is the
  first byte of the entry.
- All entries have the same size. Each entry is aligned to the
  entry size.

If these points hold, the dynamic linker may optimize the calls. It
uses `DT_X86_64_PLT`, `DT_X86_64_PLTSZ` and `DT_X86_64_PLTENT`
together with the `r_offset` and `r_addend` fields of the
`R_X86_64_JUMP_SLOT` relocation. The dynamic linker reads the
branch target address stored in the global offset table entry. Then
it may rewrite the indirect branch in the procedure linkage table
entry into a direct branch to that address.

**Example:** The generic ABI defines the `DT_NEEDED` dynamic entry.
Each `DT_NEEDED` entry names one shared object that the file needs.
Imagine a program `app` that calls `printf` from the C library and
`sqrt` from the math library. Its dynamic section contains entries
like this (output in the style of `readelf -d app`):

```
Dynamic section at offset 0x2d78 contains 27 entries:
  Tag                Type      Name/Value
  0x0000000000000001 (NEEDED)  Shared library: [libm.so.6]
  0x0000000000000001 (NEEDED)  Shared library: [libc.so.6]
  ...
```

At program start, the dynamic linker reads these entries. It loads
`libm.so.6` and `libc.so.6`, and also every shared object that
those libraries need themselves. The `ldd` tool shows the full
result:

```
$ ldd ./app
    libm.so.6 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libm.so.6
    libc.so.6 => /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6
    /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2
```

The last line is the program interpreter (see the section "Program
Interpreter"). It is the dynamic linker itself.

### Global Offset Table (GOT)

Position-independent code cannot, in general, contain absolute
virtual addresses. An *absolute address* is a fixed, final address
in memory. Global offset tables hold absolute addresses in private
data. So the addresses are available, and the program text stays
position-independent and shareable. A program references its global
offset table with position-independent addressing. It extracts
absolute values from the table. In this way the program redirects
position-independent references to absolute locations.

If a program needs the absolute address of a symbol, that symbol
gets a global offset table entry. The executable file and each
shared object have separate global offset tables. So the address of
one symbol may appear in several tables. The dynamic linker
processes all global offset table relocations before it gives
control to any code in the process image. This makes sure that the
absolute addresses are ready during execution.

The first entry of the table (entry number zero, GOT[0]) is
reserved. It holds the address of the dynamic structure. The symbol
`_DYNAMIC` references this structure. With this entry, a program
such as the dynamic linker can find its own dynamic structure
before it has processed its own relocation entries. This is very
important for the dynamic linker. The dynamic linker must
initialize itself without help from other programs.

On the x86-64 architecture, entries one and two (GOT[1] and
GOT[2]) are also reserved. The dynamic linker sets them when it
creates the memory image of the program:

- GOT[0] holds the address of the `_DYNAMIC` structure.
- GOT[1] holds a word of identifying information for the dynamic
  linker. It identifies this object (its link map). The first
  procedure linkage table entry pushes this word on the stack.
- GOT[2] holds the address of the dynamic linker's symbol
  resolution routine. The first procedure linkage table entry jumps
  to this address.

The section "Procedure Linkage Table" below explains how the
program uses GOT[1] and GOT[2].

The global offset table contains 64-bit addresses.

For the large code models, the GOT may be up to 16 EB (exabytes) in
size.

**Figure: Global Offset Table**

```c
extern Elf64_Addr _GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_ [];
```

The symbol `_GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_` may sit in the middle of the
`.got` section. This allows both negative and non-negative offsets
into the array of addresses.

**Example:** This example shows why the GOT is needed. A shared
library must work at any load address. Two processes can load the
same library at two different addresses. Both processes share the
same read-only text pages of the library in physical memory. So
the text pages must not contain any absolute address. But the
library still needs the real address of a global variable, for
example `counter`, which the executable defines. The solution: the
compiler reads the address from the GOT. The GOT lives in the
private data of each process, so each process can hold a different
value there.

```asm
    # int counter;  (defined in the executable)
    # read the value of counter in position-independent code:
    mov  rax, [rip + counter@GOTPCREL]  # rax = address of counter,
                                        # loaded from the GOT
    mov  eax, DWORD PTR [rax]           # eax = value of counter
```

The instruction uses only a `rip`-relative offset to the GOT slot.
That offset is the same at every load address. The dynamic linker
writes the absolute address of `counter` into the GOT slot at
program start. The text of the library never changes, so all
processes can share it.

### Function Addresses

A reference to the address of a function may come from the
executable file or from a shared object. These references might not
resolve to the same value in a naive implementation. The ABI adds
rules so that they do.

References from inside shared objects normally resolve to the
virtual address of the function itself. The dynamic linker does
this resolution. References from inside the executable file to a
function in a shared object work differently. The link editor
normally resolves them to the address of the procedure linkage
table entry for that function inside the executable file.

Comparisons of function addresses must work as expected. So the ABI
adds this rule: if an executable file references a function that a
shared object defines, the link editor places the address of the
procedure linkage table entry for that function into the symbol
table entry of the executable. The result is a symbol table entry
with section index `SHN_UNDEF`, but with type `STT_FUNC` and a
non-zero `st_value`. A reference to the address of that function
from inside a shared library will use this definition in the
executable. So both sides see the same address value.

Some relocations belong to procedure linkage table entries. These
relocations serve direct function calls, not references to function
addresses. These relocations do not use the special symbol value
described above. Otherwise the call would jump to the PLT entry,
and the PLT entry would jump to itself. A very tight endless loop
would be the result.

**Example:** The executable `app` compares function pointers:

```c
/* in the executable */
extern int f(int);       /* f is defined in libx.so */
int (*p1)(int) = f;      /* address taken in the executable */

/* in libx.so */
int f(int x) { return x + 1; }
int (*p2)(int) = f;      /* address taken in the shared object */
```

Without the rule, `p1` would point to the PLT entry of `f` in
`app`, and `p2` would point to the real code of `f` in `libx.so`.
Then `p1 == p2` would be false, and correct C programs would break.
With the rule, the symbol table of `app` publishes the PLT entry
address as the value of `f`. The dynamic linker resolves the
reference in `libx.so` to that same value. So `p1 == p2` is true.
A direct call `f(5)` inside `libx.so` still uses the relocation
for direct calls, so it does not go through this special value.

### Procedure Linkage Table

The global offset table redirects position-independent address
calculations to absolute locations. In a similar way, the
*procedure linkage table* (PLT) redirects position-independent
function calls to absolute locations. The link editor cannot
resolve execution transfers (such as function calls) from one
executable or shared object to another. So the link editor makes
the program transfer control to entries in the procedure linkage
table.

On the x86-64 architecture, procedure linkage tables live in shared
text, but they use addresses in the private global offset table.
The dynamic linker determines the absolute addresses of the call
destinations. It writes these addresses into the memory image of
the global offset table. So the dynamic linker can redirect the
entries without changing the program's text. The text stays
position-independent and shareable.

Executable files and shared object files have separate procedure
linkage tables. Unlike the Intel386 ABI (the 32-bit x86 ABI), this
ABI uses the same procedure linkage table layout for both programs
and shared objects. The next figure shows it.

**Figure: Procedure Linkage Table (small and medium models)**

```asm
  .PLT0: push   QWORD PTR [rip + GOT + 8]     # GOT[1]
         jmp    [rip + GOT + 16]              # GOT[2]
         nop    DWORD PTR [rax]               # padding
  .PLT1: jmp    [rip + name1@GOTPCREL]        # 16 bytes from .PLT0
         push   index1
         jmp    .PLT0
  .PLT2: jmp    [rip + name2@GOTPCREL]        # 16 bytes from .PLT1
         push   index2
         jmp    .PLT0
  .PLT3: ...
```

The dynamic linker and the program "cooperate" to resolve symbolic
references through the procedure linkage table and the global
offset table. They follow these steps:

1. When the dynamic linker first creates the memory image of the
   program, it sets the second and the third entries in the global
   offset table (GOT[1] and GOT[2]) to special values. The steps
   below explain these values.
2. Each shared object file in the process image has its own
   procedure linkage table. Control transfers to a procedure
   linkage table entry only from within the same object file.
3. For illustration, assume the program calls `name1`. The call
   transfers control to the label `.PLT1`.
4. The first instruction jumps to the address stored in the global
   offset table entry for `name1`. Initially, this global offset
   table entry does not hold the real address of `name1`. It holds
   the address of the `push` instruction that follows the `jmp` in
   the PLT entry. So the jump lands on the next instruction.
5. Now the program pushes a relocation index (*index*) on the
   stack. The relocation index is a 32-bit, non-negative index into
   the relocation table. The `DT_JMPREL` dynamic section entry
   gives the address of that relocation table. The selected
   relocation entry has type `R_X86_64_JUMP_SLOT`. Its offset field
   names the global offset table entry that the earlier `jmp`
   instruction used. The relocation entry also contains a symbol
   table index. That index points to the right symbol, `name1` in
   this example.
6. After it pushes the relocation index, the program jumps to
   `.PLT0`, the first entry in the procedure linkage table. The
   `push` instruction there places the value of the second global
   offset table entry (GOT + 8) on the stack. This gives the
   dynamic linker one word of identifying information. The program
   then jumps to the address in the third global offset table entry
   (GOT + 16). This jump transfers control to the dynamic linker.
7. The dynamic linker receives control. It unwinds the stack and
   looks at the selected relocation entry. It finds the value of
   the symbol. It stores the "real" address of `name1` in the
   global offset table entry for `name1`. Then it transfers control
   to the wanted destination.
8. All later executions of this procedure linkage table entry
   transfer directly to `name1`. The dynamic linker does not run a
   second time for this symbol. The `jmp` instruction at `.PLT1`
   now jumps to `name1` itself. It no longer "falls through" to the
   `push` instruction.

This delayed resolution is called *lazy binding*. The program pays
the cost of symbol lookup only for functions that it really calls.

**Example:** Here is a full walk-through of the first call to
`puts` with lazy binding. The program contains:

```asm
main:
    lea   rdi, [rip + msg]     # rdi = address of the string
    call  puts@PLT             # call through the PLT
    ...
```

Assume `puts` uses the second PLT slot and the relocation index 1:

```asm
.PLT2: jmp   [rip + puts@GOTPCREL]   # jump via the GOT entry
       push  1                       # relocation index for puts
       jmp   .PLT0
```

The first call runs like this:

1. `call puts@PLT` transfers control to `.PLT2`.
2. `jmp [rip + puts@GOTPCREL]` reads the GOT entry for `puts`. The
   entry still holds its start value: the address of the next
   instruction, `push 1`. So the jump lands on `push 1`.
3. `push 1` puts the relocation index for `puts` on the stack.
4. `jmp .PLT0` goes to the first PLT entry.
5. `.PLT0` runs `push QWORD PTR [rip + GOT + 8]`. This pushes
   GOT[1], the identifying word for this object.
6. `.PLT0` then runs `jmp [rip + GOT + 16]`. This jumps to GOT[2],
   the resolver routine of the dynamic linker.
7. The dynamic linker pops the two words from the stack. It reads
   relocation entry 1 of type `R_X86_64_JUMP_SLOT`. It finds the
   real address of `puts` in the C library. It writes this address
   into the GOT entry for `puts`. Then it jumps to `puts`. The
   string is printed.
8. The second call to `puts` again reaches `.PLT2`. But now
   `jmp [rip + puts@GOTPCREL]` jumps directly to `puts`. The
   dynamic linker is not involved anymore. The cost is only one
   extra `jmp` instruction.

The `LD_BIND_NOW` environment variable can change this behavior.
If its value is non-null, the dynamic linker resolves all procedure
linkage table entries before it gives control to the program. In
other words, the dynamic linker processes all relocation entries of
type `R_X86_64_JUMP_SLOT` during process initialization. Otherwise,
the dynamic linker evaluates procedure linkage table entries
lazily. It delays symbol resolution and relocation until the first
execution of a table entry.

Relocation entries of type `R_X86_64_TLSDESC` may also use lazy
relocation. TLS means thread-local storage: each thread gets its
own copy of a variable. Lazy TLSDESC relocation uses a single entry
in the procedure linkage table and a single entry in the global
offset table. The dynamic section entries `DT_TLSDESC_PLT` and
`DT_TLSDESC_GOT` give their locations. The document "Thread-Local
Storage Descriptors for IA32 and AMD64/EM64T" describes this
mechanism.

> **Note:** That document is currently available at
> http://www.fsfla.org/~lxoliva/writeups/TLS/RFC-TLSDESC-x86.txt

The design is self-contained. `DT_TLSDESC_GOT` names a GOT entry.
The dynamic loader stores the address of its internal TLS
descriptor resolver function in this GOT entry. `DT_TLSDESC_PLT`
names the address of a PLT entry. This PLT entry works as the TLS
descriptor resolver function for lazy resolution from within this
module. The PLT entry must push the link map of the module onto the
stack and then tail-call the internal TLS descriptor resolver
function.

### Large Models

In the small and medium code models, the maximum 32-bit
displacement limits the size of both the PLT and the GOT. So the
base of the PLT and the top of the GOT can be at most 2 GB apart.

The full addressing space is 16 EB. To support it, both the PLT and
the GOT must be extended. The PLT must work when the GOT is more
than 2 GB away. The GOT itself can be over 2 GB in size.

> **Note:** Sometimes the link editor can prove that the base of
> the PLT is within 2 GB of the top of the GOT. Then a large code
> model object may also use the same PLT layout as the small and
> medium code models.

The next figure shows the extended PLT. It assumes that register
`r15` holds the GOT address.

> **Note:** See the section "Function Prologue" for the rule that
> puts the GOT address in `r15` in the large code models.

**Figure: Final Large Code Model PLT**

```asm
.PLT0:  push   QWORD PTR [r15 + 8]     # GOT[1]
        jmp    [r15 + 16]              # GOT[2]
        nop    DWORD PTR [rax + rax*1] # padding
.PLT1:  movabs r11, OFFSET name1@GOT   # 16 bytes from .PLT0
        jmp    [r11 + r15]
.PLT1a: push   index1                  # "call" dynamic linker
        jmp    .PLT0
.PLT2:  ...                            # 21 bytes from .PLT1
.PLTx:  movabs r11, OFFSET namex@GOT   # 102261125th entry
        jmp    [r11 + r15]
.PLTxa: push   indexx
        push   QWORD PTR [r15 + 8]     # repeat .PLT0 code
        jmp    [r15 + 16]
.PLTy:  ...                            # 27 bytes from .PLTx
```

With this layout, each PLT entry besides `.PLT0` uses only 21 bytes
for the first 102261125 entries. After that point, a `jmp .PLT0`
instruction with a 32-bit displacement can no longer reach
`.PLT0`. So the PLT entry code changes: it repeats the code of
`.PLT0` inside the entry. Each of these later PLT entries is 27
bytes long. Note that this layout drops all alignment rules. This
keeps the PLT size down.

So each extended PLT entry is 5 to 11 bytes larger than a small or
medium code model PLT entry.

The function of entry `.PLT0` stays the same as in the small and
medium code models.

Note that the symbol index is still limited to 32 bits. This allows
up to 4G (about four billion) global and external functions.

UNIX compilers usually support two types of PLT. The options
`-fpic` and `-fPIC` normally select them. When you build
position-independent objects with the large code model, only
`-fPIC` is allowed. The option `-fpic` with the large code model is
reserved for future use.

### Program Interpreter

The *program interpreter* is the program that the system starts to
load and link an executable. For dynamic executables, it is the
dynamic linker. The next table lists the valid program interpreter
for programs that conform to the x86-64 ABI. It also shows the
program interpreter that Linux uses.

**Table: x86-64 Program Interpreter**

| Data Model | Path | Linux Path |
|---|---|---|
| LP64 | `/lib/ld64.so.1` | `/lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2` |
| ILP32 | `/lib/ldx32.so.1` | `/libx32/ld-linux-x32.so.2` |

### Initialization and Termination Functions

The implementation is responsible for running the initialization
functions of a process. The dynamic entries `DT_INIT`,
`DT_INIT_ARRAY`, and `DT_PREINIT_ARRAY` in the executable file and
in the shared object files specify these functions. The
implementation is also responsible for running the termination (or
finalization) functions. The entries `DT_FINI` and `DT_FINI_ARRAY`
specify them, as the *System V ABI* describes. The user program
plays no further part in running the initialization and termination
functions that these dynamic tags specify.

**Example:** A C++ shared library defines a global object with a
constructor. The compiler places a pointer to the constructor code
into the `.init_array` section. The dynamic tag `DT_INIT_ARRAY`
points to that array. When the dynamic linker loads the library, it
calls every function in that array before `main` starts. When the
process exits, the functions from `DT_FINI_ARRAY` run. The program
itself never calls these functions directly.

## Program Property

Program properties describe features of a binary. The following
processor-specific program property type ranges are defined:

> **Note:** See "Linux Extensions to gABI" at
> https://gitlab.com/x86-psABIs/Linux-ABI

**Table: Program Property Type Ranges**

| Name | Value |
|---|---|
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_AND_LO` | `0xc0000002` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_AND_HI` | `0xc0007fff` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_LO` | `0xc0008000` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_HI` | `0xc000ffff` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_AND_LO` | `0xc0010000` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_AND_HI` | `0xc0017fff` |

The `pr_data` field of each property contains a 4-byte unsigned
integer. The link editor combines the `pr_data` values of the input
relocatable files into one output value. The three ranges use three
different combine rules:

- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_AND_LO` up to
  `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_AND_HI`: A bit in the output `pr_data`
  field is set only if it is set in all relocatable input `pr_data`
  fields. In simple words: the rule is a logical AND. If all bits
  in the output `pr_data` field are zero, this property should be
  removed from the output. If a bit is 1, all input relocatables
  have the feature. If a bit is 0, or if the property is missing,
  it is unknown whether all input relocatables have the feature.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_LO` up to
  `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_HI`: A bit in the output `pr_data`
  field is set if it is set in any relocatable input `pr_data`
  field. In simple words: the rule is a logical OR. If all bits in
  the output `pr_data` field are zero, this property should be
  removed from the output. If a bit is 1, some input relocatables
  have the feature. If a bit is 0, or if the property is missing,
  it is unknown whether any input relocatables have the feature.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_AND_LO` up to
  `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_AND_HI`: A bit in the output
  `pr_data` field is set if it is set in any relocatable input
  `pr_data` field, and this property is present in all relocatable
  input files. A missing property means that its bits have unknown
  values. When all bits in the output `pr_data` field are zero,
  this property should stay in the output. It shows that all bits
  really are zero. If the property is in the output, all input
  relocatables have the property. If a bit is 1, some input
  relocatables have the feature. If a bit is 0, none of the input
  relocatables have the feature.

**Example:** Two object files carry the property
`GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_AND` (an AND property). In `a.o` the
`pr_data` value is `0x3` (IBT and SHSTK). In `b.o` the value is
`0x1` (only IBT). The link editor computes `0x3 AND 0x1 = 0x1`. So
the output binary carries the value `0x1`: all inputs support IBT,
but not all inputs support SHSTK. If a third file `c.o` has no
property at all, the linker cannot claim IBT for the whole output,
because it does not know anything about `c.o`.

The following property types are defined:

**Table: Program Property Types**

| Name | Value |
|---|---|
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_AND` | `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_AND_LO + 0` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_USED` | `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_AND_LO + 1` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_NEEDED` | `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_LO + 1` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_USED` | `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_AND_LO + 2` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_NEEDED` | `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_UINT32_OR_LO + 2` |

- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_AND`: The program uses the x86
  processor features that the corresponding bits indicate.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_USED`: The program uses the x86
  processor features that the corresponding bits indicate. Hardware
  support for them is optional. If an x86 ELF binary does not have
  this property, the program may use any x86 processor features.
  You can use `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_USED` to check which
  features a program uses.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_NEEDED`: The program needs the x86
  processor features that the corresponding bits indicate. The
  hardware must support them. The loader may refuse to load a
  program whose `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_NEEDED` features the
  hardware does not support.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_USED`: The program uses the x86
  instruction sets that the corresponding bits indicate. Hardware
  support for them is optional. You can use
  `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_USED` to check which instruction sets a
  program uses.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_NEEDED`: The program needs the x86
  instruction sets that the corresponding bits indicate. The
  hardware must support them. The loader may refuse to load a
  program whose `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_NEEDED` instruction sets
  the hardware does not support.

The following bits are defined for
`GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_AND`:

**Table: GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_AND Bit Flags**

| Name | Value |
|---|---|
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT` | `1U << 0` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK` | `1U << 1` |

- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT`: All executable sections are
  compatible with IBT, indirect branch tracking (see the section
  about IBT). This holds when an `endbr64` instruction starts each
  valid target where an indirect branch instruction can land.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK`: All executable sections are
  compatible with SHSTK, the shadow stack (see the section about
  SHSTK). This holds when the return address popped from the shadow
  stack always matches the return address popped from the normal
  stack.

The following bits are defined for
`GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_USED` and
`GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_NEEDED`:

**Table: Bit Flags For X86 Processor Features**

| Name | Value |
|---|---|
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_X86` | `1U << 0` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_X87` | `1U << 1` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_MMX` | `1U << 2` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XMM` | `1U << 3` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_YMM` | `1U << 4` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_ZMM` | `1U << 5` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_FXSR` | `1U << 6` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XSAVE` | `1U << 7` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XSAVEOPT` | `1U << 8` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XSAVEC` | `1U << 9` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_TMM` | `1U << 10` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_MASK` | `1U << 11` |

> **Note:** The `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_X86` bit should always
> be set when `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_USED` is used.

- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_X86`: Basic x86 processor features
  are used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_X87`: The x87 FPU is used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_MMX`: The MMX register feature is
  used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XMM`: The XMM register feature is
  used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_YMM`: The YMM register feature is
  used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_ZMM`: The ZMM register feature is
  used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_FXSR`: The FXSR feature is used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XSAVE`: The XSAVE feature is used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XSAVEOPT`: The XSAVEOPT feature is
  used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_XSAVEC`: The XSAVEC feature is used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_TMM`: The TMM register feature is
  used.
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_2_MASK`: The mask register feature is
  used.

The following bits are defined for `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_USED`
and `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_NEEDED`. They are based on the
micro-architecture levels in the table "Micro-Architecture Levels"
(see the section about instruction set architecture levels):

**Table: Bit Flags For X86 Instruction Sets**

| Name | Value |
|---|---|
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_BASELINE` | `1U << 0` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_V2` | `1U << 1` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_V3` | `1U << 2` |
| `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_V4` | `1U << 3` |

- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_BASELINE`: The hardware must support the
  `baseline` micro-architecture level (see the table
  "Micro-Architecture Levels").
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_V2`: The hardware must support the
  micro-architecture level `x86-64-v2` (see the table
  "Micro-Architecture Levels").
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_V3`: The hardware must support the
  micro-architecture level `x86-64-v3` (see the table
  "Micro-Architecture Levels").
- `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_ISA_1_V4`: The hardware must support the
  micro-architecture level `x86-64-v4` (see the table
  "Micro-Architecture Levels").

# 6. Libraries

This chapter describes library interfaces that every AMD64 system must
provide. The most important part is the unwind library. The unwind library
is the low-level machinery that walks back through the stack, frame by
frame. C++ exception handling is built on top of it.

> **Note:** The editors of the original ABI document wrote here: a further
> review of the Intel386 ABI is still needed for this chapter.

## C Library

### Global Data Symbols

Some older ABIs, such as the Intel386 ABI, define special global data
symbols for floating-point support. The AMD64 ABI does not provide the
symbols `_fp_hw`, `__flt_rounds`, and `__huge_val`. Programs must not
depend on them.

### Floating Point Environment Functions

ISO C 99 defines the floating-point environment functions in the header
`<fenv.h>`. These functions read and change settings like the rounding
mode.

AMD64 has two floating-point units: the x87 unit and the SSE unit. Each
unit has its own control word. A *control word* is a register that holds
settings such as the rounding mode. Because there are two control words,
the programming environment must keep both values in sync.

This rule has a useful result. A function that only *reads* the control
settings needs to look at one unit only. In these cases, the function
should read the SSE unit. So the function `fegetround` only needs to
report the rounding value of the SSE unit. It can ignore the x87 unit.

**Example:** The next program changes the rounding mode and reads it back.

```c
#include <fenv.h>
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    /* fesetround must update BOTH control words:
       the SSE control word (MXCSR) and the x87 control word.
       This keeps the two units in sync.  */
    fesetround(FE_UPWARD);

    /* fegetround only needs to read the SSE unit (MXCSR).
       Both units are in sync, so one read is enough.  */
    if (fegetround() == FE_UPWARD)
        printf("rounding mode: toward +infinity\n");
    return 0;
}
```

In simple words: writers update both units, readers ask only the SSE unit.

## Unwind Library Interface

This section defines the *Unwind Library interface*. Every AMD64
psABI-compliant system must provide it. The C++ ABI exception-handling
facilities are built on this interface. The interface uses, as its basis,
the Call Frame Information tables described in the DWARF Debugging
Information Format document. These tables tell the unwinder how to find
the previous stack frame at any instruction address.

> **Note:** The overall structure and the external interface of this
> library come from the IA-64 UNIX System V ABI.

The goal of this section is a language-independent interface. Higher-level
exception facilities, such as the ones in C++, can be built on top of it.
Other languages can use it too.

The unwind library interface consists of at least the following routines:

- `_Unwind_RaiseException`
- `_Unwind_Resume`
- `_Unwind_DeleteException`
- `_Unwind_GetGR`
- `_Unwind_SetGR`
- `_Unwind_GetIP`
- `_Unwind_GetIPInfo`
- `_Unwind_SetIP`
- `_Unwind_GetRegionStart`
- `_Unwind_GetLanguageSpecificData`
- `_Unwind_ForcedUnwind`
- `_Unwind_GetCFA`
- `_Unwind_Backtrace`

In addition, the interface defines two data types: `_Unwind_Context` and
`_Unwind_Exception`. They connect a calling runtime (such as the C++
runtime) with the routines above. All routines and interfaces behave as if
they were defined `extern "C"`. In particular, the names are not mangled.
*Name mangling* is the way C++ encodes types into symbol names; it does
not happen here. All names in this interface start with the prefix
`_Unwind_`.

Finally, there is the *personality routine*. This is a function that is
specific to one language and one vendor. The compiler stores a pointer to
it in the unwind descriptor of every stack frame that needs exception
processing. The unwinder calls the personality routine to do
language-specific work. For example, the personality routine decides
which frame handles a given exception.

### Exception Handler Framework

#### Reasons for Unwinding

There are two major reasons to unwind the stack:

- exceptions, as defined by languages that support them (such as C++);
- "forced" unwinding, for example caused by `longjmp` or by thread
  termination.

The interface described here tries to keep both cases similar. But there
is one major difference:

- When a program throws an exception, the stack is unwound while the
  exception travels up. The personality routine of each stack frame knows
  if that frame wants to catch the exception or pass it on. So this choice
  belongs to the personality routine. The personality routine must act
  properly for any type of exception, both "native" and "foreign". A
  *native* exception comes from the same language runtime; a *foreign*
  exception comes from a different one. Guidelines for acting properly
  appear later in this section.

- During "forced unwinding", an external agent drives the unwinding. For
  example, this agent can be the `longjmp` routine. This external agent,
  not each personality routine, knows when to stop unwinding. A
  personality routine gets no choice here. The flag `_UA_FORCE_UNWIND`
  tells the personality routine that it cannot stop the unwinding.

To support both cases, the interface has two different routines.
`_Unwind_RaiseException` performs exception-style unwinding, under control
of the personality routines. `_Unwind_ForcedUnwind` also performs
unwinding, but it lets an external agent intercept the calls to the
personality routine. It does this with a proxy personality routine. The
proxy catches each call and lets the external agent override the choices
of the stack frame's own personality routine.

Because of this design, a personality routine does not need to know about
any external agent that may cause an unwind. For example, the C++
personality routine only needs to handle C++ exceptions (and maybe wrap
foreign exceptions). It does not need to know anything about unwinding
done for `longjmp` or for POSIX thread cancellation.

#### The Unwind Process

The standard ABI exception handling and unwind process starts when the
program raises an exception, in one of the forms above. This call names an
exception object and an exception class.

The runtime framework then runs a two-phase process:

- In the *search* phase, the framework calls the personality routine again
  and again, with the `_UA_SEARCH_PHASE` flag (described below). It starts
  with the current `rip` and register state. Then it unwinds one frame at
  each step, moving to a new `rip`. It stops when a personality routine
  reports success (a handler exists in the queried frame) or when all
  frames report failure (no handler anywhere). In this phase the framework
  does not really restore the unwound state. The personality routine must
  read the state through the API.

- If the search phase reports failure (for example, no handler was found),
  the framework calls `terminate()` instead of starting phase 2.

  If the search phase reports success, the framework restarts in the
  *cleanup* phase. Again it calls the personality routine repeatedly, now
  with the `_UA_CLEANUP_PHASE` flag. It starts with the current `rip` and
  register state, and unwinds one frame at each step. It stops at the
  frame with the identified handler. At that point it restores the
  register state, and control moves to the user's landing pad code. A
  *landing pad* is a piece of code inside a function that runs cleanup
  code or catch code for that frame.

Both phases use the unwind library and the personality routines together.
The reason is a split of duties. Checking if a handler is valid, and
moving control to it, depend on the language. Finding and restoring
previous stack frames does not depend on the language.

A two-phase model is not strictly needed to implement C++ semantics. But
it gives some benefits. For example, the first phase allows an
exception-handling mechanism to *dismiss* an exception before any stack
unwinding begins. This allows *presumptive* exception handling: the
program corrects the problem and continues at the point where the
exception was raised. C++ does not support presumptive exception handling.
Other languages do. The two-phase model lets C++ share the stack with
those languages.

Note that even with two phases, each phase may run more than once for a
single exception. It looks as if the exception is thrown more than once.
For example, nobody can know if a catch clause will re-throw before the
catch clause runs. So exception propagation effectively stops at each
catch clause. If it must restart, it restarts at phase 1. Destructor-only
frames (pure cleanup code) do not need this treatment. So phase 1 can
safely pass over all destructor-only frames at once, and stop at the next
enclosing catch clause.

For example, imagine the first two unwound frames contain only cleanup
code, and the third frame contains a C++ catch clause. In phase 1, the
personality routine does not report a handler for the first two frames.
It must report a handler for the third frame. The reason: nobody knows how
the exception will leave that third frame. In C++, the catch clause can
re-throw the exception or throw a new one.

**Example:** A small C++ program, followed step by step.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

struct Guard {
    ~Guard() { std::puts("cleanup in g()"); }
};

void f() { throw 42; }          // frame 1: throws

void g() { Guard guard; f(); }  // frame 2: only a destructor

int main() {                    // frame 3: has a catch clause
    try {
        g();
    } catch (int value) {
        std::printf("caught %d\n", value);
    }
    return 0;
}
```

This is what happens, step by step:

1. `f` executes `throw 42`. The C++ runtime allocates an exception object.
   The object starts with an `_Unwind_Exception` header. The runtime fills
   in `exception_class` and `exception_cleanup`. Then it calls
   `_Unwind_RaiseException`.
2. Phase 1 (search) starts. The unwinder calls the personality routine for
   the frame of `f`, with `_UA_SEARCH_PHASE` set. There is no handler in
   `f`, so the routine returns `_URC_CONTINUE_UNWIND`.
3. The unwinder computes the frame of `g` and asks again. The frame of `g`
   has only cleanup work (the destructor of `guard`). That is not a
   handler. The routine returns `_URC_CONTINUE_UNWIND` again.
4. The unwinder reaches the frame of `main`. The `catch (int)` clause
   matches the thrown type. The personality routine returns
   `_URC_HANDLER_FOUND`. The unwinder remembers this handler frame, using
   the private fields of the exception header. The stack is still
   untouched.
5. Phase 2 (cleanup) starts from the top again, now with
   `_UA_CLEANUP_PHASE` set. For the frame of `f` there is nothing to do:
   `_URC_CONTINUE_UNWIND`.
6. For the frame of `g`, the destructor must run. The personality routine
   sets the registers for the landing pad of `g` (for example with
   `_Unwind_SetIP` and `_Unwind_SetGR`) and returns
   `_URC_INSTALL_CONTEXT`. The unwinder restores the frame's registers and
   jumps into the landing pad. The landing pad runs `~Guard()` and prints
   "cleanup in g()". Then it calls `_Unwind_Resume` to continue the
   unwinding.
7. For the frame of `main`, the unwinder sets both `_UA_CLEANUP_PHASE` and
   `_UA_HANDLER_FRAME`. The personality routine prepares the landing pad
   of the catch clause. It passes the exception object pointer in a
   reserved scratch register. It returns `_URC_INSTALL_CONTEXT`. Control
   moves into the catch clause, which prints "caught 42". At the end of
   the catch, the C++ runtime frees the exception object.

The program prints "cleanup in g()" first, then "caught 42".

The next sections describe the API that the AMD64 psABI specifies for this
framework.

### Data Structures

#### Reason Codes

The unwind interface uses reason codes in several places. A reason code
names the reason for a failure or for another action. The codes are:

```c
typedef enum {
    _URC_NO_REASON = 0,
    _URC_FOREIGN_EXCEPTION_CAUGHT = 1,
    _URC_FATAL_PHASE2_ERROR = 2,
    _URC_FATAL_PHASE1_ERROR = 3,
    _URC_NORMAL_STOP = 4,
    _URC_END_OF_STACK = 5,
    _URC_HANDLER_FOUND = 6,
    _URC_INSTALL_CONTEXT = 7,
    _URC_CONTINUE_UNWIND = 8
} _Unwind_Reason_Code;
```

The meaning of each code is described below, in the section for the
routine that uses it.

#### Exception Header

The unwind interface represents an exception in flight as a pointer to an
exception header object. The full layout of an exception object depends on
the language and the implementation. But the object always starts with a
header that the unwind interface understands. The header is defined as
follows:

```c
typedef void (*_Unwind_Exception_Cleanup_Fn)
    (_Unwind_Reason_Code reason,
     struct _Unwind_Exception *exc);

struct _Unwind_Exception {
    uint64                       exception_class;
    _Unwind_Exception_Cleanup_Fn exception_cleanup;
    uint64                       private_1;
    uint64                       private_2;
};
```

An `_Unwind_Exception` object must be eightbyte aligned. That means its
address must be a multiple of 8. User code sets the first two fields
before it raises the exception. Only the runtime may touch the last two
fields.

The `exception_class` field identifies the kind of exception. Its value
depends on the language and the implementation. It lets a personality
routine tell native exceptions from foreign ones. By convention, the high
4 bytes name the vendor (for example `AMD\0`), and the low 4 bytes name
the language. For the C++ ABI described in this document, the low four
bytes are `C++\0`.

**Example:** Take the vendor `AMD\0` and the language `C++\0`. The eight
bytes of the class are the characters `A`, `M`, `D`, `\0`, `C`, `+`, `+`,
`\0`, from the most significant byte down to the least significant byte.
In ASCII, `A` is `0x41`, `M` is `0x4D`, `D` is `0x44`, `C` is `0x43`, and
`+` is `0x2B`. So, read as one 64-bit number, this `exception_class` is
`0x414D4400432B2B00`. A C++ personality routine can compare the low 4
bytes against `C++\0` (`0x432B2B00`) to check: is this a native C++
exception?

The runtime calls the `exception_cleanup` routine when an exception object
must be destroyed by a different runtime than the runtime that created it.
This happens, for example, when a C++ catch handler catches a Java
exception. In such a case, a reason code (see above) says why the
exception object must be deleted:

- `_URC_FOREIGN_EXCEPTION_CAUGHT = 1`: a different runtime caught this
  exception. Nested foreign exceptions, and re-throwing a foreign
  exception, cause undefined behavior.
- `_URC_FATAL_PHASE1_ERROR = 3`: the personality routine met an error
  during phase 1, other than the specific error codes defined.
- `_URC_FATAL_PHASE2_ERROR = 2`: the personality routine met an error
  during phase 2, for example a stack corruption.

Normally, the library should report all errors during phase 1, by
returning from `_Unwind_RaiseException`. But landing pad code can corrupt
the stack between phase 1 and phase 2. For a C++ exception, the runtime
should call `terminate()` in that case.

The private unwinder state lives in `private_1` and `private_2` inside the
exception object. Personality routines and other parts of the
language-specific runtime must not read or write these fields. The
unwinder implementation on the host uses them for internal data. For
example, it can remember the final handler frame there between the two
unwinding phases.

A typical runtime, such as the C++ runtime, adds language-specific data to
the exception. This data is used to process the exception. Usually it is a
contiguous block of memory right after the `_Unwind_Exception` object. But
this layout is not required. Any layout works, as long as the matching
personality routines know how to handle it, and the `exception_cleanup`
routine frees it properly.

#### Unwind Context

The `_Unwind_Context` type is an opaque type. *Opaque* means: user code
sees only a pointer to it, never its fields. It refers to a
system-specific data structure that the system unwinder uses. The system
creates and destroys this context. The unwinder passes it to the
personality routine during unwinding.

```c
struct _Unwind_Context
```

### Throwing an Exception

#### _Unwind_RaiseException

```c
_Unwind_Reason_Code _Unwind_RaiseException
    ( struct _Unwind_Exception *exception_object );
```

This routine raises an exception. It passes along the given exception
object. The caller must set the `exception_class` and `exception_cleanup`
fields of the object first. The language-specific runtime allocates the
exception object, in a language-specific format. The only fixed rule: the
object must contain an `_Unwind_Exception` struct (see the section
"Exception Header" above).

`_Unwind_RaiseException` does not return, unless it finds an error
condition. Error conditions are, for example: no handler exists for the
exception, or the stack format is bad. In an error case, it returns an
`_Unwind_Reason_Code` value. The possible values are:

- `_URC_END_OF_STACK`: the unwinder reached the end of the stack during
  phase 1 without finding a handler. The unwind runtime has not modified
  the stack. The C++ runtime normally calls `uncaught_exception()` in this
  case.
- `_URC_FATAL_PHASE1_ERROR`: the unwinder met an unexpected error during
  phase 1, for example stack corruption. The unwind runtime has not
  modified the stack. The C++ runtime normally calls `terminate()` in this
  case.

If the unwinder meets an unexpected error during phase 2, it should return
`_URC_FATAL_PHASE2_ERROR` to its caller. In C++, this caller is usually
`__cxa_throw`, which then calls `terminate()`.

In the phase 2 error case, the unwind runtime has probably already
modified the stack (for example, popped frames from it) or the register
context. Landing pad code may also have corrupted them. As a result, the
caller of `_Unwind_RaiseException` can make no assumptions about the state
of its stack or its registers.

#### _Unwind_ForcedUnwind

```c
typedef _Unwind_Reason_Code (*_Unwind_Stop_Fn)
    (int version,
     _Unwind_Action actions,
     uint64 exceptionClass,
     struct _Unwind_Exception *exceptionObject,
     struct _Unwind_Context *context,
     void *stop_parameter );

_Unwind_Reason_Code _Unwind_ForcedUnwind
    ( struct _Unwind_Exception *exception_object,
      _Unwind_Stop_Fn stop,
      void *stop_parameter );
```

This routine raises an exception for forced unwinding. It passes along the
given exception object. The caller must set the `exception_class` and
`exception_cleanup` fields first. The language-specific runtime allocates
the exception object, in a language-specific format. The only fixed rule:
the object must contain an `_Unwind_Exception` struct (see the section
"Exception Header" above).

Forced unwinding is a single-phase process. It is like phase 2 of the
normal exception-handling process. The `stop` and `stop_parameter`
arguments control the end of the unwind process. They replace the usual
personality routine query. The unwinder calls the `stop` function for each
unwind frame. It passes the same parameters as for the usual personality
routine (described below), plus the extra `stop_parameter`.

When the `stop` function identifies the destination frame, it transfers
control to the user code. How it transfers control is its own business;
the ABI does not specify it. It does not return. Normally it calls
`_Unwind_DeleteException` first. If the current frame is not the
destination, the `stop` function returns an `_Unwind_Reason_Code` value:

- `_URC_NO_REASON`: this is not the destination frame. The unwind runtime
  then calls the frame's personality routine, with the `_UA_FORCE_UNWIND`
  and `_UA_CLEANUP_PHASE` flags set in `actions`. After that, it unwinds
  to the next frame and calls the `stop` function again.
- `_URC_END_OF_STACK`: `_Unwind_ForcedUnwind` may need to do special work
  when it reaches the end of the stack. So the unwind runtime calls the
  `stop` function one more time after the last frame is rejected. In this
  final call, the stack pointer in the context is `NULL`. The `stop`
  function must detect this condition (by noticing the `NULL` stack
  pointer). It may return this reason code if it cannot handle the
  end-of-stack case.
- `_URC_FATAL_PHASE2_ERROR`: the `stop` function may return this code for
  other fatal conditions, for example stack corruption.

If the `stop` function returns any reason code other than
`_URC_NO_REASON`, the stack state is unknown from the point of view of the
caller of `_Unwind_ForcedUnwind`. So the unwind library should not try to
return normally. Instead, it should return `_URC_FATAL_PHASE2_ERROR` to
its caller.

**Example: `longjmp_unwind()`.** The expected implementation of
`longjmp_unwind()` works like this. The `setjmp()` routine saves the state
to restore, including the frame pointer, in its usual place. The
`longjmp_unwind()` routine then calls `_Unwind_ForcedUnwind` with a `stop`
function. This `stop` function compares the frame pointer in the context
record with the saved frame pointer. If the two are equal, it restores the
`setjmp()` state in the usual way. Otherwise it returns `_URC_NO_REASON`
or `_URC_END_OF_STACK`.

Maybe someone will need two-phase forced unwinding in the future. In that
case, a new routine can be defined to request it, and a new `actions` flag
can be defined to support it.

#### _Unwind_Resume

```c
void _Unwind_Resume
    (struct _Unwind_Exception *exception_object);
```

This routine resumes the propagation of an existing exception. It is used,
for example, after cleanup code has run in a partially unwound stack. The
compiler inserts a call to this routine at the end of a landing pad that
only did cleanup and did not resume normal execution. The call makes the
unwinding continue.

Do not use `_Unwind_Resume` to implement re-throwing. From the point of
view of the unwind runtime, the catch code that re-throws was a handler.
The previous unwinding session ended when control entered it. Re-throwing
is implemented by calling `_Unwind_RaiseException` again, with the same
exception object.

`_Unwind_Resume` is the only routine in the unwind library that generated
code calls directly. The compiler emits the call at the end of a landing
pad, in a "landing-pad" model of exception handling.

### Exception Object Management

#### _Unwind_DeleteException

```c
void _Unwind_DeleteException
    (struct _Unwind_Exception *exception_object);
```

This routine deletes the given exception object. Imagine a runtime that
catches a foreign exception and then resumes normal execution. This
runtime does not know how to delete the foreign exception itself. It
deletes the exception by calling `_Unwind_DeleteException`. This is a
convenience function. It simply calls the function stored in the
`exception_cleanup` field of the exception header.

### Context Management

The context management functions pass information about the unwind context
between three parties: the unwind library, the personality routine, and
the landing pad. The unwind context means the unwind descriptors plus the
user register state. The functions can read or set the saved register
images of a stack frame, for a given unwind context. They can also find
the current unwind descriptors and the current unwind frame.

#### _Unwind_GetGR

```c
uint64 _Unwind_GetGR
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context, int index);
```

This function returns the 64-bit value of the given general register. The
`index` names the register. It uses the DWARF register numbers; see the
table "DWARF register number mapping" in the section about DWARF
definitions. For example, index 5 is `rdi` and index 7 is `rsp`.

During the two phases of unwinding, no register has a guaranteed value.

#### _Unwind_SetGR

```c
void _Unwind_SetGR
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context,
     int index,
     uint64 new_value);
```

This function sets the 64-bit value of the given register. The `index`
names the register, in the same way as for `_Unwind_GetGR`.

The behavior is only guaranteed in one situation: the call happens during
phase 2 of unwinding, and the unwind context represents a handler frame,
and the personality routine will return `_URC_INSTALL_CONTEXT` for it. In
that case, only the registers `rdi`, `rsi`, `rdx`, and `rcx` should be
set. These scratch registers are reserved for passing arguments between
the personality routine and the landing pads.

#### _Unwind_GetIP

```c
uint64 _Unwind_GetIP
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context);
```

This function returns the 64-bit value of the instruction pointer (IP).
The instruction pointer is the address of the code position in the frame.

During unwinding, this value is guaranteed to be the address of the
instruction directly after the call site, in the function that the unwind
context identifies. This address may lie outside the procedure fragment.
That happens when the call is known to never return, such as a call to
`_Unwind_Resume`.

Some applications unwind through asynchronous signals or through other
places that are not calls. Such applications should use
`_Unwind_GetIPInfo` (below) and the extra flag that it provides.

#### _Unwind_GetIPInfo

```c
uint64 _Unwind_GetIPInfo
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context, int *ip_before_insn);
```

This function returns the same value as `_Unwind_GetIP`. In addition, the
argument `ip_before_insn` must not be null. The function stores a flag in
`*ip_before_insn`. The flag says if the returned pointer is at, or after,
the first instruction that has not fully executed yet.

If `*ip_before_insn` is false, the caller should assume that the returned
instruction pointer points after a call instruction that has not returned
yet. In general, the caller should then use the call instruction before
that address as the code location of the unwind context. In practice, the
caller can approximate this by subtracting one from the returned
instruction pointer.

If `*ip_before_insn` is true, the instruction pointer does not point after
an active call site. Usually this means the instruction pointer marks the
place where an asynchronous signal arrived. In this case, the caller
should use the returned value directly as the code location of the unwind
context, without any adjustment.

#### _Unwind_SetIP

```c
void _Unwind_SetIP
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context,
     uint64 new_value);
```

This function sets the value of the instruction pointer (IP) for the
routine that the unwind context identifies.

The behavior is only guaranteed when the unwind context represents a
handler frame, for which the personality routine will return
`_URC_INSTALL_CONTEXT`. In this case, control will move to the given
address. That address should be the address of a landing pad.

#### _Unwind_GetLanguageSpecificData

```c
uint64 _Unwind_GetLanguageSpecificData
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context);
```

This routine returns the address of the language-specific data area
(LSDA) for the current stack frame. The LSDA holds tables that the
personality routine reads, for example the list of call sites and their
handlers.

This routine is not strictly required. A personality routine could find
the same address itself, starting from `_Unwind_GetIP` and using the
documented format of the DWARF Call Frame Information tables. But the
unwinder already did this work when it looked up the personality routine.
So it makes sense to cache the result in the context. Another option would
be to pass the address as an argument to the personality routine.

#### _Unwind_GetRegionStart

```c
uint64 _Unwind_GetRegionStart
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context);
```

This routine returns the address where the current procedure, or code
fragment, begins. "Current" means: the one that the current unwind
descriptor block describes.

This information is needed to read data that is stored relative to the
start of the procedure fragment. For example, a call site table may be
stored relative to the start of the procedure fragment that contains the
calls. During unwinding, the function returns the start of the procedure
fragment that contains the call site in the current stack frame.

#### _Unwind_GetCFA

```c
uint64 _Unwind_GetCFA
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context);
```

This function returns the 64-bit Canonical Frame Address (CFA). The CFA is
defined as the value of `rsp` at the call site in the previous frame. In
simple words: it is where the stack pointer stood in the caller, just
before the caller pushed the return address. This value is guaranteed to
be correct any time the context has been passed to a personality routine
or to a stop function.

#### _Unwind_Backtrace

```c
typedef _Unwind_Reason_Code (*_Unwind_Trace_Fn)
    (struct _Unwind_Context *context, void *trace_parameter);

_Unwind_Reason_Code _Unwind_Backtrace
    (_Unwind_Trace_Fn trace, void *trace_parameter);
```

This function walks the stack of the caller without raising any exception.
For each stack frame, it calls the `trace` callback. It passes the unwind
context of that frame and the user's `trace_parameter`. Inside the
callback, you can use the context functions above, for example
`_Unwind_GetIP`, `_Unwind_GetIPInfo`, `_Unwind_GetCFA`, and
`_Unwind_GetRegionStart`.

The callback returns `_URC_NO_REASON` to continue the walk. Any other
value stops the walk. When the walk reaches the end of the stack,
`_Unwind_Backtrace` returns `_URC_END_OF_STACK`. Tools like the
`backtrace()` function of the C library are built on this routine.

**Example:** A tiny backtrace program in C. It prints one line for each
active stack frame.

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <unwind.h>

static _Unwind_Reason_Code
print_frame(struct _Unwind_Context *context, void *arg)
{
    int *count = arg;
    int ip_before_insn = 0;
    unsigned long ip = _Unwind_GetIPInfo(context, &ip_before_insn);

    /* In a normal call frame, the IP points after the call
       instruction.  Step back one byte, so the address lies
       inside the call site.  */
    if (!ip_before_insn)
        ip -= 1;

    printf("frame %d: ip=0x%lx cfa=0x%lx region=0x%lx\n",
           *count, ip,
           (unsigned long) _Unwind_GetCFA(context),
           (unsigned long) _Unwind_GetRegionStart(context));
    (*count)++;
    return _URC_NO_REASON;   /* keep walking */
}

static void c(void)
{
    int count = 0;
    _Unwind_Backtrace(print_frame, &count);
}

static void b(void) { c(); }
static void a(void) { b(); }

int main(void)
{
    a();
    return 0;
}
```

Build it with `gcc -O0 -funwind-tables backtrace.c`. The option
`-funwind-tables` makes sure every function has unwind information in
`.eh_frame`. On most AMD64 systems this is already the default. The
program prints the frames of `c`, `b`, `a`, and `main`, and then the
frames of the C startup code, from the newest frame to the oldest one.

### Personality Routine

```c
_Unwind_Reason_Code (*__personality_routine)
    (int version,
     _Unwind_Action actions,
     uint64 exceptionClass,
     struct _Unwind_Exception *exceptionObject,
     struct _Unwind_Context *context);
```

The personality routine is the function in the C++ runtime library (or in
the runtime of another language) that connects two worlds: the system
unwind library and the language-specific exception handling rules. The
routine is specific to the code fragment that one unwind info block
describes. The unwinder always reaches it through the pointer in the
unwind info block. So the personality routine has no name that the psABI
specifies.

#### Parameters

The personality routine takes these parameters:

- `version`: the version number of the unwinding runtime. It helps to
  detect a mismatch between the unwinder conventions and the personality
  routine, and it allows backward compatibility. For the conventions in
  this document, `version` is 1.
- `actions`: a bit mask that says what work the personality routine should
  do. The possible actions are described below.
- `exceptionClass`: an 8-byte identifier for the type of the thrown
  exception. By convention, the high 4 bytes name the vendor (for example
  `AMD\0`), and the low 4 bytes name the language. For the C++ ABI in this
  document, the low four bytes are `C++\0`. This is not a null-terminated
  string. Some implementations use no null bytes at all.
- `exceptionObject`: a pointer to the memory that records the information
  needed to process the exception under the rules of the given language
  (see the section "Exception Header" above).
- `context`: unwinder state information for the personality routine. This
  is an opaque handle. The personality routine uses it, for example, to
  access the registers of the frame (see the section "Unwind Context"
  above).
- return value: the returned reason code tells the unwinder how to
  continue, and reports possible errors. See the following section.

#### Personality Routine Actions

The `actions` argument of the personality routine is a bitwise OR of one
or more of these constants:

```c
typedef int _Unwind_Action;
const _Unwind_Action _UA_SEARCH_PHASE  = 1;
const _Unwind_Action _UA_CLEANUP_PHASE = 2;
const _Unwind_Action _UA_HANDLER_FRAME = 4;
const _Unwind_Action _UA_FORCE_UNWIND  = 8;
```

- `_UA_SEARCH_PHASE`: the personality routine should check if the current
  frame contains a handler. If yes, it returns `_URC_HANDLER_FOUND`. If
  no, it returns `_URC_CONTINUE_UNWIND`. `_UA_SEARCH_PHASE` cannot be set
  at the same time as `_UA_CLEANUP_PHASE`.

- `_UA_CLEANUP_PHASE`: the personality routine should perform cleanup for
  the current frame. It has two options. It can perform the cleanup
  itself, by calling nested procedures, and then return
  `_URC_CONTINUE_UNWIND`. Or it can set up the registers (including the
  IP) to transfer control to a landing pad, and return
  `_URC_INSTALL_CONTEXT`.

- `_UA_HANDLER_FRAME`: during phase 2, this flag tells the personality
  routine: the current frame is the frame that was marked as the handler
  frame during phase 1. The personality routine may not change its mind
  between phase 1 and phase 2. It must handle the exception in this frame
  in phase 2.

- `_UA_FORCE_UNWIND`: during phase 2, this flag says that no language may
  "catch" the exception. The unwinder sets this flag while it unwinds the
  stack for `longjmp` or during thread cancellation. User code in a catch
  clause may still run. But the catch clause must resume the unwinding
  with a call to `_Unwind_Resume` when it finishes.

#### Transferring Control to a Landing Pad

Suppose the personality routine decides, in phase 2, that control should
move to a landing pad. It may set up registers (including the IP) with
values that fit the landing pad, for example landing pad parameters. It
does this with the context management routines above. Then it returns
`_URC_INSTALL_CONTEXT`.

Before the landing pad code runs, the unwind library restores the
registers that the personality routine did not change. It uses the context
record for this. Each register gets the value it had in that frame, before
the call that threw the exception. The library restores all registers that
the base ABI lists as callee-saved. It also restores the scratch registers
`rdi`, `rsi`, `rdx`, and `rcx` (see below). All other scratch
(caller-saved) registers are not preserved. Their contents are undefined
at the transfer.

The landing pad has two options. It can resume normal execution, for
example at the end of a C++ catch. Or it can resume the unwinding, by
calling `_Unwind_Resume` with the `exceptionObject` argument that the
personality routine received. `_Unwind_Resume` never returns.

The landing pad must call `_Unwind_Resume` if, and only if, the
personality routine did not return `_URC_HANDLER_FOUND` during phase 1.
Because of this rule, the unwinder can allocate resources (for example
memory) and track them in the reserved words of the exception object. It
should free these resources before it transfers control to the last
landing pad, the handler. It does not need to free them before it enters a
non-handler landing pad, because `_Unwind_Resume` will run later anyway.

The landing pad may receive arguments from the runtime. The personality
routine typically places them in registers with `_Unwind_SetGR`. If a
landing pad can call `_Unwind_Resume`, one argument must be the
`exceptionObject` pointer. The landing pad must preserve this pointer, so
it can pass it to `_Unwind_Resume`.

The landing pad may receive more arguments. A common one is a switch value
that names the type of the exception. Four scratch registers are reserved
for this use: `rdi`, `rsi`, `rdx`, and `rcx`.

> **Note:** GCC uses `rax` and `rdx` instead.

#### Rules for Correct Inter-Language Operation

The following rules must be observed. They make different languages, and
runtimes from different vendors, work together correctly.

The personality routine must not alter an exception whose class is
unknown. The rules for processing a foreign exception depend on the
language of the stack frame being unwound. In particular, that language
decides how exceptions from a foreign language map to the native language
in that frame.

Suppose a runtime resumes normal execution, and the caught exception was
created by another runtime. Then it should call
`_Unwind_DeleteException`. This is true even if it understands the format
of the exception object. That can happen, for example, between two
different C++ runtimes.

A runtime may not catch an exception if the `_UA_FORCE_UNWIND` flag was
passed to the personality routine.

**Example: foreign exceptions in C++.** In C++, a `catch(...)` statement
can catch foreign exceptions. They can also be caught as if they were of a
`__foreign_exception` class, defined in `<exception>`. The
`__foreign_exception` class may have subclasses, such as
`__java_exception` and `__ada_exception`, if the runtime can identify some
foreign languages.

The behavior is undefined in the following cases:

- A `__foreign_exception` catch argument is accessed in any way
  (including taking its address).
- A `__foreign_exception` is active at the same time as another exception.
  This means: either a nested exception occurs while the program catches
  the foreign exception, or the foreign exception was itself nested.
- `uncaught_exception()`, `set_terminate()`, `set_unexpected()`,
  `terminate()`, or `unexpected()` is called while a foreign exception
  exists. An example is calling `set_terminate()` during the unwinding of
  a foreign exception.

All these cases might touch C++-specific content of the thrown exception,
for example to chain active exceptions.

Otherwise, a catch block that catches a foreign exception is allowed:

- to resume normal execution. This stops the propagation of the foreign
  exception and deletes it. Or
- to re-throw the foreign exception. In that case, the C++ runtime must
  leave the original exception object unchanged.

A catch-all block may run during forced unwinding. For example, a
`longjmp` may execute code in a `catch(...)` block during stack unwinding.
But if this happens, unwinding continues at the end of the catch-all
block. It continues whether or not there is an explicit re-throw.

Setting the low 4 bytes of the exception class to `C++\0` is reserved.
Only C++ runtimes that are compatible with the common C++ ABI may use it.

## Unwinding Through Assembler Code

For successful unwinding on AMD64, every function must provide valid debug
information in the DWARF Debugging Information Format. For high-level
languages (for example C, C++, Fortran, or Ada), the compiler generates
this information itself. For hand-written assembly routines, the author of
the code must provide the debug info. To make this task easier, the
assembler offers some special directives:

- `.cfi_startproc` is used at the start of each function that should have
  an entry in `.eh_frame`. It initializes some internal data structures
  and emits architecture-dependent initial CFI instructions. CFI means
  Call Frame Information: the rules that tell the unwinder how to restore
  the previous frame. Each `.cfi_startproc` directive must be closed by a
  `.cfi_endproc`.

- `.cfi_endproc` is used at the end of a function. It closes the unwind
  entry that `.cfi_startproc` opened, and emits the entry to `.eh_frame`.

- `.cfi_def_cfa REGISTER, OFFSET` defines a new rule for computing the
  CFA: take the address from REGISTER and add OFFSET to it.
