- `.cfi_def_cfa_register REGISTER` modifies the rule for computing the
  CFA. From now on, REGISTER is used instead of the old register. The
  offset stays the same.

- `.cfi_def_cfa_offset OFFSET` modifies the rule for computing the CFA.
  The register stays the same, but OFFSET is new. Note that OFFSET is the
  absolute offset. The unwinder adds it to the defined register to get the
  CFA address.

- `.cfi_adjust_cfa_offset OFFSET` is similar to `.cfi_def_cfa_offset`,
  but OFFSET is a relative value. It is added to, or subtracted from, the
  previous offset.

- `.cfi_offset REGISTER, OFFSET` records that the previous value of
  REGISTER is saved at offset OFFSET from the CFA.

- `.cfi_rel_offset REGISTER, OFFSET` records that the previous value of
  REGISTER is saved at offset OFFSET from the current CFA register. The
  assembler transforms this into `.cfi_offset`, using the known distance
  between the CFA register and the CFA. This form is often easier to use,
  because the number matches the code that it annotates.

- `.cfi_escape EXPRESSION[, ...]` lets you add arbitrary bytes to the
  unwind info. You can use this for OS-specific CFI opcodes, or for
  generic CFI opcodes that the assembler does not support.

**Figure: Examples for unwinding in assembler**

```asm
        .intel_syntax noprefix

# - function with local variable allocated on the stack
        .type   func_locvars,@function
func_locvars:
        .cfi_startproc
        # allocate space for local vars
        sub     rsp, 0x1234
        .cfi_adjust_cfa_offset  0x1234
        # body
        ...
        # release space of local vars and return
        add     rsp, 0x1234
        .cfi_adjust_cfa_offset  -0x1234
        ret
        .cfi_endproc

# - function that moves frame pointer to another register
#   and then allocates space for local variables
        .type   func_otherreg,@function
func_otherreg:
        .cfi_startproc
        # save frame pointer to r12
        mov     r12, rsp
        .cfi_def_cfa_register   r12
        # allocate space for local vars
        # (no .cfi_{def,adjust}_cfa_offset needed here,
        # because CFA is computed from r12!)
        sub     rsp, 100
        # body
        ...
        # restore frame pointer from r12
        mov     rsp, r12
        .cfi_def_cfa_register   rsp
        ret
        .cfi_endproc
```

In the first function, the CFA rule follows `rsp`. Every change of `rsp`
needs a matching `.cfi_adjust_cfa_offset`. In the second function, the CFA
rule follows `r12`. The register `r12` does not move, so the `sub` on
`rsp` needs no CFI directive at all.

**Example:** A complete minimal function with a classic frame pointer
prologue, in Intel syntax, with correct CFI annotations. A *prologue* is
the code at the start of a function that sets up the stack frame.

```asm
        .intel_syntax noprefix
        .text
        .globl  add_ten
        .type   add_ten, @function
add_ten:
        .cfi_startproc
        push    rbp
        .cfi_def_cfa_offset 16
        .cfi_offset rbp, -16
        mov     rbp, rsp
        .cfi_def_cfa_register rbp
        lea     rax, [rdi + 10]
        pop     rbp
        .cfi_def_cfa rsp, 8
        ret
        .cfi_endproc
        .size   add_ten, .-add_ten
```

Line by line:

- `.cfi_startproc` opens the unwind entry. It also sets the initial rule
  for AMD64: CFA = `rsp` + 8. Why 8? The `call` instruction pushed the
  8-byte return address, and the CFA is the value of `rsp` before that
  push.
- `push rbp` moves `rsp` down by 8 bytes. The CFA itself never moves, so
  the rule must change: CFA = `rsp` + 16. The directive
  `.cfi_def_cfa_offset 16` records the new offset.
- The pushed `rbp` now lies at address CFA - 16. The directive
  `.cfi_offset rbp, -16` tells the unwinder where to find the caller's
  `rbp` value.
- `mov rbp, rsp` copies the stack pointer into the frame pointer. From
  here on, `rbp` is stable, even if `rsp` moves later. So we switch the
  CFA rule to it: `.cfi_def_cfa_register rbp`. The offset 16 stays. Now
  CFA = `rbp` + 16.
- `lea rax, [rdi + 10]` is the body. The first integer argument arrives in
  `rdi`, and the return value goes in `rax`. So the function returns its
  argument plus 10.
- `pop rbp` restores the caller's `rbp` and moves `rsp` up by 8. Now `rbp`
  no longer points into this frame. The rule must go back to the stack
  pointer: `.cfi_def_cfa rsp, 8` sets both the register and the offset in
  one step.
- `ret` pops the return address and returns.
- `.cfi_endproc` closes the entry and emits it to `.eh_frame`.

With these annotations, the unwinder can stop at any instruction of
`add_ten` and still compute the CFA, the return address (always at
CFA - 8), and the saved `rbp`. If an exception passes through this
function, or if a profiler takes a backtrace here, the unwind succeeds.

# 7. Development Environment

This chapter describes what the compiler gives you when you build code
for x86-64.

When you compile C or C++ code, the *pre-processor* defines some symbols
for you. The pre-processor is the first stage of the compiler. It handles
lines that start with `#`, such as `#include` and `#ifdef`. A *pre-defined
symbol* (also called a pre-defined *macro*) is a name that the compiler
defines by itself. You do not define it in your source code. During
compilation of C or C++ code, at least the symbols in the table below are
defined.

> **Note:** The symbols `_LP64` and `__LP64__` were added to GCC 3.3 in
> March 2003. Very old compilers may not define them.

These symbols depend on the *programming model*. A programming model
fixes the sizes of the basic C types:

- *LP64* means: `long` and pointers are 64 bits, and `int` is 32 bits.
  This is the normal 64-bit model on x86-64.
- *ILP32* means: `int`, `long`, and pointers are all 32 bits. This model
  is also called "x32". It runs on x86-64 processors, but it uses 32-bit
  pointers to save memory.

**Table: Predefined pre-processor symbols**

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `__amd64` | Defined for both the LP64 and the ILP32 programming models. |
| `__amd64__` | Defined for both the LP64 and the ILP32 programming models. |
| `__x86_64` | Defined for both the LP64 and the ILP32 programming models. |
| `__x86_64__` | Defined for both the LP64 and the ILP32 programming models. |
| `_LP64` | Defined for the LP64 programming model. |
| `__LP64__` | Defined for the LP64 programming model. |
| `_ILP32` | Defined for the ILP32 programming model. |
| `__ILP32__` | Defined for the ILP32 programming model. |

In simple words: the first four symbols tell you the target processor.
The last four symbols tell you the pointer size model. Each symbol exists
in two spellings, because different compilers historically used different
forms. New code should prefer the forms with underscores on both sides,
such as `__x86_64__` and `__LP64__`, because they are reserved names in
the C standard.

**Example:** You can use these symbols to write portable code. The code
below picks the right path for each target:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
#ifdef __x86_64__
    /* This branch compiles only for x86-64 targets. */
# ifdef __LP64__
    puts("x86-64, LP64 model: pointers are 64 bits");
# else
    puts("x86-64, ILP32 (x32) model: pointers are 32 bits");
# endif
#else
    puts("some other architecture");
#endif
    return 0;
}
```

The check `#ifdef __x86_64__` is true for both models. The extra check on
`__LP64__` then separates the two models.

**Example:** This table shows how the two models change the basic type
sizes:

**Table: Type sizes in the two programming models**

| Type | LP64 size | ILP32 size |
|---|---|---|
| `int` | 4 bytes | 4 bytes |
| `long` | 8 bytes | 4 bytes |
| `long long` | 8 bytes | 8 bytes |
| pointer | 8 bytes | 4 bytes |

**Example:** You can check which symbols your compiler defines. The GCC
option `-E` stops after the pre-processor. The option `-dM` prints all
defined macros. Together they list every pre-defined symbol:

```
$ gcc -dM -E - < /dev/null | grep -E 'amd64|x86_64|LP64'
#define __amd64 1
#define __amd64__ 1
#define __x86_64 1
#define __x86_64__ 1
#define _LP64 1
#define __LP64__ 1
```

With `gcc -mx32`, the same command shows `_ILP32` and `__ILP32__` instead
of `_LP64` and `__LP64__`. The full list of macros depends on your
compiler version.

# 9. Conventions

This chapter documents some features that are special to the AMD64
(x86-64) ABI.

> **Note:** Editor's note from the original document: This chapter is
> used to document some features special to the AMD64 ABI. The different
> sections might be moved to another place or removed completely.

## C++

For the C++ ABI we use the IA-64 C++ ABI and *instantiate* it
appropriately. IA-64 is the Itanium processor architecture. So this
document is also known as the *Itanium C++ ABI*. Many platforms use it
today, not only Itanium. "Instantiate" means: take the generic rules of
that document and fill in the x86-64 values. For example, the sizes and
alignments of the basic types come from this ABI (see the chapter about
low-level system information).

The current draft of that ABI is available at:

http://mentorembedded.github.io/cxx-abi/

> **Note:** The Itanium C++ ABI document has moved over time. Today it is
> maintained at https://itanium-cxx-abi.github.io/cxx-abi/ on GitHub.

The Itanium C++ ABI answers all C++-specific questions, so this document
does not repeat them. The main topics are:

- *Name mangling*: how the compiler encodes C++ names into plain symbol
  names for the linker.
- Class layout: where fields, base classes, and the hidden vtable pointer
  go inside an object. This includes the rules for empty classes.
- *Vtables* (virtual function tables): tables of pointers that make
  virtual function calls work.
- The representation of pointers to members.
- Run-time type information (RTTI), constructors, destructors, exception
  handling, and template instantiation.

**Example:** Why does C++ need mangled names? In C, a function name maps
directly to a symbol name. The C function `int f(int)` gets the symbol
`f`. But C++ allows *overloading*: two functions with the same name and
different parameter types. The linker only sees symbol names, and every
symbol must be unique. So the C++ compiler *mangles* each name. It
encodes the namespace, the class, and the parameter types into the symbol
name:

**Table: Some mangled names under the Itanium C++ ABI**

| C++ declaration | Mangled symbol |
|---|---|
| `int f(int)` | `_Z1fi` |
| `int f(double)` | `_Z1fd` |
| `void ns::g(char*)` | `_ZN2ns1gEPc` |
| `int A::h() const` | `_ZNK1A1hEv` |

You can read `_Z1fi` like this: `_Z` starts every mangled name. `1f`
means a name of length 1, which is "f". The final `i` means one parameter
of type `int`. The return type of a normal function is not encoded. You
can decode a mangled name with the `c++filt` tool:

```
$ c++filt _Z1fi
f(int)
```

If you declare a function as `extern "C"`, the compiler does not mangle
its name. Then `int f(int)` gets the plain symbol `f`, like in C. But you
cannot overload such a function, because the symbol name must stay
unique.

**Example:** A small class scenario. Take this class:

```cpp
class Shape {
public:
    virtual ~Shape();
    virtual double area() const;
    int id;
};
```

The class has virtual functions, so the Itanium C++ ABI gives it a
vtable. On x86-64 with the LP64 model, the object layout is:

```
offset 0:   vptr  (8 bytes, hidden pointer to the vtable of Shape)
offset 8:   id    (4 bytes)
offset 12:  4 bytes of padding
total size: 16 bytes, alignment: 8 bytes
```

Every object of `Shape` starts with the hidden *vptr*. A virtual call
like `s->area()` loads the vptr, loads the function pointer from the
vtable, and calls it. The mangled name of `Shape::area() const` is
`_ZNK5Shape4areaEv`.

Pointers to members of `Shape` also follow the Itanium rules. A pointer
to a data member, such as `int Shape::*`, is one 8-byte value. It holds
the byte offset of the member inside the object. A pointer to a member
function, such as `double (Shape::*)() const`, is a pair of two 8-byte
values, so 16 bytes in total. The pair stores the function (or a vtable
offset for virtual functions) and an adjustment for the `this` pointer.

**Example:** Empty classes. An object of an empty class still needs a
unique address, so its size is 1 byte, not 0:

```cpp
struct Empty {};                       // sizeof(Empty)   == 1
struct Derived : Empty { int x; };     // sizeof(Derived) == 4
```

But when an empty class is used as a base class, it can take no space
inside the derived object. This rule is called the *empty base
optimization*. So `Derived` above has size 4, not 5 or 8.

## Fortran

A formal Fortran ABI does not exist. Most Fortran compilers are built for
very special high performance computing programs. So Fortran compilers use
different calling conventions and memory layouts, each tuned for its own
purpose. For example, a Fortran program that runs on a distributed memory
machine needs a different format for array descriptors than a program that
runs on a shared memory multiprocessor machine. An *array descriptor* (also
called a dope vector, or a fat pointer) is a small record that describes an
array: its address, its size, and its bounds. Because of these differences,
a strict, normative ABI for Fortran is not wanted.

Still, different Fortran compilers should be able to work together. Fortran
code should also work together with other languages. For this reason, this
section gives some guidelines for data type representation and argument
passing. The guidelines come from the GNU Fortran 77 (G77) compiler. The GNU
Fortran 95 (`gfortran`) compiler also follows them, when you use only
Fortran 77 features. Other Fortran compilers for AMD64 may use different
conventions. So compatibility with them is not guaranteed.

When this text uses the term *Fortran procedure*, it means both Fortran
`FUNCTION` and `SUBROUTINE` subprograms. It also means alternate `ENTRY`
points, unless the text says otherwise. An *alternate `ENTRY` point* is an
extra entry into a subprogram, declared with the `ENTRY` statement.

Everything that this ABI does not define in a clear way is left to the
implementation. In simple words: if a rule is not written here, each
compiler may choose its own rule.

### Names

External names in Fortran are names that all subprograms can see at link
time. This includes the names of `COMMON` blocks and the names of Fortran
procedures. These names could clash with names in linked-in libraries. To
avoid this, the compiler must change (mangle) all external names. The
mangled external names could then clash with local names. To avoid that,
the compiler must also mangle all local names. The mangling scheme is
simple:

- A name with no underscore in it gets *one* underscore appended at the
  end.
- An external name with one or more underscores in it (at any place) gets
  *two* underscores appended at the end.
- All external names are mapped to lower case. This follows the
  traditional UNIX model for Fortran compilers.

> **Note:** The rule about two underscores exists for a historical reason.
> It keeps the names compatible with the f2c convention. (f2c is an old
> translator from Fortran to C.)

The next table shows some examples of this mapping.

**Table: Example mapping of names**

| Fortran external name | Linker name |
|---|---|
| `FOO` | `foo_` |
| `foo` | `foo_` |
| `Foo` | `foo_` |
| `foo_` | `foo___` |
| `f_oo` | `f_oo__` |

The entry point of the main program unit is called `MAIN__`. The symbol
name for the blank common block is `__BLNK__`. The external name of the
unnamed `BLOCK DATA` routine is `__BLOCK_DATA__`.

**Example:** A Fortran subroutine and the C code that can call it. The
Fortran source is:

```fortran
      SUBROUTINE ADDVEC (A, B, N)
      INTEGER N
      REAL A(N), B(N)
      INTEGER I
      DO 10 I = 1, N
         A(I) = A(I) + B(I)
10    CONTINUE
      END
```

The name `ADDVEC` has no underscore. So the linker name is the lower-case
name plus one underscore: `addvec_`. All arguments are passed by
reference, so the matching C prototype uses pointers:

```c
void addvec_(float *a, float *b, int *n);
```

A C program calls it like this:

```c
float a[3] = { 1.0f, 2.0f, 3.0f };
float b[3] = { 4.0f, 5.0f, 6.0f };
int n = 3;
addvec_(a, b, &n);
```

Under the C ABI, the pointer to `a` goes in `rdi`, the pointer to `b`
goes in `rsi`, and the pointer to `n` goes in `rdx`.

### Representation of Fortran Types

For historical reasons, GNU Fortran 77 maps Fortran programs to the C ABI.
So the best way to explain the data representation is to show how G77 maps
Fortran types to C types on AMD64. The next table shows this mapping. The
notation "`TYPE*N`" means that a variable or aggregate member of type
`TYPE` takes `N` bytes of storage.

> **Note:** G77 ships a header file called `g2c.h`. It contains the
> equivalent C type definitions for all supported Fortran scalar types.

**Table: Mapping of Fortran to C types**

| Fortran | Data kind | Equivalent C type |
|---|---|---|
| `INTEGER*4` | Default integer | `signed int` |
| `INTEGER*8` | Double precision integer | `signed long` |
| `REAL*4` | Single precision FP number | `float` |
| `REAL*8` | Double precision FP number | `double` |
| `COMPLEX*4` | Single precision complex FP number | `complex float` |
| `COMPLEX*8` | Double precision complex FP number | `complex double` |
| `LOGICAL` | Boolean logical type | `signed int` |
| `CHARACTER` | Text string | `char[] + length` |

The type `LOGICAL` has two values: `.TRUE.` is stored as 1, and `.FALSE.`
is stored as 0.

A data object with a `CHARACTER` type is stored as an array of C `char`
elements. The array is not guaranteed to end with a `\0` byte. A separate
length counter goes with it. This counter makes it possible to tell apart
a single `CHARACTER` object with a length parameter and an aggregate of
`CHARACTER` objects, which may also have a length parameter.

> **Note:** This rule also applies to sub-strings.

The layout of other aggregate types is implementation defined. GNU Fortran
puts all arrays in contiguous memory, in column-major order. In
column-major order, the elements of one column sit next to each other in
memory. GNU Fortran 95 builds an equivalent C struct for derived types and
does not reorder the fields. Other compilers may use other layouts if they
need to. The representation and use of Fortran 90/95 array descriptors is
implementation defined. Note that array indices start at 1 by default.

Fortran 90/95 allow different kinds of each basic type. You select a kind
with the `kind` type parameter of a type. The values of kind type
parameters are implementation defined.

The layout of the commonly used Cray pointers is implementation defined.

**Example:** Sizes and alignments that follow from the C mapping above.

| Fortran type | C type | Size (bytes) | Alignment (bytes) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `INTEGER*4` | `signed int` | 4 | 4 |
| `INTEGER*8` | `signed long` | 8 | 8 |
| `REAL*4` | `float` | 4 | 4 |
| `REAL*8` | `double` | 8 | 8 |
| `COMPLEX*4` | `complex float` | 8 | 4 |
| `COMPLEX*8` | `complex double` | 16 | 8 |
| `LOGICAL` | `signed int` | 4 | 4 |

### Argument Passing

For every Fortran 77 procedure you can build an equivalent C prototype.
Once you know this C prototype, apply the normal C ABI rules to it. The
result tells you how the arguments reach the Fortran procedure.

G77 passes all user-defined formal arguments of a procedure by reference.
In simple words: the caller passes the address, not the value. The actual
arguments are pointers to the memory location of a variable, an array, an
array element, a temporary location that holds the result of an
expression, or a temporary or permanent location that holds the value of a
constant (compare the g77 manual). The compiler may also create artificial
extra arguments. Those may go by value or by reference, because they are
by nature compiler specific, and so implementation specific.

A data object with a `CHARACTER` type is passed as two things: a pointer
to the character string, and its length. So each `CHARACTER` formal
argument of a Fortran procedure becomes two actual arguments in the
equivalent C prototype:

- The first argument keeps the position of the formal argument in the
  Fortran argument list. It is a pointer to the array of characters that
  form the string. The caller passes this pointer.
- The second argument is appended at the end of the user-specified formal
  argument list. It has the default integer type. Its value is the length
  of the character array from the first argument. This length is passed by
  value.

When an argument list has more than one `CHARACTER` argument, the length
arguments are appended in the same order as the original arguments. All of
this also applies to sub-strings.

**Example:** A subroutine with a `CHARACTER*(*)` argument. The declaration
`CHARACTER*(*)` means a character string whose length comes from the
caller.

```fortran
      SUBROUTINE PRTMSG (MSG, CODE)
      CHARACTER*(*) MSG
      INTEGER CODE
      WRITE (*,*) MSG, CODE
      END
```

The equivalent C prototype has a hidden length parameter at the end:

```c
void prtmsg_(char *msg, int *code, int msg_len);
```

A C caller writes:

```c
prtmsg_("overflow", &code, 8);
```

The string pointer goes in `rdi`. The pointer to `code` goes in `rsi`.
The hidden length 8 goes by value in `edx`. Note the order: the hidden
length comes after *all* user arguments, not directly after the string.
With two `CHARACTER` arguments, for example
`SUBROUTINE TWO (S1, N, S2)`, the prototype becomes:

```c
void two_(char *s1, int *n, char *s2, int s1_len, int s2_len);
```

This ABI does not define the passing of optional arguments. Optional
arguments exist only in Fortran 90/95, and their passing is implementation
defined.

This ABI does not define array functions (functions that return arrays).
They exist only in Fortran 90/95, and they need a definition of array
descriptors.

Note the following about Fortran 90/95 procedure arguments with the
`INTENT(IN)` attribute. If the procedure must link with code written in
Fortran 77, these arguments should also be passed by reference. Fortran 77
does not and cannot support the `INTENT` attribute, because it has no
concept of explicit interfaces. So it is not possible to declare the
callee's arguments as `INTENT(IN)`. A Fortran 77 compiler must assume that
all procedure arguments are `INTENT(INOUT)` in the Fortran 90/95 sense.

### Functions

The calling of statement functions is implementation defined. A statement
function is a small function defined in one line inside a subprogram. It
is visible only locally, so the compiler is free to use any calling
convention for it.

Because a Fortran `FUNCTION` maps to an equivalent C prototype, its return
value follows the C ABI. From the type mapping table above, this gives:

- `INTEGER*4` and `LOGICAL` results come back like a C `signed int`, in
  `eax`.
- `INTEGER*8` results come back like a C `signed long`, in `rax`.
- `REAL*4` and `REAL*8` results come back like C `float` and `double`, in
  `xmm0`.
- `COMPLEX*4` results come back like a C `complex float`. Both parts are
  packed together in `xmm0`.
- `COMPLEX*8` results come back like a C `complex double`. The real part
  is in `xmm0` and the imaginary part is in `xmm1`.

**Example:** A function that returns a `COMPLEX*8` value.

```fortran
      FUNCTION CMUL (A, B)
      COMPLEX*8 CMUL, A, B
      CMUL = A * B
      END
```

The equivalent C prototype is:

```c
complex double cmul_(complex double *a, complex double *b);
```

The pointers to `a` and `b` arrive in `rdi` and `rsi`. After the call,
the real part of the result is in `xmm0` and the imaginary part is in
`xmm1`, as the C ABI demands for `complex double`.

> **Note:** This ABI does not define how a function with a `CHARACTER`
> result returns its value. G77, from which these guidelines come, treats
> such a function like a subroutine. It passes a hidden pointer to the
> result buffer and a hidden length as extra leading arguments. This is
> implementation behavior, not an ABI rule.

A subroutine can have alternate returns. For example, `SUBROUTINE X(*,*)`
is called as `CALL X(*10,*20)`. Each `*` in the argument list stands for a
statement label in the caller. Such a subroutine is implemented as a
function that returns an `INTEGER` of the default kind. The value of this
returned integer is the integer given in the `RETURN` statement of the
subroutine. For a `RETURN` statement without an argument, the value is 0.
The caller must then jump to the matching alternate return label. The
actual alternate-return arguments are left out of the calling sequence.

> **Note:** The integer in the `RETURN` statement gives the position of an
> alternate return in the formal argument list of the subroutine.

An example:

```fortran
       SUBROUTINE SHOW_ALTERNATE_RETURN (N)
          INTEGER N
          CALL ALTERNATE_RETURN_EXAMPLE (N, *10, *20, *30)
          WRITE (*,*) 'OK - Normal Return'
          RETURN
10        WRITE (*,*) '1st alternate return'
          RETURN
20        WRITE (*,*) '2nd alternate return'
          RETURN
30        WRITE (*,*) '3rd alternate return'
          RETURN
       END

       SUBROUTINE ALTERNATE_RETURN_EXAMPLE (N, *, *, *)
          INTEGER N
          IF (N .EQ. 0 ) RETURN     ! Implicit "RETURN 0"
          IF ( N .EQ. 1 ) RETURN 1
          IF ( N .EQ. 2 ) RETURN 2
          RETURN 3
       END
```

Here the compiler implements `SUBROUTINE ALTERNATE_RETURN_EXAMPLE` as a
function that returns an `INTEGER*4`. The value is 0 if N is 0, 1 if N is
1, 2 if N is 2, and 3 for all other values of N. The caller uses this
return value as if the call were replaced by this sequence (this is
pseudo-code, not valid Fortran):

```fortran
          INTEGER X
          X = CALL ALTERNATE_RETURN_EXAMPLE (N)
          GOTO (10, 20, 30), X
```

The total effect is this: after the call, `rax` contains the index of the
label that the code returns to. The index starts at 1. A value of 0 means
a normal return.

**Example:** The code that the caller can generate after such a call, in
Intel syntax. The return value arrives in `eax`.

```asm
        call    alternate_return_example_
        cmp     eax, 1
        je      .Llab10          ; RETURN 1 -> label 10
        cmp     eax, 2
        je      .Llab20          ; RETURN 2 -> label 20
        cmp     eax, 3
        je      .Llab30          ; RETURN 3 -> label 30
        ; eax == 0: normal return, fall through
```

Alternate `ENTRY` points of a `SUBROUTINE` or `FUNCTION` must be treated
as separate subprograms. The Fortran standard demands this. In other
words: pass arguments to an alternate `ENTRY` as if that `ENTRY` were a
separate `SUBROUTINE` or `FUNCTION`. If a `FUNCTION` has alternate `ENTRY`
points, each of them must return its result as if it were a separate
`FUNCTION` with the result type of that `ENTRY`. The external naming of
alternate `ENTRY` points follows the rules in the section "Names" above.

### COMMON blocks

First, take the case without any `EQUIVALENCE` declaration that involves
variables in `COMMON` blocks. In this case the layout of a `COMMON` block
is exactly the same as the layout of the equivalent C structure. You get
this structure by replacing each variable type with its C type from the
section "Representation of Fortran Types". The alignment requirements are
also the same as for the C structure.

**Example:** A `COMMON` block and the C struct with the same layout.

```fortran
      COMMON /PHYS/ X, Y, N, M
      REAL*8 X, Y
      INTEGER*4 N, M
```

The equivalent C structure is:

```c
struct phys {
    double x;   /* offset  0 */
    double y;   /* offset  8 */
    int    n;   /* offset 16 */
    int    m;   /* offset 20 */
};                /* size 24, alignment 8 */
```

The block name `PHYS` has no underscore. So the linker symbol for the
block is `phys_`, following the name mangling rules. C code can access
the same memory like this:

```c
extern struct phys phys_;
phys_.n = 42;
```

An `EQUIVALENCE` statement makes two or more variables share the same
memory. This ABI defines the layout in the presence of `EQUIVALENCE`
statements only in some cases:

- The layout of the `COMMON` block must not change if you ignore the
  `EQUIVALENCE`. Among other things, this means the following points.
- If two arrays are equivalenced, the larger array must be the one named
  in the `COMMON` block. There must be complete inclusion. In particular,
  the other array may not extend the size of the equivalenced segment. It
  may also not change the alignment requirement.
- If an array element and a scalar are equivalenced, the array must be
  the one named in the `COMMON` block. The array must not be smaller than
  the scalar. The type of the scalar must not need a bigger alignment
  than the array.
- If two scalars are equivalenced, they must have the same size and the
  same alignment requirements.

Other cases are implementation defined.

The Fortran standard allows the blank `COMMON` block to have different
sizes in different subprograms. Because of this, it may be impossible to
know if the block is small enough to fit in the `.bss` section. So when
you compile for the medium or large code model, always put the blank
`COMMON` block in the `.lbss` section.

### Intrinsics

This section lists the set of intrinsics that a conforming compiler must
support at minimum. An *intrinsic* is a procedure that the compiler knows
and provides itself. The intrinsics are grouped by origin. They follow the
regular calling and naming conventions.

The signature of an intrinsic uses the syntax
*return-type(argtype1, argtype2, ...)*. Each type can be one of the
following characters: **V** (as in void) marks a `SUBROUTINE`, **L** a
`LOGICAL`, **I** an `INTEGER`, **R** a `REAL`, and **C** a `CHARACTER`.
So `I(R,L)` marks a `FUNCTION` that returns an `INTEGER` and takes a
`REAL` and a `LOGICAL`. If an argument is an array, a trailing number
shows this. For example, **I13** is an `INTEGER` array with 13 elements.
If a `CHARACTER` argument or return value has a fixed length, an asterisk
and a trailing number show this. For example, **C*16** is a
`CHARACTER(len=16)`. If a `CHARACTER` argument of any length must be
passed, the trailing number becomes `N`. For example, **C*N**.

**Table: Mil intrinsics**

| Name | Signature | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `BTest` | L(I,I) | Test bit |
| `IAnd` | I(I,I) | Boolean AND |
| `IOr` | I(I,I) | Boolean OR |
| `IEOr` | I(I,I) | Boolean XOR |
| `Not` | I(I) | Boolean NOT |
| `IBClr` | I(I,I) | Clear a bit |
| `IBits` | I(I,I,I) | Extract a bit subfield of a variable |
| `IBSet` | I(I,I) | Set a bit |
| `IShft` | I(I,I) | Logical bit shift |
| `IShftC` | I(I,I,I) | Circular bit shift |
| `MvBits` | V(I,I,I,I,I) | Move a bit field |

- `BTest (I, Pos)`
  Returns `.TRUE.` if bit `Pos` in `I` is set. Returns `.FALSE.`
  otherwise.
- `IAnd (I, J)`
  Returns the value that results from a boolean AND on each pair of bits
  in `I` and `J`.
- `IOr (I, J)`
  Returns the value that results from a boolean OR on each pair of bits
  in `I` and `J`.
- `IEOr (I, J)`
  Returns the value that results from a boolean XOR on each pair of bits
  in `I` and `J`.
- `Not (I)`
  Returns the value that results from a boolean NOT on each bit in `I`.
- `IBClr (I, Pos)`
  Returns the value of `I` with bit `Pos` cleared (set to zero).
- `IBits (I, Pos, Len)`
  Extracts a subfield from `I`. The subfield starts at bit position `Pos`
  and has a length of `Len` bits, counted towards the most significant
  bit. The result is right-justified, and the remaining bits are zeroed.
- `IBSet (I, Pos)`
  Returns the value of `I` with the bit in position `Pos` set to one.
- `IShft (I, Shift)`
  All bits of `I` are shifted `Shift` places. `Shift.GT.0` means a left
  shift. `Shift.EQ.0` means no shift. `Shift.LT.0` means a right shift.
  Bits shifted out at the least significant end (when shifting right) or
  at the most significant end (when shifting left) are lost. Bits shifted
  in at the opposite end are not set (they are zero).
- `IShftC (I, Shift, Size)`
  The rightmost `Size` bits of the argument `I` are shifted in a circle
  by `Shift` places. The unshifted bits of the result are the same as the
  unshifted bits of `I`.
- `MvBits (From, FromPos, Len, To, ToPos)`
  Moves `Len` bits of `From`, from bit positions `FromPos` through
  `FromPos+Len-1`, to bit positions `ToPos` through `ToPos+Len-1` of
  `To`. The bit portions of `To` that the move does not touch stay
  unchanged.

**Example:** `IBits(I, Pos, Len)` maps to a small piece of C or assembly.
For `IBits(X, 4, 3)` with `X = 0xF4` (binary `11110100`), the function
takes 3 bits starting at bit 4: binary `111`. The result is 7. In C this
is `(x >> 4) & ((1 << 3) - 1)`. In Intel syntax assembly, with `X` in
`edi`:

```asm
        mov     eax, edi
        shr     eax, 4          ; drop the low 4 bits
        and     eax, 7          ; keep 3 bits
```

**Table: F77 intrinsics**

| Name | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Abs | Absolute value |
| ACos | Arc cosine |
| AInt | Truncate to whole number |
| ANInt | Round to nearest whole number |
| ASin | Arc sine |
| ATan | Arc Tangent |
| ATan2 | Arc Tangent |
| Char | Character from code |
| Cmplx | Construct `COMPLEX(KIND=1)` value |
| Conjg | Complex conjugate |
| Cos | Cosine |
| CosH | Hyperbolic cosine |
| Dble | Convert to double precision |
| DiM | Difference magnitude (non-negative subtract) |
| DProd | Double-precision product |
| Exp | Exponential |
| IChar | Code for character |
| Index | Locate a `CHARACTER` substring |
| Int | Convert to `INTEGER` value truncated to whole number |
| Len | Length of character entity |
| LGe | Lexically greater than or equal |
| LGt | Lexically greater than |
| LLe | Lexically less than or equal |
| LLt | Lexically less than |
| Log | Natural logarithm |
| Log10 | Common logarithm |
| Max | Maximum value |
| Min | Minimum value |
| Mod | Remainder |
| NInt | Convert to `INTEGER` value rounded to nearest whole number |
| Real | Convert value to type `REAL(KIND=1)` |
| Sin | Sine |
| SinH | Hyperbolic sine |
| SqRt | Square root |
| Tan | Tangent |
| TanH | Hyperbolic tangent |

For the signature and definition of the F77 intrinsics in the table
above, see the Fortran 77 language standard. These intrinsics can have a
prefix, as the standard allows. So the table is not exhaustive.

**Table: F90 intrinsics**

| Name | Meaning |
|---|---|
| AChar | ASCII character from code |
| Bit_Size | Number of bits in arguments type |
| CPU_Time | Get current CPU time |
| IAChar | ASCII code for character |
| Len_Trim | Get last non-blank character in string |
| System_Clock | Get current system clock value |

For the signature and definition of the F90 intrinsics in the table
above, see the Fortran 90 language standard.

**Table: Math intrinsics**

| Name | Signature | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `BesJ0` | R(R) | Bessel function |
| `BesJ1` | R(R) | Bessel function |
| `BesJN` | R(I,R) | Bessel function |
| `BesY0` | R(R) | Bessel function |
| `BesY1` | R(R) | Bessel function |
| `BesYN` | R(I,R) | Bessel function |
| `ErF` | R(R) | Error function |
| `ErFC` | R(R) | Complementary error function |
| `IRand` | I(I) | Random number |
| `Rand` | R(I) | Random number |
| `SRand` | V(I) | Random seed |

- `BesJ0 (X)`
  Calculates the Bessel function of the first kind of order 0 of X.
  Returns a `REAL` of the same kind as `X`.
- `BesJ1 (X)`
  Calculates the Bessel function of the first kind of order 1 of X.
  Returns a `REAL` of the same kind as `X`.
- `BesJN (N, X)`
  Calculates the Bessel function of the first kind of order N of X.
  Returns a `REAL` of the same kind as `X`.
- `BesY0 (X)`
  Calculates the Bessel function of the second kind of order 0 of X.
  Returns a `REAL` of the same kind as `X`.
- `BesY1 (X)`
  Calculates the Bessel function of the second kind of order 1 of X.
  Returns a `REAL` of the same kind as `X`.
- `BesYN (N, X)`
  Calculates the Bessel function of the second kind of order N of X.
  Returns a `REAL` of the same kind as `X`.
- `ErF (X)`
  Calculates the error function of X. Returns a `REAL` of the same kind
  as `X`.
- `ErFC (X)`
  Calculates the complementary error function of X, that is
  `1 - ERF(X)`. Returns a `REAL` of the same kind as `X`.
- `IRand (Flag)`
  `Flag` is optional. Returns a uniform quasi-random number up to a
  system-dependent limit. If `Flag .EQ. 0`, or if `Flag` is not passed,
  the next number in the sequence is returned. If `Flag .EQ. 1`, the
  generator is restarted. If `Flag` has any other value, the generator is
  restarted with the value of `Flag` as the new seed.
- `Rand (Flag)`
  `Flag` is optional. Returns a uniform quasi-random number between 0
  and 1. If `Flag .EQ. 0`, or if `Flag` is not passed, the next number in
  the sequence is returned. If `Flag .EQ. 1`, the generator is restarted.
  If `Flag` has any other value, the generator is restarted with the
  value of `Flag` as the new seed.
- `SRand (Seed)`
  Reinitializes the random number generator for `IRand` and `Rand` with
  the seed in `Seed`.

**Table: Unix intrinsics**

| Name | Signature | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `Abort` | V() | Abort the program |
| `Access` | I(C,C) | Check file accessibility |
| `DTime` | V(R2,R) | Get elapsed time since last call |
| `ETime` | V(R2,R) | Get elapsed time for process |
| `Flush` | V(I) | Flush buffered output |
| `FNum` | I(I) | Get file descriptor from Fortran unit number |
| `FStat` | V(I,I13,I) | Get file information |
| `GError` | V(C*N) | Get error message for last error |
| `GetArg` | V(I,C*N) | Obtain command-line argument |
| `GetCWD` | V(C*N,I) | Get current working directory |
| `GetEnv` | V(C*N,C*N) | Get environment variable |
| `GetGId` | I() | Get process group ID |
| `GetPId` | I() | Get process ID |
| `GetUId` | I() | Get process user ID |
| `GetLog` | V(C*N) | Get login name |
| `HostNm` | V(C*N,I) | Get host name |
| `IArgC` | I() | Obtain count of command-line arguments |
| `IDate` | V(I3) | Get local date info |
| `IErrNo` | I() | Get error number for last error |
| `ITime` | V(I3) | Get local time of day |
| `LStat` | V(C*N,I13,I) | Get file information |
| `PError` | V(C*N) | Print error message for last error |
| `Rename` | V(C*N,C*N,I) | Rename file |
| `Sleep` | V(I) | Sleep for a specified time |
| `System` | V(C*N,I) | Invoke shell (system) command |

- `Abort ()`
  Prints a message and may cause a core dump.
- `Access (Name, Mode)`
  Checks the file `Name` for accessibility in the mode given by `Mode`.
  Returns 0 if the file is accessible in that mode. Otherwise it returns
  an error code. `Name` must be a `NULL`-terminated string of `CHARACTER`
  (that is, a C-style string). Trailing blanks in `Name` are ignored.
  `Mode` must be a concatenation of any of the following characters:
  **r** means test for read permission, **w** means test for write
  permission, **x** means test for execute or search permission, and a
  space means test that the file exists.
- `DTime (TArray, Result)`
  On the first call, it returns the number of seconds of runtime since
  the start of the program in `Result`. It returns the user component of
  this runtime in `TArray(1)` and the system time in `TArray(2)`. Later
  calls return values collected since the previous call.
- `ETime (TArray, Result)`
  Returns the number of seconds of runtime since the start of the program
  in `Result`. It returns the user component of this runtime in
  `TArray(1)` and the system time in `TArray(2)`. Later calls return
  values collected since the previous call.
- `Flush (Unit)`
  Flushes the Fortran I/O unit with the ID `Unit`. The unit must be open
  for output. If you leave out the optional `Unit` argument, all open
  units are flushed.
- `FNum (Unit)`
  Returns the UNIX(tm) file descriptor number that belongs to the Fortran
  I/O unit `Unit`. The unit must be open.
- `FStat (Unit, SArray, Status)`
  Gets data about the file open on Fortran I/O unit `Unit` and puts it in
  the array `SArray`. The values in this array are as follows:
  1. Device ID
  2. Inode number
  3. File mode
  4. Number of links
  5. Owner's UID
  6. Owner's GID
  7. ID of device containing directory entry for file
  8. File size (bytes)
  9. Last access time
  10. Last modification time
  11. Last file status change time
  12. Preferred I/O block size (-1 if not available)
  13. Number of blocks allocated (-1 if not available)

  If an element is not available, or not relevant on the host system, it
  is returned as 0, except where the list above says otherwise. If you
  supply the optional `Status` argument, it holds 0 on success or a
  nonzero error code on return.
- `GError (Message)`
  Returns the system error message for the last system error (`errno` in
  C). The message is returned in `Message`. If `Message` is longer than
  the error message, it is padded with blanks after the message. If
  `Message` is not long enough to hold the error message, the error
  message is truncated to the length of `Message`.
- `GetArg (Pos, Value)`
  Returns in `Value` the command-line argument in position `Pos`. If
  there are fewer than `Pos` command-line arguments, `Value` is filled
  with blanks. If `Pos` is 0, the name of the program is returned. If
  `Value` is longer than the command-line argument, it is padded with
  blanks after the argument. If `Value` is not long enough to hold the
  command-line argument, the argument is truncated to the length of
  `Value`.
- `GetCWD (Name, Status)`
  Returns in `Name` the current working directory. If you supply the
  optional `Status` argument, it holds 0 on success or a nonzero error
  code on return.
- `GetEnv (Name, Value)`
  Returns in `Value` the environment variable named by `Name`. If `Name`
  has not been set, `Value` is filled with blanks. A `null` character
  marks the end of the name in `Name`. Trailing blanks in `Name` are
  ignored. If `Value` is longer than the environment variable, it is
  padded with blanks after the variable. If `Value` is not long enough to
  hold the environment variable, the variable is truncated to the length
  of `Value`.
- `GetGId ()`
  Returns the group ID for the current process.
- `GetPId ()`
  Returns the process ID for the current process.
- `GetUId ()`
  Returns the user ID for the current process.
- `GetLog (Login)`
  Returns the login name for the process in `Login`. It returns a blank
  string if the host system does not support `getlogin(3)`. If `Login` is
  longer than the login name, it is padded with blanks after the login
  name. If `Login` is not long enough to hold the login name, the login
  name is truncated to the length of `Login`.
- `HostNm (Name, Status)`
  Returns in `Name` the system's host name. If you supply the optional
  `Status` argument, it holds 0 on success or a nonzero error code on
  return. If `Name` is longer than the host name, it is padded with
  blanks after the host name. If `Name` is not long enough to hold the
  host name, the host name is truncated to the length of `Name`.
- `IArgC ()`
  Returns the number of command-line arguments. The program name itself
  is not included in this number.
- `IDate (TArray)`
  Returns the current local date as day, month, and year in elements 1,
  2, and 3 of `TArray`, in that order. The year has four significant
  digits.
- `IErrNo ()`
  Returns the last system error number (`errno` in C).
- `ITime (TArray)`
  Returns the current local time as hour, minutes, and seconds in
  elements 1, 2, and 3 of `TArray`, in that order.
- `LStat (File, SArray, Status)`
  Gets data about a file named `File` and puts it in the array `SArray`.
  The values in this array are as follows:
  1. Device ID
  2. Inode number
  3. File mode
  4. Number of links
  5. Owner's UID
  6. Owner's GID
  7. ID of device containing directory entry for file
  8. File size (bytes)
  9. Last access time
  10. Last modification time
  11. Last file status change time
  12. Preferred I/O block size (-1 if not available)
  13. Number of blocks allocated (-1 if not available)

  If an element is not available, or not relevant on the host system, it
  is returned as 0, except where the list above says otherwise. If you
  supply the optional `Status` argument, it holds 0 on success or a
  nonzero error code on return.
- `PError (MsgPrefix)`
  Prints a newline-terminated error message for the last system error.
  The message starts with the string `MsgPrefix`, then a colon and a
  space. The error message is printed on the C `stderr` stream.
- `Rename (Path1, Path2, Status)`
  Renames the file named `Path1` to `Path2`. A `null` character marks the
  end of the names. Trailing blanks are ignored. If you supply the
  optional `Status` argument, it holds 0 on success or a nonzero error
  code on return.
- `Sleep (Seconds)`
  Makes the program pause for `Seconds` seconds.
- `System (Command, Status)`
  Passes the string in `Command` to a shell through `system(3)`. If the
  optional argument `Status` is present, it holds the value that
  `system(3)` returned.

# 10. ILP32 Programming Model

People often use the short name "x32" for the x86-64 ILP32 programming
model. This chapter describes that model.

The name ILP32 tells you the data sizes: **I**nt, **L**ong, and
**P**ointer are all **32** bits wide. The normal x86-64 model is called
LP64. In LP64, `long` and pointers are 64 bits, and `int` is 32 bits.
So when you move from LP64 to ILP32, the types `long`, `size_t`,
`ptrdiff_t`, and all pointer types shrink from 8 bytes to 4 bytes.

An x32 program still runs in full 64-bit mode. It can use all 16
general purpose registers, the 64-bit registers, and the SSE registers.
Only the addresses and some type sizes get smaller. Because all
addresses fit in 32 bits, x32 code must use the small code model or the
small position independent code (PIC) model (see the section "Address
Space" below).

**Table: Type sizes in LP64 and ILP32**

| Type | LP64 size | ILP32 size |
|---|---|---|
| `int` | 4 bytes | 4 bytes |
| `long` | 8 bytes | 4 bytes |
| `long long` | 8 bytes | 8 bytes |
| any pointer | 8 bytes | 4 bytes |
| `size_t` | 8 bytes | 4 bytes |
| `ptrdiff_t` | 8 bytes | 4 bytes |

**Example:** This small C program prints the type sizes.

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    printf("sizeof(int)    = %zu\n", sizeof(int));
    printf("sizeof(long)   = %zu\n", sizeof(long));
    printf("sizeof(void *) = %zu\n", sizeof(void *));
    printf("sizeof(size_t) = %zu\n", sizeof(size_t));
    return 0;
}
```

If you build it as a normal LP64 program (`gcc test.c`), it prints:

```
sizeof(int)    = 4
sizeof(long)   = 8
sizeof(void *) = 8
sizeof(size_t) = 8
```

If you build it as an x32 program (`gcc -mx32 test.c`), it prints:

```
sizeof(int)    = 4
sizeof(long)   = 4
sizeof(void *) = 4
sizeof(size_t) = 4
```

**Example:** Why can x32 be faster than LP64? Imagine a program that
holds a large tree with millions of nodes. Each node stores several
pointers. In LP64, every pointer needs 8 bytes. In ILP32, every pointer
needs only 4 bytes. So the same tree needs much less memory. Smaller
data fits better in the processor caches, so the program often runs
faster. At the same time, x32 code still runs in 64-bit mode. So it
keeps all 16 general purpose registers and the fast register-based
calling convention. A classic 32-bit (i386) program has only 8
registers and passes arguments on the stack. x32 gives you the small
pointers of i386 and the many registers of x86-64 at the same time.

## Parameter Passing

x32 uses the same calling convention as LP64 (see the section
"Parameter Passing" in the chapter "Function Calling Sequence"). There
is one extra rule for pointers.

When a value of pointer type is returned or passed in a register, bits
32 to 63 shall be zero. In simple words: a pointer is only 32 bits, but
it travels in a 64-bit register. The upper half of that register must
be zero, not garbage.

**Example:** The function `f` takes a pointer argument. The first
integer or pointer argument goes in `rdi`.

```c
static int v;
void f(int *p);

void caller(void)
{
    f(&v);
}
```

The compiler can load the 32-bit address into `edi`:

```asm
mov edi, OFFSET v         # write to edi, upper 32 bits become zero
call f
```

On x86-64, a write to a 32-bit register always clears bits 32 to 63 of
the full 64-bit register. So this single `mov` already follows the
rule: `rdi` holds the pointer with a zero upper half. The callee can
safely use the full `rdi` to access memory. The same rule applies to
return values: a function that returns a pointer puts it in `eax`, and
bits 32 to 63 of `rax` are zero.

## Address Space

ILP32 binaries reside in the lower 32 bits of the 64-bit virtual
address space. All addresses are 32 bits in size. The binaries should
conform to the small code model or the small position independent code
model (PIC). Both models are described in the section "Code Models" of
the chapter "Object Files".

In simple words: the whole program, with all its code and data, must
fit in the low 4 GB of memory. In the small code model, the linker
keeps all symbols within reach of a 32-bit offset. So the compiler can
use short, fast address forms like `[rip + symbol]`.

**Example:** Imagine an x32 program that allocates a buffer and stores
its address in a global variable. The address that `malloc` returns is
below 4 GB (see the section "Kernel Support"). So the address fits in a
4-byte variable. A load like `mov eax, [rip + buf_p]` reads the full
pointer with one 32-bit access.

## Thread-Local Storage Support

Thread-local storage (TLS) gives each thread its own copy of a
variable. In C you mark such a variable with `__thread`. The ILP32 TLS
support is based on the LP64 TLS implementation, with some
modifications. This section shows the LP64 and ILP32 code sequences
side by side.

Some background terms help to read the tables:

- The `fs` segment register points to the thread control block of the
  current thread. The first word at `fs:[0]` holds the address of that
  block. So `mov eax, fs:[0]` loads the thread pointer.
- `__tls_get_addr` is a runtime function. It returns the address of a
  TLS variable for the current thread.
- The GOT (global offset table) is a table of addresses that the
  dynamic linker fills in at load time.
- Operators like `x@tlsgd`, `x@tlsld`, `x@dtpoff`, `x@gottpoff`, and
  `x@tpoff` create TLS relocations. A relocation is a note for the
  linker: "put the right value here later".
- The `rex` prefix is a one-byte instruction prefix used in 64-bit
  mode. Some sequences below need it only to give the instruction a
  fixed, known length.

There are four TLS access models. General dynamic is the most general:
it works even in a shared library loaded at run time. Local dynamic is
for variables that are local to one module. Initial exec works when the
module is loaded at program start. Local exec is the fastest: it works
only for variables in the main program itself.

### Global Thread-Local Variable

For a global thread-local variable x:

```c
extern __thread int x;
```

#### General Dynamic Model

This sequence loads the address of `x` into `rax`.

**Table: General Dynamic Model Code Sequence**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `.byte 0x66` | 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` |
| 0x01 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x07 | `.word 0x6666` |
| 0x08 | `.word 0x6666` | 0x09 | `rex64` |
| 0x0a | `rex64` | 0x0a | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` |
| 0x0b | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | | |

**Example:** Why does the sequence contain the strange bytes
`.byte 0x66`, `.word 0x6666`, and `rex64`? These are padding prefixes.
They do not change what the instructions do. They only make the whole
sequence a fixed, known size. The linker needs this fixed size: when it
optimizes the code (see the section "TLS Linker Optimization"), it
replaces the whole sequence with a different one of the same size.

In the TLSDESC code sequence, the `lea` instruction with a 32-bit
destination must be encoded with a `rex` prefix, even if the
destination register does not require it. The reason: if the `lea`
encoding has a variable length, the linker cannot tell where the
instruction starts. Then it cannot safely perform the GDesc -> IE/LE
optimization.

**Table: General Dynamic Model Code Sequence with TLSDESC**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rax, [rip + x@tlsdesc]` | 0x00 | `rex lea eax, [rip + x@tlsdesc]` |
| 0x07 | `call [rax + x@tlsdesc]` | 0x07 | `call [eax + x@tlsdesc]` |
| 0x08 | `add eax, fs:[0x0]` | 0x09 | `add eax, fs:[0x0]` |

#### Initial Exec Model

This sequence loads the address of `x` into `rax`. The 32-bit `add`
instruction must be encoded with a `rex` prefix, even if the
destination register does not require it. Otherwise the linker cannot
safely perform the IE -> LE optimization.

**Table: Initial Exec Model Code Sequence**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x09 | `add rax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` | 0x08 | `rex add eax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` |

#### Initial Exec Model, II

This sequence loads the value of `x` into `edi`. The memory operand
`fs:[eax]` cannot be used for ILP32. Its effective address is the base
address of `fs` plus the value of `eax` zero-extended to a 64-bit
result. This is incorrect when `eax` holds a negative value. So the
code below uses the 64-bit register `rax` instead.

**Table: Initial Exec Model Code Sequence, II**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov rax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` | 0x00 | `mov rax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` |
| 0x07 | `mov edi, fs:[rax]` | 0x07 | `mov edi, fs:[rax]` |

### Static Thread-Local Variable

For a static thread-local variable x:

```c
static __thread int x;
```

#### Local Dynamic Model

This sequence loads the address of `x` into `rax`.

**Table: Local Dynamic Model Code Sequence With Lea**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` |
| 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` |
| 0x0c | `lea rax, [rax + x@dtpoff]` | 0x0c | `lea eax, [rax + x@dtpoff]` |

or

**Table: Local Dynamic Model Code Sequence With Add**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` |
| 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` |
| 0x0c | `add rax, OFFSET x@dtpoff` | 0x0c | `add eax, OFFSET x@dtpoff` |

For the code sequence with TLSDESC, the local dynamic model is similar
to the general dynamic model. The same encoding requirement for the
32-bit `lea` instruction also applies here: it must carry a `rex`
prefix so the linker knows its length.

**Table: General Dynamic Model Code Sequence with TLSDESC**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rax, [rip + x@tlsdesc]` | 0x00 | `rex lea eax, [rip + x@tlsdesc]` |
| 0x07 | `call [rax + x@tlsdesc]` | 0x07 | `call [eax + x@tlsdesc]` |
| 0x08 | `add eax, fs:[0x0]` | 0x09 | `add eax, fs:[0x0]` |

#### Local Dynamic Model, II

This sequence loads the value of `x` into `edi`.

**Table: Local Dynamic Model Code Sequence, II**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x09 | `mov edi, [rax + x@dtpoff]` | 0x08 | `mov edi, [rax + x@dtpoff]` |

#### Local Exec Model

This sequence loads the address of `x` into `rax`.

**Table: Local Exec Model Code Sequence With Lea**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x09 | `lea rax, [rax + x@tpoff]` | 0x08 | `lea eax, [rax + x@tpoff]` |

or

**Table: Local Exec Model Code Sequence With Add**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x09 | `add rax, OFFSET x@tpoff` | 0x08 | `add eax, OFFSET x@tpoff` |

#### Local Exec Model, II

This sequence loads the value of `x` into `edi`.

**Table: Local Exec Model Code Sequence, II**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x09 | `mov edi, [rax + x@tpoff]` | 0x08 | `mov edi, [rax + x@tpoff]` |

#### Local Exec Model, III

This sequence loads the value of `x` into `edi` with one instruction.

**Table: Local Exec Model Code Sequence, III**

| LP64 offset | LP64 code | ILP32 offset | ILP32 code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov edi, fs:[x@tpoff]` | 0x00 | `mov edi, fs:[x@tpoff]` |

### TLS Linker Optimization

The linker can replace a slow TLS access sequence with a faster one
when it knows more about the final program. For example, when the
linker builds the main executable, it knows that a general dynamic (GD)
access can become an initial exec (IE) or local exec (LE) access. The
old and the new sequence have the same size, so the linker can rewrite
the bytes in place. The tables below show these ILP32 transitions.

**Example:** Imagine you write a shared library that uses
`extern __thread int x;`. The compiler does not know where `x` will
live, so it emits the general dynamic sequence with a call to
`__tls_get_addr`. Later you link the same object file into a normal
executable, and `x` is defined in that executable. Now the linker knows
the exact thread-local offset of `x`. It rewrites the GD sequence into
the LE sequence. The call to `__tls_get_addr` disappears, and the
access becomes two simple instructions.

#### General Dynamic To Initial Exec

This transition loads the address of `x` into `rax`.

**Table: GD -> IE Code Transition**

| GD offset | GD code | IE offset | IE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x07 | `.word 0x6666` | 0x08 | `add rax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` |
| 0x09 | `rex64` | | |
| 0x0a | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | | |

**Table: GDesc -> IE Code Transition**

| GDesc offset | GDesc code | IE offset | IE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `rex lea eax, [rip + x@tlsdesc]` | 0x00 | `rex mov eax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` |
| 0x07 | `call [eax + x@TLSCALL]` | 0x07 | `nop DWORD PTR [rax]` |

**Table: GD -> LE Code Transition**

| GD offset | GD code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x07 | `.word 0x6666` | 0x08 | `lea eax, [rax + x@tpoff]` |
| 0x09 | `rex64` | | |
| 0x0a | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | | |

**Table: GDesc -> LE Code Transition**

| GDesc offset | GDesc code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `rex lea eax, [rip + x@tlsdesc]` | 0x00 | `rex mov eax, OFFSET x@tpoff` |
| 0x07 | `call [eax + x@TLSCALL]` | 0x07 | `nop DWORD PTR [rax]` |

#### Initial Exec To Local Exec

This transition loads the address of `x` into `rax`.

**Table: IE -> LE Code Transition With Lea**

| IE offset | IE code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x08 | `rex add eax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` | 0x08 | `rex lea eax, [rax + x@tpoff]` |

or

**Table: IE -> LE Code Transition With Add**

| IE offset | IE code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov r12d, fs:[0]` | 0x00 | `mov r12d, fs:[0]` |
| 0x09 | `add r12d, [rip + x@gottpoff]` | 0x09 | `add r12d, OFFSET x@tpoff` |

#### Initial Exec To Local Exec, II

This transition loads the value of `x` into `edi`.

**Table: IE -> LE Code Transition, II**

| IE offset | IE code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `mov rax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` | 0x00 | `mov rax, OFFSET x@tpoff` |
| 0x07 | `mov edi, fs:[rax]` | 0x07 | `mov edi, fs:[rax]` |

#### Local Dynamic To Local Exec

This transition loads the address of `x` into `rax`.

**Table: LD -> LE Code Transition With Lea**

| LD offset | LD code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `nop DWORD PTR [rax + 0x0]` |
| 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | 0x04 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x0c | `lea eax, [rax + x@dtpoff]` | 0x0c | `lea eax, [rax + x@tpoff]` |

or

**Table: LD -> LE Code Transition With Add**

| LD offset | LD code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `nop DWORD PTR [rax + 0x0]` |
| 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | 0x04 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x0c | `add rax, OFFSET x@dtpoff` | 0x0c | `add eax, OFFSET x@tpoff` |

#### Local Dynamic To Local Exec, II

This transition loads the value of `x` into `edi`.

**Table: LD -> LE Code Transition, II**

| LD offset | LD code | LE offset | LE code |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `nop DWORD PTR [rax + 0x0]` |
| 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@plt` | 0x04 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x0c | `mov eax, [rax + x@dtpoff]` | 0x0c | `mov eax, [rax + x@tpoff]` |

## Kernel Support

The kernel should limit the stack and all addresses returned from
system calls to the range between 0x00000000 and 0xffffffff. In simple
words: the kernel must never give an x32 process an address above 4 GB,
because the process stores addresses in 32-bit variables.

> **Note:** On Linux, x32 processes use the `syscall` instruction and
> the normal 64-bit system call convention: the arguments go in `rdi`,
> `rsi`, `rdx`, `r10`, `r8`, and `r9`, and the result comes back in
> `rax`. The kernel recognizes an x32 system call by a special marker
> bit, `__X32_SYSCALL_BIT` (0x40000000), which is added to the system
> call number in `eax`.

**Example:** Imagine an x32 program that calls `mmap` to map a large
file. On a 64-bit kernel, `mmap` could in theory place the mapping
anywhere in the huge 64-bit address space. For an x32 process the
kernel must not do that. It must pick an address below 4 GB. Then the
returned pointer fits in the 4-byte `void *` of the x32 program.

## Coding Examples

Although ILP32 binaries run in the 64-bit mode, not all 64-bit
instructions are supported. This section discusses example code
sequences for fundamental operations which are different from the
64-bit mode.

### Indirect Branch

An indirect branch through memory loads a 64-bit address from the
memory location. So it is not supported in ILP32; an x32 function
pointer is only 4 bytes. Use an indirect branch through a register
instead. First load the 32-bit address from memory into the lower 32
bits of a register. This write automatically zero-extends the upper 32
bits of the register. Then perform the indirect call or jump through
the 64-bit register.

**Table: Indirect Branch**

| LP64 | ILP32 |
|---|---|
| `call rax` | `call rax` |
| `call [rip + func_p]` | `mov eax, [rip + func_p]` then `call rax` |
| `call [func_p]` | `mov eax, [func_p]` then `call rax` |
| `jmp rax` | `jmp rax` |
| `jmp [rip + func_p]` | `mov eax, [rip + func_p]` then `jmp rax` |
| `jmp [func_p]` | `mov eax, [func_p]` then `jmp rax` |

**Example:** This C code calls a function through a function pointer.

```c
void (*func_p)(void);

void run(void)
{
    func_p();
}
```

For LP64, the compiler can call straight through memory, because the
pointer in memory is 8 bytes:

```asm
call [rip + func_p]       # load 64-bit address and call it
```

For ILP32, the pointer in memory is only 4 bytes. So the compiler
splits the call into a load and a register call:

```asm
mov eax, [rip + func_p]   # load 32-bit address, upper bits become zero
call rax                  # indirect call through the 64-bit register
```

The `mov` writes to `eax`, so the processor clears bits 32 to 63 of
`rax`. The `call rax` then jumps to a clean 64-bit address. The same
pattern works for `jmp`.

# 11. Alternate Code Sequences for Security

This chapter shows alternate code sequences. They do the same work as the
normal sequences, but they avoid the Procedure Linkage Table (PLT). Programs
use them to reduce the attack surface. In simple words: they make some
common attack tricks harder.

## Code Sequences without PLT

The *Procedure Linkage Table (PLT)* is a small table of code stubs. A
program uses it to call external functions that are defined in shared
objects. See the section "Procedure Linkage Table" for details. The PLT
supports two features:

- **Lazy symbol resolution.** The address of the function is resolved only
  when the program calls the function the first time at run time.
- **Canonical function address.** The PLT entry of the external function is
  used as the address of that function. In simple words: when the program
  takes a function pointer, the pointer holds the address of the PLT entry.

The first instruction in a PLT entry is an indirect branch. It jumps through
the entry of the external function in the Global Offset Table (GOT). The
*GOT* is a table of addresses that the dynamic linker fills in. See the
section "Global Offset Table" for details. The GOT entry is set up in a
special way: the first call updates it to the address of the real function
body. Because the GOT entry is writable, any address can be written into it
at run time. This is a potential security risk.

**Example:** Imagine a program with a memory bug. The bug lets an attacker
write eight bytes to a chosen address. The attacker writes the address of
harmful code into the writable GOT entry for a common function. The next
time the program calls that function, control jumps to the harmful code
instead. A read-only GOT stops this kind of attack. The sequences in this
chapter make a fully read-only GOT possible.

### Indirect Call via the GOT Slot

For the small and medium code models, a different code sequence can avoid
the PLT.

**Figure: Function call without PLT (small and medium models)**

| C code | Assembly |
|---|---|
| `extern void func (void);` | `.globl func` |
| `func ();` | `call [rip + func@GOTPCREL]` |

The direct branch to the PLT is replaced by an indirect branch through the
GOT slot. This branch is similar to the first instruction in the PLT slot.
The `@GOTPCREL` operator asks for the GOT entry of `func`, addressed
relative to `rip`.

**Figure: Function address without PLT (small and medium models)**

| C code | Assembly |
|---|---|
| `extern void func (void);` | `.globl func` |
| `void* ptr (void)` | `ptr:` |
| `{` | `mov rax, [rip + func@GOTPCREL]` |
| `return func;` | `ret` |
| `}` | |

Here the program does not use the PLT slot as the function address. It
reads the function address from the GOT slot instead.

Sometimes the linker sees that the function is defined locally, in the same
linked file. Then the linker converts the indirect branch through the GOT
slot into a direct branch with a one-byte `nop`-style prefix. It also
converts a load through the GOT slot into a load of an immediate value or
into a `lea` instruction. The compiler marks these instructions with the
relocations `R_X86_64_GOTPCRELX` and `R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX`, so the
linker knows it may rewrite them. See the section "Optimize GOTPCRELX
Relocations" for details.

**Example:** The compiler emits this 6-byte call with an
`R_X86_64_GOTPCRELX` relocation:

```asm
call [rip + func@GOTPCREL]    # indirect call, 6 bytes
```

If `func` ends up local, the linker rewrites it in place as a 5-byte direct
call plus one prefix byte of padding, so the length stays 6 bytes:

```asm
call func                     # direct call with a one-byte prefix
```

In the same way, this load with an `R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX` relocation:

```asm
mov rax, [rip + func@GOTPCREL]
```

becomes a simple address computation:

```asm
lea rax, [rip + func]
```

After the dynamic linker has resolved all symbols, all GOT entries hold the
final symbol addresses. Then the system can make the GOT read-only. From
that point, any write to the GOT is a hard error at once. Because the PLT
is no longer used to call external functions, lazy symbol resolution is
disabled. A function can then only be *interposed* during symbol resolution
at program startup. To interpose a function means to replace it with
another version, for example from a preloaded library. Tools and features
that depend on lazy symbol resolution will not work properly in this mode.
However, there are also a few side benefits:

- **No extra direct branch to a PLT entry.** An indirect branch is 6 bytes
  long. A direct branch is 5 bytes long. So when the program uses an
  indirect branch through the GOT slot to call a local function, the code
  grows by one byte for each call. One PLT slot has 16 bytes. So when the
  program uses indirect branches through the GOT slot to call an external
  function, the code only grows if the program calls that function more
  than 16 times. In simple words: you save one 16-byte PLT slot, and you
  pay one extra byte per call.
- **Custom calling convention.** The program calls the external function
  directly through the GOT slot. It does not invoke the dynamic linker to
  look up the function symbol at the first call. So the caller and the
  callee can pass parameters in a different way than this document
  specifies. This is safe because no dynamic linker code runs between them
  during the call.

### Thread-Local Storage without PLT

Thread-local storage (TLS) gives each thread its own copy of a variable.
The function `__tls_get_addr` is a runtime helper. It returns the address
of a thread-local variable for the current thread. The general dynamic and
local dynamic TLS models call this function.

The TLS code sequences for the general dynamic and local dynamic models can
be updated. The direct call to `__tls_get_addr` through the PLT entry is
replaced with an indirect call to `__tls_get_addr` through the GOT slot.
The figure "`__tls_get_addr` call" shows both forms.

**Figure: `__tls_get_addr` call**

| Direct via PLT | Indirect via GOT |
|---|---|
| `call __tls_get_addr@PLT` | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` |

The direct `call` instruction is 4 bytes long. The indirect `call`
instruction is 5 bytes long. So the alternate sequence must handle the
extra byte properly.

The tables below show byte offsets on the left of each instruction. The
offset is the distance, in bytes, from the start of the sequence. The
`0x66` bytes are operand-size prefixes. Here they do nothing; they only pad
the sequence to a fixed length. `rex64` tells the assembler to emit a REX
prefix byte before the next instruction. *LP64* is the normal 64-bit model,
where pointers are 8 bytes. *ILP32* is the x32 model, where pointers are
4 bytes.

#### General Dynamic Model for Global Variable

The general dynamic model is the most general TLS access model. It works
for a thread-local variable in any module. For this model, one `0x66`
prefix before the `call` instruction is removed. This makes room for the
longer indirect call. For this variable:

```c
extern __thread int x;
```

the following alternate code sequence loads the address of `x` into `rax`
without the PLT. The `@tlsgd` operator asks for the GOT entry that
describes `x` for the general dynamic model.

**Table: General dynamic model code sequence (LP64)**

| Offset | With PLT | Offset | Without PLT |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `.byte 0x66` | 0x00 | `.byte 0x66` |
| 0x01 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x01 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` |
| 0x08 | `.word 0x6666` | 0x08 | `.byte 0x66` |
| 0x0a | `rex64` | 0x09 | `rex64` |
| 0x0b | `call __tls_get_addr@PLT` | 0x0a | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` |

**Table: General dynamic model code sequence (ILP32)**

| Offset | With PLT | Offset | Without PLT |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` |
| 0x07 | `.word 0x6666` | 0x07 | `.byte 0x66` |
| 0x09 | `rex64` | 0x08 | `rex64` |
| 0x0a | `call __tls_get_addr@PLT` | 0x09 | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` |

**Example:** Count the padding in the LP64 table. The old sequence has
three `0x66` padding bytes: one at offset 0x00, and two in the `.word
0x6666` at offset 0x08. The new sequence keeps only two of them, because
the indirect call needs one more byte. Both sequences have the same total
length. This is important: the linker can later rewrite the sequence in
place, byte for byte, without moving any code.

#### Static Thread-Local Variable

The local dynamic model is used for thread-local variables that are local
to one module, for example `static` variables. For this model, the
alternate sequence uses an indirect call instead of a direct call. For this
variable:

```c
static __thread int x;
```

the following alternate code sequence loads the address of the TLS block of
the module into `rax`, without the PLT. This TLS block contains the
variable `x`. The program then adds the offset of `x` inside the block. The
`@tlsld` operator asks for the GOT entry that describes the module for the
local dynamic model.

**Table: Local dynamic model code sequence (LP64)**

| Offset | With PLT | Offset | Without PLT |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` |
| 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@PLT` | 0x07 | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` |

**Table: Local dynamic model code sequence (ILP32)**

| Offset | With PLT | Offset | Without PLT |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` |
| 0x07 | `call __tls_get_addr@PLT` | 0x07 | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` |

#### TLS Linker Optimization

The general dynamic sequence with the indirect call has the same length as
the sequence with the direct call. So the linker only needs to recognize
the new instruction pattern. Then it can convert a general dynamic access
into an initial exec or local exec access, as before.

The *initial exec (IE)* model reads the offset of the variable from the
GOT. The *local exec (LE)* model uses the offset directly, because the
linker already knows it. Both models are faster, because they do not call
`__tls_get_addr` at all.

Two more operators appear below. `@gottpoff` asks for the GOT entry that
holds the offset of `x` from the thread pointer. `@tpoff` is that offset
itself, filled in by the linker. The `fs` segment register points to the
thread control block. The 8 bytes at `fs:[0]` hold the thread pointer. So
`mov rax, fs:[0]` loads the thread pointer into `rax`.

**General dynamic to initial exec.** To load the address of `x` into
`rax`:

**Table: GD -> IE code transition (LP64)**

| Offset | GD | Offset | IE |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `.byte 0x66` | 0x00 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x01 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x09 | `add rax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` |
| 0x09 | `.byte 0x66` | | |
| 0x0a | `rex64` | | |
| 0x0b | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` | | |

**Table: GD -> IE code transition (ILP32)**

| Offset | GD | Offset | IE |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x07 | `.byte 0x66` | 0x08 | `add rax, [rip + x@gottpoff]` |
| 0x08 | `rex64` | | |
| 0x0a | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` | | |

**General dynamic to local exec.** To load the address of `x` into `rax`:

**Table: GD -> LE code transition (LP64)**

| Offset | GD | Offset | LE |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `.byte 0x66` | 0x00 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x01 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x09 | `lea rax, [rax + x@tpoff]` |
| 0x08 | `.byte 0x66` | | |
| 0x09 | `rex64` | | |
| 0x0a | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` | | |

**Table: GD -> LE code transition (ILP32)**

| Offset | GD | Offset | LE |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsgd]` | 0x00 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |
| 0x07 | `.byte 0x66` | 0x08 | `lea rax, [rax + x@tpoff]` |
| 0x08 | `rex64` | | |
| 0x09 | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` | | |

**Local dynamic to local exec.** The indirect call makes the local dynamic
sequence one byte longer. So the replacement code must also grow by one
byte. For LP64, the linker generates four `0x66` prefixes before the `mov`
instruction, instead of three. For ILP32, the linker generates a 5-byte
`nop`, instead of a 4-byte one, before the `mov` instruction. To load the
address of the TLS block of the module, which contains variable `x`, into
`rax` without the PLT:

**Table: LD -> LE code transition (LP64)**

| Offset | LD | Offset | LE |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `.long 0x66666666` |
| 0x07 | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` | 0x04 | `mov rax, fs:[0]` |

**Table: LD -> LE code transition (ILP32)**

| Offset | LD | Offset | LE |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `lea rdi, [rip + x@tlsld]` | 0x00 | `nop WORD PTR [rax + 0x0]` |
| 0x07 | `call [rip + __tls_get_addr@GOTPCREL]` | 0x05 | `mov eax, fs:[0]` |

**Example:** Imagine you first build a shared library. It reads a
thread-local variable `x` from another module, so the compiler emits the
general dynamic sequence. Later you link the same object file into the main
executable, together with the module that defines `x`. Now the linker knows
the final offset of `x`. It rewrites the sequence in place to the local
exec form. The call to `__tls_get_addr` disappears. The access becomes two
simple instructions: load the thread pointer, then add the fixed offset.
The program behaves the same, but runs faster.

# 12. Intel CET Extension

Intel CET (Control-flow Enforcement Technology) is a processor extension.
It protects the control flow of a program. The *control flow* is the path
that the processor takes through the code. The Intel CET Extension
includes:

- **IBT (Indirect Branch Tracking).** Branch protection. It defends
  against Jump Oriented Programming and Call Oriented Programming. With
  IBT, an indirect `jmp` or indirect `call` may only land on a special
  instruction: `endbr64`. This instruction marks a valid branch target. On
  older processors, `endbr64` acts as a `nop`, so the code still runs
  there. If an indirect branch lands anywhere else, the processor raises a
  fault.
- **SHSTK (Shadow Stack).** Return address protection. It defends against
  Return Oriented Programming. The *shadow stack* is a second stack that
  the processor manages. Normal code cannot write to it. Every `call`
  pushes the return address on both stacks. Every `ret` compares the two
  return addresses. If they differ, the processor raises a fault.

**Example:** Here is a story about the problem that the shadow stack
solves. A program has a stack buffer overflow bug. An attacker uses the bug
to overwrite the return address on the normal stack. The attacker points it
at a small piece of existing code that ends in `ret`, then chains many such
pieces. This technique is called Return Oriented Programming (ROP). The
attacker never injects new code, so many older defenses do not trigger.
With SHSTK, the story ends early. The `ret` in the buggy function compares
the changed address on the normal stack with the true copy on the shadow
stack. The addresses differ. The processor raises a control-protection
fault, and the operating system stops the program. The attack fails at the
very first step.

**Example:** This C function:

```c
int add1 (int x)
{
  return x + 1;
}
```

compiled without control-flow protection, looks like this:

```asm
add1:
        lea eax, [rdi + 1]
        ret
```

Compiled with `-fcf-protection`, the compiler adds `endbr64` at the start,
because a function pointer may call `add1` indirectly:

```asm
add1:
        endbr64
        lea eax, [rdi + 1]
        ret
```

The argument `x` arrives in `edi`, and the result returns in `eax`, as the
calling convention requires. Only the `endbr64` marker is new.

## Program Loading

The system must decide, for each process, whether to enable IBT and SHSTK.
