It reads two feature bits from the file: `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT`
and `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK`. A file has a bit set when all of
its code supports that feature.

> **Note:** These bits live in the GNU property note of the file, in the
> `.note.gnu.property` section. The `PT_GNU_PROPERTY` program header
> segment covers this note, so the loader can find it fast. See the section
> "Program Property" for details.

An *interpreter* here means the program interpreter of the executable.
This is normally the dynamic linker, named in the `PT_INTERP` program
header. *Legacy interwork* means: the process mixes new protected code
with old code that has no CET markers, and the system allows this mix.

### Process GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT

On an IBT capable processor, the system should take the following steps:

1. When it loads an executable without an interpreter: enable IBT if
   `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT` is set on the executable.
2. When it loads an executable with an interpreter: enable IBT if
   `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT` is set on the interpreter. The
   interpreter should disable IBT if `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT` is
   not set on the executable, or not set on any shared object loaded
   through the `DT_NEEDED` tag. The `DT_NEEDED` tag lists the shared
   objects that the executable depends on.
3. After IBT is enabled, when the process loads a shared object without
   `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_IBT`:
   1. In single-threaded applications, if legacy interwork is allowed, the
      system should disable IBT.
   2. In multi-threaded applications, or if legacy interwork is not
      allowed, this causes an error.

> **Note:** The legacy code page bitmap is not used. It cannot cover the
> jitted code that legacy JIT engines generate. A *JIT engine* creates
> machine code at run time, so no fixed bitmap can describe its pages.

**Example:** A program `app` and its libraries `libnew.so` both have the
IBT bit set. The dynamic linker also has it. So the process starts with IBT
enabled. Later, the program loads an old plugin `libold.so` at run time,
and this plugin has no IBT bit. If the program still has only one thread,
and the system allows legacy interwork, the system turns IBT off for the
process. If the program already runs several threads, or interwork is not
allowed, loading `libold.so` fails with an error.

### Process GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK

On a SHSTK capable processor, the system should take the following steps:

1. When it loads an executable without an interpreter: enable SHSTK if
   `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK` is set on the executable.
2. When it loads an executable with an interpreter: enable SHSTK if
   `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK` is set on the interpreter. The
   interpreter should disable SHSTK if `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK`
   is not set on the executable, or not set on any shared object loaded
   through the `DT_NEEDED` tag.
3. After SHSTK is enabled, when the process loads a shared object without
   `GNU_PROPERTY_X86_FEATURE_1_SHSTK`:
   1. In single-threaded applications, if legacy interwork is allowed, the
      system should disable SHSTK.
   2. In multi-threaded applications, or if legacy interwork is not
      allowed, this causes an error.

## Dynamic Linking

With IBT, every indirect branch must land on an `endbr64` instruction. The
normal PLT does not start its entries with `endbr64`. So, to support IBT,
the linker should generate an IBT-enabled PLT, shown in the figure "The
IBT-enabled PLT", together with a second PLT, shown in the figure "The
second IBT-enabled PLT".

**Figure: The IBT-enabled PLT (small and medium models)**

```asm
.PLT0: push QWORD PTR [rip + GOT+8]    # GOT[1]
       jmp [rip + GOT+16]              # GOT[2]
       nop DWORD PTR [rax + 0x0]
.PLT1: endbr64                         # 16 bytes from .PLT0
       push index1
       jmp .PLT0
       xchg ax, ax
.PLT2: endbr64                         # 16 bytes from .PLT1
       push index2
       jmp .PLT0
       xchg ax, ax
.PLT3: ...
```

**Figure: The second IBT-enabled PLT (small and medium models)**

```asm
.SPLT1: endbr64
        jmp [rip + name1@GOTPCREL]
        nop WORD PTR [rax + rax*1 + 0x0]
.SPLT2: endbr64
        jmp [rip + name2@GOTPCREL]     # 16 bytes from .SPLT1
        nop WORD PTR [rax + rax*1 + 0x0]
.SPLT3: ...                            # 16 bytes from .SPLT2
```

Each PLT entry is 16 bytes long. The `nop` and `xchg ax, ax` instructions
only pad the entries to that size. The first entry, `.PLT0`, enters the
dynamic linker. `GOT[1]` holds an identifier for this object, and `GOT[2]`
holds the address of the resolver function of the dynamic linker. `.PLT0`
pushes the identifier and jumps to the resolver. The values `index1` and
`index2` are relocation indexes. They tell the resolver which function to
resolve. Every entry that an indirect branch can reach starts with
`endbr64`.

When the IBT-enabled procedure linkage table is used, the initial value of
the global offset table entry for the external function is the address of
the corresponding entry of the second procedure linkage table. In simple
words: the address of the external function, as the program sees it, is
the second PLT entry. This is why each second PLT entry starts with
`endbr64`: a function pointer can call it indirectly.

> **Note:** In the GNU linker, the second PLT is placed in a section named
> `.plt.sec`.

Lazy binding still works with this layout. The linker sets up the GOT slot
of the function so that the first call reaches the lazy-binding code in
the first PLT.

**Example:** Follow the first call to the external function `name1`.

1. The program calls the second PLT entry, `.SPLT1`. The entry starts
   with `endbr64`, so both direct calls and calls through function
   pointers may land here.
2. `.SPLT1` jumps through the GOT slot of `name1`. At program start, this
   slot points to the lazy-binding code at `.PLT1` in the first PLT.
3. The jump is indirect, and `.PLT1` starts with `endbr64`, so the landing
   is allowed. `.PLT1` pushes `index1` and jumps to `.PLT0`.
4. `.PLT0` pushes the object identifier from `GOT[1]` and jumps to the
   resolver through `GOT[2]`. The resolver finds the real address of
   `name1`, writes it into the GOT slot, and jumps to `name1`.
5. Every later call goes through `.SPLT1` and jumps straight to `name1`.
   The lazy-binding code in the first PLT is no longer used for `name1`.

# Appendix A: Linux Conventions

This appendix describes some details that only matter on GNU/Linux
systems and in the Linux kernel. Other operating systems can make
different choices.

## Execution of 32-bit Programs

The AMD64 processors can execute 64-bit AMD64 programs. They can also
execute 32-bit ia32 programs. Here, *ia32* means the older 32-bit Intel
architecture (also called i386 or x86).

The two kinds of programs need two kinds of libraries. Linux places them
in different directories:

- Libraries that follow the 32-bit Intel386 ABI live in the normal
  places. Examples are `/lib`, `/usr/lib`, and `/usr/bin`.
- Libraries that follow the AMD64 ABI use `lib64` subdirectories.
  Examples are `/lib64` and `/usr/lib64`.

Programs are different from libraries. Programs of both ABIs share the
same directories, like `/usr/bin`. In particular, there is no `/bin64`
directory.

**Example:** Imagine a Linux system that has both a 32-bit and a 64-bit
C library. You could see this layout:

```
/lib/libc.so.6        <- 32-bit C library (Intel386 ABI)
/lib64/libc.so.6      <- 64-bit C library (AMD64 ABI)
/usr/bin/prog32       <- a 32-bit program
/usr/bin/prog64       <- a 64-bit program
```

Both programs sit in `/usr/bin`. When you run `prog32`, the system loads
the 32-bit library from `/lib`. When you run `prog64`, the system loads
the 64-bit library from `/lib64`.

## AMD64 Linux Kernel Conventions

This section is informative only. This means it gives helpful facts
about Linux, but it does not add binding ABI rules.

### Calling Conventions

Inside itself, the Linux AMD64 kernel uses the same calling conventions
as user-level applications. See the section "Function Calling Sequence"
for the details of these conventions.

A *system call* is a request from a program to the kernel, for example
"write these bytes to a file". User-level applications that want to make
system calls should use the functions from the C library. The C library
then talks to the kernel. The interface between the C library and the
Linux kernel is the same as for user-level applications, with the
following differences:

1. User-level applications pass integer arguments in this register
   sequence: `rdi`, `rsi`, `rdx`, `rcx`, `r8`, and `r9`. The kernel
   interface uses `rdi`, `rsi`, `rdx`, `r10`, `r8`, and `r9`. So the
   fourth argument moves from `rcx` to `r10`. The reason is simple:
   the `syscall` instruction destroys `rcx` (and `r11`), so `rcx`
   cannot carry an argument into the kernel.
2. A system call is done with the `syscall` instruction. The kernel
   *clobbers* (overwrites) the registers `rcx` and `r11`. It preserves
   all other registers, except `rax`.
3. You must pass the number of the system call in register `rax`.
   Every system call has its own fixed number.
4. System calls are limited to six arguments. No argument is passed
   directly on the stack.
5. When the `syscall` instruction returns, register `rax` holds the
   result of the system call. A value in the range between -4095 and -1
   means an error. The value is `-errno`, so the negative of the error
   code.
6. Only values of class INTEGER or class MEMORY are passed to the
   kernel. See the section "Classification" for these classes. In
   simple words: you can pass integers, pointers, and structures in
   memory, but not floating-point values in `xmm` registers.

**Example:** The `write` system call has number 1 and takes three
arguments: a file descriptor, a buffer address, and a length. The
`exit` system call has number 60 and takes one argument: the exit code.
This complete program prints "Hello, world!" and then exits. It uses no
C library at all.

```asm
        .intel_syntax noprefix

        .section .rodata
msg:    .ascii  "Hello, world!\n"
msg_len = . - msg               # length of the text: 14 bytes

        .text
        .globl  _start
_start:
        mov     eax, 1          # rax = 1: system call number of write
        mov     edi, 1          # rdi = 1: file descriptor 1 is stdout
        lea     rsi, [rip + msg] # rsi = address of the buffer
        mov     edx, msg_len    # rdx = number of bytes to write
        syscall                 # enter the kernel

        mov     eax, 60         # rax = 60: system call number of exit
        xor     edi, edi        # rdi = 0: exit code 0
        syscall                 # enter the kernel; does not return
```

Note the register use: the system call number goes in `rax`, and the
arguments go in `rdi`, `rsi`, and `rdx`. After the first `syscall`,
`rax` holds the result of `write`, and `rcx` and `r11` are destroyed.

**Example:** Here is how the error rule works. If you call `write` with
the bad file descriptor 999, the kernel returns -9 in `rax`. The value
-9 is in the error range from -4095 to -1. It means `-EBADF` ("bad file
descriptor", error code 9). The C library sees this value, stores 9 in
`errno`, and returns -1 from the `write()` function.

### Stack Layout

The Linux kernel may align the end of the input argument area to an
8-byte boundary, instead of a 16-byte boundary. So inside the kernel,
you cannot count on the 16-byte stack alignment that user code has.

The kernel does not honor the *red zone*. The red zone is the 128-byte
area below the stack pointer that user code may use freely; see the
section "The Stack Frame". Kernel code is not allowed to use this area,
because an interrupt can overwrite it at any time. So you should compile
kernel code with the GCC option `-mno-red-zone`.

**Example:** In user space, a small leaf function can keep a temporary
value at `[rsp - 8]` without moving `rsp`, because the red zone protects
it. In the kernel, the same code is a bug. A hardware interrupt can
arrive between two instructions and write its own data below `rsp`. The
temporary value is then destroyed. With `-mno-red-zone`, GCC always
adjusts `rsp` first, for example with `sub rsp, 16`, so the data is safe.

### Miscellaneous Remarks

Linux kernel code is not allowed to change the x87 and SSE units. The
*x87 unit* is the old floating-point hardware, and the *SSE unit* holds
the `xmm` registers. If kernel code changes them anyway, it has to
restore them properly before sleeping or before leaving the kernel.

On preemptive kernels, even more care may be needed. A *preemptive*
kernel can interrupt its own code at almost any time to run another
task.

# Appendix B: Linker Optimization

This appendix describes optimizations which a linker may perform. The
*linker* is the tool that combines object files into a program or a
shared library. These optimizations are optional: a linker may do them,
but it does not have to.

## Combine GOTPLT and GOT Slots

First, some short definitions. The *GOT* (Global Offset Table) is a
table of addresses that the dynamic linker fills in. The *PLT*
(Procedure Linkage Table) is a table of small code stubs used to call
functions in other modules. Each regular PLT entry jumps through its own
address slot. That slot lives in the `.got.plt` section, so we call it
the *GOTPLT slot*.

In the small and medium code models, a program can reference the same
function symbol in two ways at the same time: through the PLT (a call)
and through the GOT (an address load). Normally, the linker then creates
two slots: a GOTPLT slot for the PLT entry and a GOT slot for the GOT
reference. It also creates two run-time relocations: a JUMP_SLOT
relocation to update the GOTPLT slot, and a GLOB_DAT relocation to
update the GOT slot. At run time, the JUMP_SLOT and GLOB_DAT relocations
apply the same symbol value to the GOTPLT slot and the GOT slot. So the
program stores the same address twice.

As an optimization, the linker may combine the GOTPLT slot and the GOT
slot into a single GOT slot. It then removes the run-time JUMP_SLOT
relocation. It replaces the regular PLT entry:

**Figure: Procedure Linkage Table entry via GOTPLT slot**

```asm
  .PLT: jmp     [GOTPLT slot]
        push    relocation index
        jmp     .PLT0
```

with a GOT PLT entry that makes an indirect jump through the GOT slot:

**Figure: Procedure Linkage Table entry via GOT slot**

```asm
  .PLT: jmp     [GOT slot]
        nop
```

The linker then resolves the PLT reference to this GOT PLT entry. The
indirect `jmp` is a 5-byte instruction. The `nop` can be encoded as a
3-byte instruction or as an 11-byte instruction. This fills an 8-byte or
a 16-byte PLT slot. A separate PLT with 8-byte slots may be used for
this optimization.

This optimization is not applicable to `STT_GNU_IFUNC` symbols. An
IFUNC symbol has a resolver function that picks the best implementation
at run time. For IFUNC symbols, the GOTPLT slots are resolved to the
selected implementation, but the GOT slots are resolved to their PLT
entries. So the two slots hold different values and cannot be combined.

The linker must also avoid this optimization if pointer equality is
needed. *Pointer equality* means: the address of a function must compare
equal everywhere in the process. In that case, the symbol value will not
be cleared, and the dynamic linker will not update the GOT slot. The
combined PLT entry would then jump to itself through the stale GOT slot.
As a result, the binary would run into an infinite loop at run time.

**Example:** Imagine a program that uses the function `qsort` from the
shared C library in two ways:

```asm
        call    qsort@PLT                    # a normal call
        mov     rax, [rip + qsort@GOTPCREL]  # take the address of qsort
```

Without the optimization, the linker creates a GOTPLT slot with a
JUMP_SLOT relocation and a GOT slot with a GLOB_DAT relocation. At run
time, the dynamic linker writes the address of `qsort` into both slots.
With the optimization, only the GOT slot and its GLOB_DAT relocation
remain. The PLT entry for `qsort` becomes a single indirect jump through
that GOT slot, followed by a `nop`. The call and the address load now
share one slot, and the dynamic linker has one relocation less to
process at startup.

## Optimize GOTPCRELX Relocations

Some instructions read a GOT slot with `rip`-relative addressing, for
example `mov rax, [rip + foo@GOTPCREL]`. Such instructions carry one of
these relocations: `R_X86_64_GOTPCRELX`, `R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX`, or
`R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTPCRELX`. The `R_X86_64_CODE_4_GOTPCRELX` relocation
applies if the first byte of the instruction, at the relocation offset
minus 4, is 0xd5. The byte 0xd5 is the REX2 prefix.

The AMD64 instruction encoding supports converting certain instructions
with these relocations against a symbol `foo`. The instruction changes
from a form with a *memory operand* (a read from the GOT slot) into a
different form with an *immediate operand* (a value written directly
inside the instruction). The linker may do this conversion if both
conditions hold:

- `foo` is defined locally (in the same module), and
- the relocation addend is -4. The *addend* is the constant that is part
  of the relocation.

In simple words: if the final address of `foo` is already known at link
time, the trip through the GOT is not needed. The linker rewrites the
instruction so it uses `foo` directly. The conversions are listed below.

**Convert call and jmp.** The linker converts the memory operand of
`call` and `jmp` into an immediate operand.

**Table: Call and jmp conversion**

| Memory operand | Immediate operand |
|---|---|
| `call [rip + foo@GOTPCREL]` | `nop` then `call foo` |
| `call [rip + foo@GOTPCREL]` | `call foo` then `nop` |
| `jmp [rip + foo@GOTPCREL]`  | `jmp foo` then `nop` |

> **Note:** Why the extra `nop`? The indirect `call` through the GOT is
> 6 bytes long. The direct `call foo` is only 5 bytes long. The 1-byte
> `nop` fills the missing byte, so the total size stays the same and no
> other code has to move. The `nop` can go before or after the `call`.
> For `jmp`, the `nop` goes after the jump.

**Convert mov.** The linker converts the memory operand of `mov` into an
immediate operand. There are two cases. When position-independent code
is disabled and `foo` is defined locally in the lower 32-bit address
space, the memory operand in `mov` can become an immediate operand: the
instruction loads the constant address of `foo`. Otherwise, the `mov`
must be changed to `lea`, which computes the address relative to `rip`.
*Position-independent code* (PIC) is code that works at any load
address.

**Table: Mov conversion**

| Memory operand | Immediate operand |
|---|---|
| `mov reg, [rip + foo@GOTPCREL]` | `mov reg, OFFSET foo` |
| `mov reg, [rip + foo@GOTPCREL]` | `lea reg, [rip + foo]` |

**Convert test and binop.** The linker converts the memory operand of
`test` and `binop` into an immediate operand, when position-independent
code is disabled. Here, `binop` is one of these instructions: `adc`,
`add`, `and`, `cmp`, `or`, `sbb`, `sub`, `xor`.

**Table: Test and binop conversion**

| Memory operand | Immediate operand |
|---|---|
| `test [rip + foo@GOTPCREL], reg` | `test reg, OFFSET foo` |
| `binop reg, [rip + foo@GOTPCREL]` | `binop reg, OFFSET foo` |

**Example:** Here is a before/after story of this optimization. A file
`main.s` loads the address of a local function `bar` from the GOT and
then calls `bar` through the GOT:

```asm
        mov     rax, [rip + bar@GOTPCREL]  # load the address of bar
        call    [rip + bar@GOTPCREL]       # call bar through the GOT
```

After assembling, `readelf -r main.o` shows the relaxable relocations.
The `mov` has a REX prefix, so it gets `R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX`. The
`call` has no REX prefix, so it gets `R_X86_64_GOTPCRELX`. Both have the
addend -4:

```
Relocation section '.rela.text':
  Offset  Type                     Sym. Name + Addend
  000003  R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX   bar - 4
  000009  R_X86_64_GOTPCRELX       bar - 4
```

The disassembly of the object file shows the memory-operand forms. The
displacement fields are still zero, because the linker has not filled
them yet:

```
0: 48 8b 05 00 00 00 00   mov  rax, [rip + 0x0]
7: ff 15 00 00 00 00      call [rip + 0x0]
```

Now we link the program without PIC, and `bar` ends up defined locally.
Both conditions for the conversion hold. The linker rewrites both
instructions. The disassembly of the linked program shows the
immediate-operand forms:

```
401000: 48 c7 c0 26 10 40 00   mov  rax, 0x401026   # mov rax, OFFSET bar
401007: 90                     nop
401008: e8 19 00 00 00         call 401026 <bar>
```

The `mov` from the GOT (7 bytes) became a `mov` with the immediate
address of `bar` (also 7 bytes). The indirect `call` (6 bytes) became a
`nop` plus a direct `call` (1 + 5 = 6 bytes). The sizes match, so no
other instruction moved. The program no longer needs GOT slots for
`bar`, and `readelf -r` on the output shows no relocations for `bar` at
all.
